### [An Ab initio Framework for Simulating Ultrafast Nonlinear Cavity Quantum Electrodynamics Spectra](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24180v1)

**Authors:** Luis Vasquez, Kewei Sun, Haibo Ma, Maxim F. Gelin  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24180v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `interference shaping light` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.24180_figures/2608.24180_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: cQED TA PP signals (GSB, SE, and ESA) of pyrazine at resonance i.e. ωc = 5.2 eV. DW input values: pulse duration τ = 5.0 fs, pump frequency ωpu = 5.2 eV, and probe carrier frequencies ωpr in the range from 3.0 eV to 7.0 eV in steps of 0.02 eV. cQDE input values: cavity loss rate κ = 0.018 fs−1 and the dipole coupling strength γ0 = 0.56 eV.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces cQUEDA, a novel, computationally efficient theoretical framework for simulating ultrafast nonlinear spectroscopy in cavity QED systems. By extending the quasi-classical Doorway-Window approximation, the authors calculate the full transient absorption pump-probe spectrum, demonstrating its power by modeling pyrazine polaritons. This advances the field's ability to predict complex quantum optical responses in molecular cavities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a computationally efficient, *ab initio* framework to model complex, nonlinear, and ultrafast dynamics in strongly coupled light-matter systems, which is a major frontier in quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating ultrafast transient absorption pump-probe spectra of molecular polaritons, specifically tackling the simulation of polaritons' ultrafast dynamics and nonlinear optical properties in cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (QED).

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed and demonstrated the cQUEDA framework, showing it can compute GSB, SE, and ESA contributions to the TA PP spectra of pyrazine strongly coupled to a cavity, confirming polariton formation.

**Method.** The method utilizes an *ab initio* framework based on the quasi-classical Doorway-Window (DW) approximation, which combines semiclassical trajectory simulations with cavity QED functions.

**Model / system.** The system involves molecular polaritons formed by coupling molecular electronic states (e.g., pyrazine) to a cavity photonic mode, described by a structured Hamiltonian. The simulation framework is termed cQUEDA/sikeda.

**Key observables.** Transient Absorption (TA) pump-probe (PP) spectra, specifically the contributions from Ground-State Bleach (GSB), Stimulated Emission (SE), and Excited-State Absorption (ESA).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity detuning ($\Delta\omega$), Rabi frequency ($\Omega_e$), cavity loss rate ($\kappa$), and the time delay ($T$) between pump and probe pulses.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is that the wavepacket dynamics during the probe time ($T$) occurs without the influence of the cavity, although future work aims to relax this assumption.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows computed TA PP signals (GSB, SE, and ESA) for pyrazine at resonance, confirming the existence of two polaritonic eigenstates and observing Rabi splitting in the SE signal.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for the simulation, derives the necessary Hamiltonian structure, develops the cQUEDA formalism using the DW approximation, and demonstrates its application to calculate the three spectral components (GSB, SE, ESA) for a specific molecule.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this letter we introduce a theoretical framework for the simulation of ultrafast transient absorption pump-probe spectra of molecular polaritons. We derive and implement the cavity quantum electrodynamics (QED) evolution equations of polaritonic states within the framework of the quasi-classical doorway-Window pproximation, hereto referred cQUEDA /sikeda/. This framework uses outputs from mixed quantum-classical dynamics simulations in the absence of the cavity. Then by including cavity parameters accounting for cavity rate loss, coupling strength, and frequency detuning, we simulate transient absorption pump-probe spectra. Consequently, we address one of the main standing problems of cavity QED, the simulation of polaritons ultrafast dynamics and nonlinear optical properties. We demonstrate the performance of our method by computing the ground-state bleach (GSB), stimulated emission (SE), and excited-state absorption (ESA) contributions of transient absorption pump-probe spectra of pyrazine strongly coupled to a cavity. The cQUEDA is an on-the-fly computationally efficient framework with low computer requirements: the pyrazine calculations, for example, took minutes on modern laptops. cQUEDA offers wide-ranging applicability and can be generalized to model diverse nonlinear spectroscopic signals and quantum optics responses.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper examines active matter confined by walls whose interaction strength varies continuously. It finds that the direction of global circulation is not solely determined by the wall's anchoring type, but rather by the collective internal order of the particles. This demonstrates a fundamental decoupling between local boundary selection and global transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of decoupling local boundary effects from global collective behavior is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, active matter, and open quantum systems where boundary conditions play a crucial role.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the boundary conditions (wall coupling) or the collective internal order of an active fluid dictates the direction of macroscopic circulation.

**Main result.** The local orientation at the wall and the direction of global circulation are selected by distinct physical processes, meaning the wall only dictates local possibilities, while collective order controls the global flow.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical derivations (e.g., single-particle fixed point conditions) with extensive numerical simulations across varying coupling parameters.

**Model / system.** The system models chiral self-propelled particles with nematic bulk alignment confined between parallel walls, whose wall coupling strength is continuously tuned between nematic and polar anchoring.

**Key observables.** Macroscopic chirality (sign of the mean current $\langle J angle$), nematic order parameter ($S$), and the local particle orientation distribution ($p(\psi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The wall coupling parameter $\lambda \in [0, 1]$, which interpolates between nematic ($\lambda=0$) and polar ($\lambda=1$) anchoring.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis separates the local single-particle dynamics from the many-body collective dynamics, and the comparison between the single-particle fixed point and the many-body sign change is key.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the local wall prediction (dashed curve) against the many-body current sign change (solid curve), showing the sign change is independent of $\lambda$ for small $\lambda$ and distinct from the isolated-particle prediction.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system's response across different wall coupling regimes ($\lambda$), comparing the local single-particle dynamics to the global many-body current behavior, culminating in the conclusion of decoupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can a boundary determine the direction of circulation in a collectively moving active fluid? We study chiral self-propelled particles with nematic bulk alignment, confined by walls whose orientational coupling varies continuously from nematic to polar. For an isolated particle at a wall, we derive the exact parameter condition under which a stable orientation is tangent to the wall. With predominantly nematic wall coupling, however, the many-body circulation changes sign at a chirality that is nearly independent of the wall symmetry. This sign change follows a pronounced decrease in nematic order and disappears when bulk alignment is removed. As the polar wall coupling becomes dominant, opposite-handed circulation is suppressed and same-handed polar locking emerges. Thus local orientation at a wall and the direction of macroscopic circulation are selected by distinct physical processes: the wall determines the possible local orientations, whereas collective order determines whether those orientations control the global flow.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that the critical dynamics of localization transitions in quasiperiodic systems can be tuned simply by varying the incommensurate frequency ($\Omega$) derived from arithmetic sequences. While the correlation-length exponent remains fixed, the dynamical critical exponent varies systematically, suggesting that the arithmetic properties of the modulation parameter control the non-equilibrium quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that the arithmetic structure of an irrational number can act as a control parameter for tuning dynamical critical behavior is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in engineered systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the dynamical critical exponents of localization transitions in quasiperiodic systems can be tuned without altering the underlying microscopic Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The correlation-length exponent remains constant ($
u \simeq 1$), while the dynamical critical exponent ($z$) varies systematically by changing the incommensurate frequency ($\Omega$) via the arithmetic structure of generalized Fibonacci sequences.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced diagnostics including generalized fidelity susceptibility, localization-length scaling, and finite-size gap analysis applied to the single-particle Schrödinger equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional tight-binding chain of spinless fermions subjected to a quasiperiodic onsite potential, defined by $V_j = V \cos(2\pi\Omega j + \phi)$. The quasiperiodicity is controlled by $\Omega$, which is derived from generalized Fibonacci sequences parameterized by $(m, n)$.

**Key observables.** Correlation-length exponent ($
u$), Dynamical critical exponent ($z$), Generalized fidelity susceptibility ($\chi^{2r+2}$), and the low-energy energy gap ($\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping amplitude ($J$), Modulation amplitude ($V$), and the incommensurate frequency ($\Omega$) controlled by the arithmetic parameters $(m, n)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is quasiperiodic (ruling out rational $\Omega$) and uses finite-size scaling theory to extract critical exponents.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate data collapse for $
u=1$ using $\ln \chi^2$ and $\xi$ vs $V$ for different modulation parameters, confirming the robustness of $
u$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and the control mechanism using arithmetic sequences. It then applies scaling analyses using fidelity susceptibility and gap measurements to extract $
u$ and $z$, demonstrating the differential dependence of these exponents on the arithmetic parameter $\Omega$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The critical exponents and universality classes of localization transitions in quasiperiodic systems are of fundamental importance for understanding critical phenomena in aperiodic systems. Here we show that the dynamical critical behavior can be tuned without adding new terms or changing the form of the Hamiltonian, but solely by varying the incommensurate frequency of the quasiperiodic onsite potential. We construct a family of incommensurate frequencies from the limiting ratios of generalized Fibonacci sequences controlled by the parameters $(m,n)$, and use them to define the quasiperiodic onsite potential. By combining generalized fidelity susceptibility, localization-length scaling, and finite-size gap analysis, we find that the correlation-length exponent is insensitive to the choice of the incommensurate frequency and remains consistent with the correlation-length critical exponent, $ν\simeq 1$, in the localization transition of the standard Aubry--Andr'e--Harper model. In contrast, the dynamical exponent extracted from the low-energy gap scaling varies systematically with the incommensurate frequency. Our results show that changing the incommensurate frequency provides a simple way to tune dynamical critical scaling in deterministic aperiodic systems. Our results suggest instead that the arithmetic structure of an irrational number can serve as a control parameter for nonequilibrium quantum dynamics, enabling the tuning of dynamical critical behavior without changing the microscopic Hamiltonian or the physical spatial dimension.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a minimal, thermodynamically consistent chemical oscillator model to study autonomous oscillations driven by non-equilibrium processes. Using advanced mathematical techniques like normal-form reduction, the authors analytically determine the Hopf bifurcation point. Crucially, they show that this dynamical transition is accompanied by sharp, quantifiable discontinuities in key thermodynamic observables, bridging non-equilibrium dynamics and thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection drawn between a non-equilibrium dynamical transition (Hopf bifurcation) and measurable thermodynamic quantities (discontinuities in free energy/work rate) provides a powerful theoretical tool for characterizing open, non-equilibrium systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To introduce and analyze a minimal, thermodynamically consistent chemical oscillator that exhibits autonomous oscillations under non-equilibrium driving.

**Main result.** The system undergoes a supercritical Hopf bifurcation at a critical parameter value, which is analytically linked to kink-like discontinuities in time-averaged thermodynamic observables like the semi-grand Gibbs free energy and non-conservative work rate.

**Method.** The analysis employs normal-form reduction near the Hopf bifurcation point, utilizing a controlled irreversible approximation to analytically determine the bifurcation structure and thermodynamic response.

**Model / system.** The model is a Chemical Reaction Network (CRN) involving five species, featuring reversible, uni- and bi-molecular reactions, and open to an environment via chemostatting. The dynamics are governed by the Reaction Rate Equation.

**Key observables.** Semi-grand Gibbs free energy, non-conservative work rate, Hopf bifurcation point, limit cycle radius, and angular frequency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemostat concentration (control parameter), reaction rate constants, and the bifurcation parameter $[S]$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reactions are assumed to be elementary, allowing the use of Local Detailed Balance (LDB), and the analysis relies on normal-form reduction near the bifurcation point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic reaction network, the time evolution of thermodynamic quantities ($\langle G angle$ and $\langle \dot{W} angle$) across the Hopf bifurcation, and the convergence of analytical predictions for the bifurcation threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the minimal CRN, derives the necessary thermodynamic balance equations, analyzes the dynamics using normal-form reduction near the Hopf bifurcation, and finally calculates the discontinuity of thermodynamic observables at the onset of oscillations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inspired by the chemical reaction network considered as the smallest system featuring a Hopf bifurcation and its reversible extension, we introduce an even more minimal reaction network that is thermodynamically consistent and exhibits autonomous oscillations under nonequilibrium driving. The model combines three features that are rarely realized simultaneously in compact oscillator networks: a chemically plausible structure restricted to uni- and bimolecular reactions, reversibility of such reactions, and analytical tractability. The system consists of three internal species coupled to two chemostatted species and still undergoes a supercritical Hopf bifurcation when a chemostat concentration is varied. To analyze the dynamics and thermodynamics near the onset of oscillations, we employ the mathematical technique of normal-form reduction, which allows obtaining a controlled irreversible approximation that preserves the leading phase-space structure of the full reversible network while enabling explicit calculations. This framework provides analytical access to the bifurcation structure and the leading contributions governing thermodynamic observables. Within this setting, we use the onset of oscillations to characterize the thermodynamic response of the system. In particular, we offer an analytical description of key thermodynamic quantities such as the semi-grand Gibbs free energy and the non-conservative work rate, which exhibit a kink-like discontinuity at the Hopf bifurcation. We thus provide an analytical description of a phenomenon previously characterized only through numerical simulations of more complex networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a geometric framework to explain how micro-swimmers can generate directed motion by exploiting thermal fluctuations when driven by time-varying external parameters. It mathematically links the resulting propulsion to the non-zero curvature of the parameter space and quantifies the associated energy dissipation using a Riemannian metric. This unifies concepts from stochastic mechanics, thermodynamics, and geometry in the context of active matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful geometric framework linking non-equilibrium thermodynamics (entropy production) directly to the topology of the control parameter space (Berry curvature), which is a concept highly relevant in quantum pumping and quantum control.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To show how thermal fluctuations allow a micro-swimmer (scallop) to achieve directed propulsion, bypassing the constraints of Purcell's scallop theorem.

**Main result.** Directed geometric propulsion is achieved when the control parameters are cyclically modulated, leading to a non-zero Berry-Sinitsyn-Nemenman curvature and excess entropy production quantified by a Riemannian metric.

**Method.** The analysis uses stochastic thermodynamics, solving the Smoluchowski equation via perturbation theory and generalized inverse operators to calculate non-equilibrium work and entropy production.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-hinge scallop swimmer whose stochastic shape dynamics are governed by a Smoluchowski equation dependent on two time-varying control parameters ($	heta_1, 	heta_2$).

**Key observables.** Non-zero Berry-Sinitsyn-Nemenman curvature ($F_{12}$), Riemannian thermodynamic metric ($g_{ij}$), and excess entropy production ($\Sigma_{ex}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The slow-driving parameter $\epsilon$, and the control parameters $	heta(t)$ which are modulated along a closed loop in the parameter space $T^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Stokes flow ($	ext{Re} \ll 1$), utilizes perturbation theory for slow driving, and relies on the generalized inverse operator for the Smoluchowski generator.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the scallop model and shows that cycling the control parameters along a closed loop in the parameter space induces the necessary geometric curvature and non-zero net displacement.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the framework by first establishing the stochastic dynamics via the Smoluchowski equation, then applying perturbation theory to calculate non-equilibrium corrections, and finally deriving the geometric connection between excess entropy production and the Riemannian metric tensor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

According to Purcell's scallop theorem, reciprocal single-degree-of-freedom shape deformations cannot achieve net propulsion in a viscous fluid. We show that this limitation is bypassed by thermal fluctuations in a two-parameter driven potential landscape. Formulating the stochastic shape dynamics via a Smoluchowski equation with position-dependent mobility $M_\mathrm{eff}(x)$, we utilize a generalized inverse operator to evaluate the slow-driving response. Cyclic modulation of the control parameters induces a non-zero Berry-Sinitsyn-Nemenman curvature $F_{12}(\bmθ)$, resulting in directed geometric propulsion. Simultaneously, the non-adiabatic excess dissipation is dictated by a Riemannian thermodynamic metric $g_{ij}(\bmθ)$. Our results provide a unified geometric foundation that bridges hydrodynamic friction, stochastic mechanics, and thermodynamic trade-offs in micro-swimmers.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper determines how the physical boundaries of an ion trap control the correlated electric-field noise acting on trapped ions. By employing techniques from spectral theory and billiards, the authors show that the enclosure geometry selectively enhances normal-field noise while suppressing tangential-field noise. This provides a fundamental understanding of how passive electrode design can be used to engineer the quantum noise environment for quantum computing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, quantitative link between macroscopic electrostatics (geometry) and microscopic quantum noise processes (heating rates, Lindblad operators) in trapped ions, offering a new diagnostic tool for trap quality.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the passive conducting geometry of an enclosed ion trap dictates the spatial structure and magnitude of correlated electric-field noise experienced by trapped ions.

**Main result.** The enclosure geometry imposes a mode-selective filtering effect, increasing normal-field noise while decreasing tangential-field noise, and provides an exact return-pair functional describing this noise.

**Method.** The analysis uses boundary-potential covariance, the Dirichlet Green function, and billiard unfolding techniques to construct the electric-field cross-spectral matrix and its associated Kossakowski generator.

**Model / system.** The system is trapped ions confined within an enclosed geometry, such as a parallel slab or a general curved cover. The noise source is modeled as a stochastic electric field fluctuating due to the surrounding passive conductors.

**Key observables.** Electric-field cross-spectral matrix, collective environmental noise eigenchannels, noise rank, and the ratio of normal-to-tangential heating rates ($\zeta(3)$ and $\eta(3)$ at specific limits).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of cover height to ion spacing ($h/d$), the frequency ($\omega$), and the ion number ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Born–Markov secular approximation and assumes fixed stochastic source statistics, focusing on the geometric reshaping of the noise operator.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mode-selective filtering effect (normal vs. tangential heating ratios) and show the correlation between the electrostatic decay exponent and the shortest specular excess length across curved covers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general framework using boundary-potential covariance to deriving the exact return-pair functional for the parallel slab. It then applies this framework to show the collective ordering theorem for normal/tangential fields and extends the analysis to general curved covers, concluding with diagnostics on noise rank and gate exposure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We ask how passive conducting geometry determines the spatial structure of electric-field noise seen by trapped ions. From a boundary-potential covariance and the Dirichlet Green function we construct the $N$-ion electric-field cross-spectral matrix and its blockwise motional Kossakowski generator. In a parallel slab, billiard unfolding turns the electrostatic response into a return-depth measure and yields an exact return-pair functional for arbitrary stationary surface spectra. For every finite equal-height ion array, the passive cover increases the normal-field covariance matrix in the positive-semidefinite ordering and decreases the tangential-field covariance matrix in the same ordering: all collective normal-field coordinates acquire more absolute noise, while all collective tangential coordinates acquire less. At $h=2d$ the local-noise single-ion ratios are exactly $ζ(3)$ and $η(3)$.   Diagonalizing the equal-frequency covariance identifies collective environmental noise eigenchannels and a geometry-dependent noise rank; within a degenerate frequency block these become the Lindblad jump channels. In a ten-ion example, closing the cover to $h=2d$ lowers the participation rank from 5.61 to 5.11 while increasing the leading channel's share from 23.7% to 28.6%; a primitive Mølmer-Sørensen calculation shows how the projected covariance sets the weak-heating gate exposure. Beyond parallel walls, specular paths emerge as large-$q_z$ saddles of the screened boundary operator. Across 16 curved covers, the fitted electrostatic decay exponent correlates at 0.9991 with the independently computed shortest specular excess length, while separate tests resolve focusing and competing saddles.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates jerky motion in active granular particles caused by the combination of self-propulsion and dry friction. The authors demonstrate that this interaction forces the dynamics into a jerk-dominated regime, predicting a quadratic increase in speed over time. The findings are supported by theoretical modeling, numerical analysis, and physical experiments using active vibrobots.

**Why it may be interesting.** The emergence of higher-order, non-Markovian dynamics from simple frictional contacts is a general mechanism in non-equilibrium statistical physics, relevant for understanding energy dissipation and transport in complex active matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The standard Newtonian description is insufficient for systems exhibiting abrupt transitions between rest and motion, necessitating the inclusion of higher-order derivatives like jerk.

**Main result.** The interplay between activity and dry friction robustly generates jerk-dominated dynamics, leading to quadratic speed increase ($v \sim t^2$) rather than the linear growth expected classically.

**Method.** The study combines analytical derivations (using path integrals and Onsager-Machlup functionals) with numerical simulations and physical experiments using active vibrobots.

**Model / system.** The system involves self-propelled particles (active granular particles) subjected to an active force and dry friction, which introduces a threshold for motion.

**Key observables.** Particle speed scaling exponent ($\alpha$), displacement scaling ($\delta r \sim t^3$), and the probability of activation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dry friction threshold force ($\Delta F$), activity strength, vibration amplitude (low vs. high), and persistence time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations such as neglecting white noise or assuming the velocity after activation is independent of the activation point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mechanism of jerky motion due to dry friction, compare speed evolution in jerky vs. Newtonian regimes, and show data collapse confirming cubic scaling of displacement.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical need for higher-order dynamics, models the system using active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes coupled with dry friction, derives scaling laws, and validates these predictions through both numerical simulations and physical experiments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Abrupt transitions between rest and motion can render the standard Newtonian description -- based on position, velocity, and acceleration -- incomplete, requiring higher-order derivatives such as the jerk, the third time derivative of position. Here, we show that the interplay between activity and dry friction gives rise to robust jerk-dominated dynamics in self-propelled particles: the particle speed increases quadratically with time under an active force, in contrast to the linear growth expected for conventional Newtonian dynamics. We demonstrate this behavior analytically, numerically, and experimentally using active vibrobots self-propelling on a vertically vibrating plate at low vibration amplitudes, where surface asperities generate dry friction and, thus, give rise to jerky motion when combined with activity. Our results establish dry friction as a simple mechanism for realizing higher-order dynamics in active matter and suggest that jerky dynamics may arise broadly in nonequilibrium systems with frictional contacts.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an experimental Quantum-Secure Time Transfer (QSTT) system designed for satellite communication. By leveraging entanglement-based photon sources and integrating Quantum Key Distribution (QKD), the system achieves unprecedented key rates and high clock synchronization precision. This advancement establishes a new benchmark for ultra-secure, space-based timing services.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates advanced quantum networking protocols applied to time synchronization, which is a critical application area for quantum information science, particularly concerning secure communication over long-haul links.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a highly secure and precise time synchronization method over satellite-to-ground channels, overcoming vulnerabilities and precision limits of classical timing protocols.

**Main result.** The developed Quantum-Secure Time Transfer (QSTT) system achieved a QKD key rate of approximately 3 bits s-1, representing a 30-fold increase over state-of-the-art Micius satellite demonstrations.

**Method.** The system combines entanglement-based quantum signaling for time transfer with Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) to encrypt time-tag data, incorporating post-quantum security and obfuscation techniques.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses an entanglement-based QSTT system emulated in a low Earth orbit channel, utilizing a type-0 Sagnac-based entangled-photon source (EPS) built with a PPKTP crystal.

**Key observables.** QKD Key Rate (3 bits s-1), Clock Offset (sub-nanosecond precision), and Timing Jitter ($\sigma_{ex} = 473$ ps).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 30-fold increase in QKD key rate vs. Micius; $10^{-10}$ security parameter; $\sim 2$ orders of magnitude increase in EPS rate using type-0 crystal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes the use of QKD keys to seed symmetric encryption (like AES) rather than encrypting all data with a One-Time Pad (OTP) due to rate mismatch.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall QSTT system schematic, while Figure 2 details the experimental setups for both the EPS and the full QSTT system.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup (EPS using type-0 PPKTP), the QSTT protocol which involves data alignment and fine synchronization, and the security enhancements using QKD and post-quantum cryptography.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally demonstrate an entanglement-based Quantum-Secure Time Transfer (QSTT) system in an emulated low Earth orbit satellite-to-ground channel using a type-0 Sagnac-based entangled-photon source. Our new QSTT system delivers a finite Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) key rate of approximately $3~$bits$~\text{s}^{-1}$ with a $10^{-10}$ security parameter, providing a $30$-fold increase in the QKD key rate compared to the state-of-the-art entanglement-based QKD system delivered by the Micius satellite. Beyond this high key rate outcome, novel to our QSTT system is a GPS-free clock synchronization, optimized use of QKD, embedded post-quantum security, and obfuscation of the system configuration via use of a pre-shared key. Collectively, these enhancements deliver the most efficient and secure deployment of QSTT to date, and point the way forward to high-accuracy ultra-secure time transfer in space.

</details>

### [Certified Fidelity Susceptibility from Classical Shadows](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24732v1)

**Authors:** Kiran Adhikari  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24732v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **3/5**

The errors remain below the geometric bound of Theorem 3 and vanish at n = N/2 = 8, where the Krylov hierarchy terminates.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper presents a rigorous, resource-efficient method to estimate the fidelity susceptibility ($\chi_F$), a key indicator of quantum phase transitions. It achieves this by reformulating $\chi_F$ as a moment reconstruction problem solvable via Krylov subspace techniques. By coupling this theory with randomized single-copy measurements (classical shadows), the authors provide a statistically certified lower bound on $\chi_F$ suitable for near-term quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial bridge between theoretical quantum diagnostics ($\chi_F$) and practical, resource-constrained quantum measurements (classical shadows), making it highly relevant for experimental quantum simulation and metrology.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of directly measuring the fidelity susceptibility ($\chi_F$), a key diagnostic for quantum phase transitions and metrology, on quantum hardware.

**Main result.** It develops a method to certify a lower bound for $\chi_F$ by approximating it using randomized single-copy measurements and Krylov subspace methods.

**Method.** The core method involves reformulating $\chi_F$ as a moment problem, approximating it via the Krylov subspace, and then estimating the required moments using classical shadow techniques.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to general quantum systems described by a Hamiltonian $H(\lambda)$ with a non-degenerate ground state, often exemplified by models like the 1D Transverse-Field Ising Model.

**Key observables.** Fidelity susceptibility ($\chi_F$), linear static susceptibilities ($\chi_O$), and response moments ($M_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The convergence rate depends on the response condition number ($\kappa_\phi$), and the required measurement resources scale with the desired accuracy ($\epsilon$) and failure probability ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, a non-degenerate ground state, and a spectral gap ($\Delta > 0$). The final bound is an additive, rather than relative, guarantee.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the calculated $\chi^{(n)}_F$ approximations against the exact $\chi_F$ for the 1D TFIM, demonstrating geometric convergence as the Krylov order $n$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the definition of $\chi_F$ via resolvent methods, reduces it to a moment reconstruction problem, applies Krylov subspace theory to establish a monotone lower bound hierarchy, and finally uses randomized Pauli measurements (classical shadows) to estimate these moments and certify the bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fidelity susceptibility is a useful probe of quantum phase transitions and quantum metrology, but its direct evaluation on a quantum device is challenging. By integrating Krylov-subspace methods with a resolvent-based reformulation, we present a method for certifying fidelity susceptibility using randomized single-copy measurements across repeated ground-state preparations. The resulting approximations form monotone lower bounds to the exact fidelity susceptibility and converge geometrically. We also present applications to metrology and linear static susceptibilities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates Dynamic Quantum Phase Transitions in the two-leg Creutz ladder with long-range hopping. By analyzing the dynamical free energy derived from an exact two-band model solution, the authors determine that the critical times are highly sensitive to the hopping decay exponent ($
u$) and the finite range ($D$). The findings suggest that for certain parameter regimes, the critical dynamics become extremely dense, indicating complex non-equilibrium behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between long-range interactions, quasi-periodicity, and the emergence of dense critical dynamics is a key topic in non-equilibrium quantum many-body physics, relevant to understanding thermalization and quantum chaos.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Dynamic Quantum Phase Transitions (DQPTs) that occur during quantum quenches in a specific lattice model.

**Main result.** The critical times where non-analyticities in the dynamical free energy appear depend on both the long-range hopping exponent (ν) and the finite range (D).

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the Loschmidt amplitude and the dynamical free energy from an exact solution of a generic two-band model in momentum space.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-leg Creutz ladder incorporating long-range hopping, modeled as a spinless two-band free-fermion system. The Hamiltonian is analyzed under quantum quenches.

**Key observables.** Dynamical free energy f(t), Loschmidt amplitude Z(t), and the location of Yang-Lee-Fisher (YLF) zeros.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Long-range hopping exponent (ν), finite range (D), and quench parameters ($	heta, 	ilde{	heta}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on obtaining an exact solution for the generic two-band model in momentum space and identifying non-analyticities where YLF zeros hit the real-time axis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the parameter regions in which DQPTs occur for various values of D and show plots of the dynamical free energy f(t) near critical times.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general theoretical framework by solving the two-band model, deriving expressions for $f(t)$ and YLF zeros. It then applies this framework to the Creutz ladder, analyzing how the critical times and the existence of DQPTs depend on the long-range hopping parameters $
u$ and $D$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we investigate quantum quenches in the two-leg Creutz model with long-range hopping, where the hopping amplitudes decay with distance as a power law characterized by an exponent $ν$ and have a finite range $D$. We first obtain the exact solution of a generic two-band model in momentum space. This allows us to compute the Loschmidt amplitude and, consequently, the dynamical free energy $f(\texttt{t})$ of the two-band model. We also show how to determine the Yang-Lee Fisher (YLF) zeros by solving a nonlinear equation. We demonstrate that the two-leg Creutz model in momentum space is a special case of the generic two-band model. Using these results, we identify the non-analyticities in the dynamical free energy $f(\texttt{t})$ at critical times $\texttt{t}_{c}$. We find that the number of nontrivial critical times $N_{s}$ depends on both $ν$ and $D$. In particular, we show that for small $ν$ and large $D$ the critical times become increasingly dense, leading, in the appropriate regime, to non-analyticities at an increasingly dense set of times---similar to what was observed by Xavier and Hoyos [Phys. Rev. B, $\textbf{108}$, 214303 (2023)] in the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model with long-range hopping terms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel hybrid quantum-classical algorithm to overcome the major hurdle of nonlinearity in quantum CFD simulations. By using tensor-based block encodings, the method avoids the exponential resource scaling associated with previous state-based approaches. This breakthrough demonstrates a scalable pathway for achieving quantum advantage in simulating complex, high-Reynolds number fluid dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on fluid dynamics, the core methodology—using tensor networks and quantum programming to manage complex, non-linear operators—is highly relevant to simulating open quantum systems or many-body dynamics where nonlinear couplings are present.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the efficient and scalable implementation of nonlinear terms within Quantum Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) simulations.

**Main result.** The proposed hybrid quantum-classical tensor network algorithm significantly reduces the computational cost and memory footprint compared to fully classical tensor network solvers, establishing a scalable path toward quantum advantage in CFD.

**Method.** The authors propose a hybrid approach combining variational time-stepping with quantum tensor programming. Nonlinearity is handled by replacing state-based encodings with tensor-based block encodings, which are compiled into unitary gates.

**Model / system.** The study models Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD), specifically simulating turbulent flow fields governed by nonlinear Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Examples include the Temporally Developing Jet (TDJ) and Taylor Green Vortex (TGV).

**Key observables.** Success probability of the nonlinear operation ($\alpha_{succ}$), computational cost, memory footprint, and the field representation error ($\epsilon_{\ell_2}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reynolds numbers ($Re \in \{10, 20, ..., 1000\}$), bond dimension ($\chi$), and time step ($\Delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the nonlinear terms can be effectively encoded into diagonal block encodings, which stabilizes the success probability against exponential decay with system size.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hybrid algorithm workflow, the quantum circuit decomposition for a single time step (distinguishing linear and nonlinear operators), and comparisons of error scaling and computational costs across various Reynolds numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework for handling nonlinear PDEs using a hybrid quantum-classical loop. It then applies this framework to simulate specific turbulent flow examples (TDJ, TGV), providing rigorous error and cost analyses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear terms present a fundamental challenge for quantum computational fluid dynamics, as their implementation on inherently linear quantum hardware typically requires resource-intensive workarounds that limit scalability to large-scale simulations. We present a hybrid quantum-classical tensor network algorithm that addresses this bottleneck by combining variational time-stepping with quantum tensor programming to efficiently compile operators and time-dependent fields into quantum circuits. Within a probabilistic framework, we replace prior state-based nonlinear implementations with tensor-based block encodings, stabilizing success probabilities that otherwise decay exponentially with system size. Benchmarking on turbulent flow fields demonstrates that the algorithm maintains high success probabilities and moderate measurement overhead across increasing Reynolds numbers and grid resolutions. Compared to fully classical tensor network solvers, our hybrid approach yields substantial reductions in both memory footprint and computational cost, establishing a scalable pathway toward practical quantum advantage in scale-resolving CFD simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a multi-shell model to study the kinetics of early-stage nucleation in condensed clusters, explicitly coupling structural reordering dynamics to the growth process. Using stochastic simulations, the authors find that the interplay between these two mechanisms leads to a quantifiable kinetic turnover effect. This provides a predictive framework for understanding multi-step phase transitions beyond classical theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on non-equilibrium kinetics, path-dependence of the free energy barrier, and the explicit coupling of structural dynamics (reordering) to phase separation kinetics is highly relevant to understanding complex many-body transitions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the discrepancy between theoretical and experimental nucleation rates, particularly when dynamic structural reordering occurs during multi-step phase transitions.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates a kinetic turnover effect where the relationship between mean first-passage time and reordering susceptibility becomes non-monotonic as coupling strength increases.

**Method.** The authors use stochastic simulations and path analysis on a multi-shell model to track the time-resolved evolution of structural order during early-stage nucleation.

**Model / system.** The system is a multi-shell condensed cluster modeled with a space-time-dependent order-parameter field $\phi(\mathbf{x}, t)$. The energy incorporates interfacial and bulk potentials, explicitly coupling structural reordering with cluster growth.

**Key observables.** Nucleation rate, mean first-passage time, reordering susceptibility, and the effective nucleation barrier.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($k_H$), reordering rate, and the structural order parameter $\phi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model neglects cluster-cluster coalescence and treats the process in an over-damped limit, focusing only on early-stage nucleation dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the reordering-nucleation process pathways, the energy landscape in $(r, \phi)$ space, and show the path-averaged order parameter, cluster radius, and transition time across varying coupling strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a multi-shell model, details the coupled reordering and adsorption mechanisms, analyzes the first-passage problem using effective barriers, and presents simulations demonstrating the turnover effect.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nucleation is a key rate-limiting process in phase transition and phase separation. Recent studies highlight a significant discrepancy between experimentally measured nucleation rates and theoretical predictions, particularly when dynamic structural reordering occurs along multi-step pathways. To bridge this gap, we develop a multi-shell model with a space-time-dependent order-parameter field to describe the reordering-nucleation process, where structural reorganization couples with the early growth of condensed clusters. Through stochastic simulations, we track the time-resolved evolution of heterogeneous structural order inside growing clusters. Path analysis of the first-passage problem in early-stage nucleation demonstrates that shifting the reordering rate alters the nucleation rate by several orders of magnitude. Furthermore, as the coupling strength increases, the relationship between the mean first-passage time and reordering susceptibility shifts from monotonic to non-monotonic, exhibiting a turnover effect. We quantitatively rationalize these behaviors with an effective nucleation barrier that accounts for non-equilibrium properties. Our findings elucidate the mechanisms behind multi-step nucleation and offer a predictive framework for future studies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper predicts the existence of stable, rotating three-dimensional vortex quantum droplets within binary Bose-Einstein condensates. These clusters are stabilized by a combination of mean-field interactions, quantum fluctuations, and the Coriolis force due to rotation. The findings open theoretical avenues for observing stable, collective motion of multiple vortex lines in ultracold atomic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the complex dynamics of interacting quantum fluids, specifically exploring how external forces (rotation) and quantum corrections stabilize exotic topological structures (vortex clusters) in BECs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper predicts and characterizes stable three-dimensional (3D) vortex quantum droplets formed in binary Bose-Einstein condensates (BEC) that exhibit persistent rotation.

**Main result.** Stable vortex clusters, distinct from localized droplets, are found to exist in a wide parameter range, suggesting a route to observing regular collective motion of vortex lines in condensates.

**Method.** The study involves solving coupled dimensionless Gross-Pitaevskii equations incorporating Lee-Huang-Yang (LHY) quantum corrections and the Coriolis force arising from rotation.

**Model / system.** The system is a binary Bose-Einstein condensate modeled in 3D geometry, analyzed under the influence of mean-field nonlinearity, quantum fluctuations (LHY), rotation, and an external potential.

**Key observables.** The norm ($N$) of the cluster, the cluster radius, the distance between adjacent vortices, and the critical rotation frequency ($\omega_{crit}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemical potential ($\mu$), rotation frequency ($\omega$), and the number of vortex lines ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes equal masses and intra-species interactions for simplicity, and the stability is critically dependent on the repulsive LHY quantum corrections balancing the attractive residual mean-field nonlinearity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the dependence of the cluster norm ($N$) on chemical potential ($\mu$) for 2, 3, and 4 vortex lines, illustrating bifurcation from single-vortex states. Other figures show 3D isosurfaces and stability evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model and governing equations, predicting the existence of the new cluster type, analyzing the dependence of cluster properties (size, norm) on rotation frequency and chemical potential, and finally demonstrating stability through numerical simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We predict a new type of stable three-dimensional (3D) vortex quantum droplets in binary Bose-Einstein condensate arranged into ring clusters that per-sistently rotate in the external potential. In contrast to clusters composed from localized quantum droplets, the states introduced here represent interacting vortex lines with identical topological charges nested in common 3D envelope and existing in much broader parameter range in comparison with local-ized quantum droplets. The intricate interplay between mean-field nonlinearity of Bose-Einstein condensate, quantum fluctuations described by Lee-Huang-Yang correction, Coriolis force arising due to rotation, and external potential leads to substantial variations of cluster shape upon increase of its rotation frequency. Rotating vortex clusters bifurcate from single-vortex quantum droplets and exist only above critical value of the rotation frequency that decreases with increase of chemical potential and depends on the number of vortex lines in the cluster. The radius of the cluster decreases with increase of rotation frequency and weakly varies with chemical potential, which determines mostly localization of the individual vortices in cluster and overall width of its envelope. Vortex droplet clusters are very robust objects existing in stable form in wide intervals of the number of particles for any number (odd or even) of vortex lines forming them. Our results may open the route to observation of stable arrays of vortex lines of different configurations per-forming regular collective motion in condensate.

</details>

**Summary.** This work connects the complex dynamics of entanglement in integrable quantum systems to fundamental concepts of transport theory. By showing that entanglement Hamiltonian cumulants are determined by conserved current cumulants, it offers a unified, transport-based description applicable across free, conformal, and interacting integrable models. This provides a deeper physical understanding of how entanglement evolves out of equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, transport-based understanding of entanglement dynamics that complements the standard quasiparticle picture, which is particularly useful for understanding non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in 1D systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a direct connection between entanglement dynamics and transport properties in integrable quantum systems following global quantum quenches.

**Main result.** The cumulants of the entanglement Hamiltonian are exactly given by the cumulants of currents of suitable conserved charges evaluated in the macrostate selected by the initial state.

**Method.** The analysis uses the framework of integrable models, employing techniques like the Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA) and space-time duality to relate entanglement measures to conserved charges.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on interacting integrable lattice Hamiltonians in (1+1) dimensions, such as the XXZ chain, undergoing global quantum quenches.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Hamiltonian cumulants ($\langle K_n^A angle_c$), Rényi entropies ($S_\alpha^A$), and conserved current cumulants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis covers the ballistic regime ($t \ll \ell_A$) and the long-time limit ($t \gg \ell_A$), comparing free, conformal, and interacting integrable limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the applicability of the TBA framework to describe macrostates via Generalized Gibbs Ensembles (GGE).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first analyzing the entanglement measures in the context of quenches, showing how the cumulants relate to conserved charges, and then demonstrating the consistency of this picture across different integrable limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the dynamics of the cumulants of the entanglement Hamiltonian in interacting integrable models following global quantum quenches. Building on recent results based on space-time duality, we show that these cumulants are exactly given by the cumulants of currents of suitable conserved charges evaluated in the macrostate selected by the initial state. This establishes a direct connection between entanglement dynamics and transport, providing a transport counterpart to the quasiparticle picture that successfully describes the evolution of the von Neumann entropy. In the free-fermion and conformal limits, our results reduce to the difference between the current cumulants carried by right- and left-moving excitations, recovering previously known expressions. In interacting integrable models, where a decomposition into independent right and left movers is no longer meaningful at the operator level, a similar structure survives at the level of the entanglement spectrum, yielding a unified description of entanglement Hamiltonian fluctuations across free, conformal, and interacting integrable systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses DMFT on the Kane-Mele-Hubbard model to predict the altermagnetic Anomalous Hall Effect (AHE) in correlated TMD monolayers. The key finding is that doping drives the system into a specific antiferromagnetic phase that breaks necessary symmetries, thereby generating a measurable AHE. This work provides a realistic, correlation-based mechanism for realizing such topological transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on correlation-driven topological phenomena (AHE) in strongly correlated materials like TMDs provides a rich theoretical playground for understanding emergent quantum states beyond simple band theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a correlation-driven route to the altermagnetic anomalous Hall effect (AHE) and its associated spin--edge-locked edge states.

**Main result.** Hole doping induces a spin-flop transition to an out-of-plane Néel-type antiferromagnetic phase, which breaks symmetry and generates a finite AHE, distinct from conventional ferromagnetism.

**Method.** The study employs Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) to incorporate dynamical electronic correlations into the analysis of the Kane-Mele-Hubbard model.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a modified Kane-Mele-Hubbard (AKMH) model, applied to transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) monolayers, incorporating hopping, spin-orbit coupling, and an onsite Hubbard interaction.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity (sigma_xy), magnetic phases (paramagnetic, xy-AF, z-AF), and spin--edge-locked chiral edge states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hubbard interaction strength (U), electron doping level (n), and spin-orbit coupling (lambda).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DMFT approximation is used, which neglects nonlocal quantum fluctuations; the analysis contrasts results derived from spin-rotationally invariant interactions versus Ising-like ones.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the honeycomb structure, the evolution of local magnetization and energy differences across magnetic transitions, and momentum-resolved spectral functions showing edge-localized modes.

**Paper structure.** The work systematically analyzes the system's behavior by varying the Hubbard interaction (U) and doping level (n). It first establishes the transition from a paramagnetic to an antiferromagnetic phase, then details how doping drives the system into the symmetry-breaking z-AF phase, which is responsible for the measurable AHE.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a correlation-driven route to the altermagnetic anomalous Hall effect (AHE) and its associated \emph{spin--edge-locked} edge states in a modified Kane--Mele--Hubbard model. Using dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT), we show that, at half-filling, increasing the Hubbard interaction drives the system from a metallic paramagnetic phase hosting antichiral edge states into an insulating in-plane Néel-type antiferromagnetic phase, in which a residual antiunitary symmetry forbids the AHE. Hole doping induces a spin-flop transition to an out-of-plane Néel-type antiferromagnetic phase, thereby breaking this symmetry and generating a finite anomalous Hall conductivity that persists into the strongly correlated regime. Distinct from a conventional spin-polarized Hall response in ferromagnets, the altermagnetic AHE receives equal and additive contributions from the two symmetry-related spin sectors and is accompanied by spin--edge-locked chiral states. Our results demonstrate that carrier doping and spin-rotationally invariant Hubbard interactions are sufficient to realize the altermagnetic AHE, without invoking an explicitly Ising-like interaction, and provide a realistic microscopic route toward its realization in correlated transition metal dichalcogenides monolayers.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work examines how non-magnetic disorder modifies the superconducting pairing mechanism in multi-Weyl semimetals. It finds that disorder tunes the system's instability from a topologically protected monopole state to a conventional s-wave state. The analysis relies on advanced diagrammatic techniques and reveals quantitative criteria for the survival of this superconducting phase transition in the presence of impurities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of pairing instabilities in topological semimetals under disorder is highly relevant to understanding superconductivity in novel quantum materials, which often exhibit strong coupling to topological features.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how non-magnetic impurity scattering tunes the pairing instability in a multi-Weyl semimetal, specifically analyzing the crossing between a topologically nontrivial monopole pairing channel and a conventional s-wave channel.

**Main result.** Disorder drives the pairing instability from the monopole to the conventional channel, and the crossing point is determined by disorder-dependent protection factors ($\eta_m, \eta_s$).

**Method.** The analysis uses Born and Abrikosov–Gor'kov theory, calculating self-energies and applying continuum Ginzburg-Landau estimates to study the competition between the two pairing instabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-node multi-Weyl semimetal characterized by opposite chirality at $\pm Q$ and a monopole charge $J$. The low-energy physics is described by a Hamiltonian involving pseudospin and orbital degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Pairing instability channels (monopole vs. s-wave), crossing criterion $rac{1 - \eta_s}{1 - \eta_m} < rac{T_c^{(0)}}{T_m^{(0)}}$, and the pair-breaking rates ($\Gamma_N, \Gamma_m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The disorder strength is characterized by the small-pocket window $q_{\max} 	ext{intra}\xi_{	ext{dis}} \ll 1 \ll |2Q|\xi_{	ext{dis}}$, and the monopole protection factor $\eta_m(J) = 1/(J+2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an explicitly specified projected impurity kernel, treating disorder as valley-diagonal and momentum-independent within each Fermi pocket.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 compares the coupled (red) versus decoupled (blue) crossing instability for $J=2$ and $J=3$, showing the persistence of the crossing under disorder broadening.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the low-energy model and the two competing pairing channels. It then calculates the disorder-induced pair-breaking rates for both channels using AG theory. Finally, it analyzes the crossing condition using both continuum Ginzburg-Landau estimates and coupled Lorentzian-DOS-plus-AG tests.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how non-magnetic impurity scattering affects the emergence and possible coexistence of pairing instabilities in a two-node multi-Weyl semimetal. Within an explicitly specified projected impurity kernel---valley diagonal and momentum independent across each Fermi pocket, the leading behaviour of non-magnetic disorder in the small-pocket window $q^{\max}_{\rm intra}ξ_{\rm dis}\ll1\ll|2\mathbf Q|ξ_{\rm dis}$, treated at leading order in Born and Abrikosov--Gor'kov theory---quenched disorder tunes the leading pairing instability from a topologically nontrivial monopole channel to a conventional ($s$-wave) one, extending our earlier clean-system analysis into the disordered regime. A Born self-energy calculation in the chiral band basis then gives: (i) a band-isotropic conventional channel that is Anderson protected against intra-node scalar disorder ($η_s=1$), introduced phenomenologically at the projected-band level; solving the competition for arbitrary $η_s$ yields the crossing criterion $(1-η_s)/(1-η_m)<T_{c0}^{(s)}/T_{c0}^{(m)}$, so the mechanism tolerates substantial loss of conventional-channel protection; and (ii) a rank-one monopole sector fixing $f_m$ as the exact eigenfunction with $η_m(J)=1/(J+2)$, exact given that kernel. The crossing location in $Γ_N/T_{c0}^{(m)}$ is set by $η_m(J)$, $η_s$, and $r=T_{c0}^{(s)}/T_{c0}^{(m)}$. From the clean projected BdG Hamiltonian the pure monopole nodes carry Berry charge $\pm J$, distinct from the gapped conventional solution; the clean nodal thermodynamics is charge-dependent, $N_{\rm SC}(E)\propto E^{2/J}$ and $C\propto T^{1+2/J}$, and the residual density of states shows a threshold only for $J=1$. The crossing lies in the moderately metallic regime, $μ/Γ_N\simeq11$--$14$ for the illustrative $T_{c0}^{(m)}/μ=0.133$ used in the figures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that active agents can collectively build robust transport networks in granular media through stigmergic interactions. By coupling movement to environmental remodeling, the system exhibits enhanced mobility in an optimal regime that exists well below the jamming limit. The findings provide a general framework for understanding self-organized transport in complex, deformable substrates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of self-organization driven by mechanical interactions and environmental memory is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, pattern formation, and active matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence of stigmergic transport—self-organized movement pathways—in deformable, granular environments.

**Main result.** Stigmergic transport emerges only above a critical packing fraction ($\phi_c$), leading to enhanced mobility, but this enhancement is suppressed as the system approaches the jamming transition ($\phi_J$).

**Method.** The study combines physical experiments using robotic walkers in granular media with a minimal stochastic simulation model.

**Model / system.** The system involves active agents (robotic walkers) moving through a deformable substrate of granular material (wooden cubes). The model tracks how movement modifies the local packing fraction ($\phi_i$) of the environment.

**Key observables.** Average velocity ($\langle v angle$), normalized mobility ($\mu$), and a memory order parameter ($m$) quantifying environmental structuring.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Packing fraction ($\phi$), critical packing fraction ($\phi_c$), and the jamming transition ($\phi_J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes material displacement is redistributed isotropically among neighbors, allowing for the persistence of depleted regions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the formation of particle traces and robot trajectories at different packing fractions, and plots show the evolution of mobility and the memory order parameter across the different transport regimes.

**Paper structure.** The work establishes the physical system, defines the competition between environmental memory and geometrical crowding, demonstrates the emergence of three regimes (random, trail formation, narrow path), and quantifies the optimal transport regime before jamming.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that stigmergic path formation emerges in a deformable environment through the interplay between environmental memory and geometrical crowding. Using experiments with robotic random walkers together with a minimal stochastic model, we demonstrate the onset of persistent self-reinforced transport pathways above a critical packing fraction $φ_c$, where environmental memory enhances walker mobility. As the jamming transition $φ_J$ is approached, increasing crowding progressively suppresses this transport enhancement despite the persistence of environmental memory. The resulting non-monotonic behavior reveals an optimal transport regime well below jamming. More generally, our work establishes how active agents can collectively build transport networks through purely mechanical interactions with a deformable substrate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method using tensor networks to directly observe the condensation of composite bosons in lattice quantum Hall states. By calculating nonlocal correlation functions, the authors confirm the existence of true long-range topological order. This provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool ($\Phi(R)$) to map out quantum phase transitions in these exotic quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between topological order, nonlocal correlation functions, and measurable local measurements (via quantum simulators) provides a concrete, testable framework for understanding exotic quantum matter beyond traditional solid-state theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to microscopically confirm the condensation of nonlocal composite bosons (CBs) that characterize the topological order in lattice quantum Hall (QH) states, and to define a nonlocal order parameter for phase transition detection.

**Main result.** Genuine long-range order is established in lattice QH states via CB condensation, and a nonlocal order parameter successfully pinpoints quantum phase transitions between trivial and topological regimes.

**Method.** The study employs tensor-network methods, specifically DMRG, to find ground states and calculate nonlocal correlation functions involving composite operators (Read-CBs and GM-CBs).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using lattice Hamiltonians for Quantum Hall states, including bosonic and fermionic Laughlin states, implemented on a lattice structure (e.g., Harper-Hofstadter model).

**Key observables.** Read-CB correlation function ($ho_R$), GM-CB correlation function ($ho_{GM}$), and the nonlocal order parameter $\Phi(R)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Flux per plaquette ($\alpha$), filling factor ($
u$), and interaction strengths ($U/t$ or $V/t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is currently limited to single-component, Abelian quantum Hall states and relies on the applicability of tensor network methods to reconstruct nonlocal operators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the correlation functions ($ho(\cdot)$) for different states and show the evolution of the normalized order parameter $\Phi(R)/ar{n}$ across phase transitions as a function of interaction strength.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from defining the problem (topological order in QH states) to applying advanced numerical techniques (DMRG/MPS) to calculate nonlocal correlators. It then demonstrates the emergence of long-range order and uses the derived order parameter to map out phase boundaries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological states of matter, such as quantum Hall states, are characterized by the absence of local order parameters. In the continuum, their topological order has been reinterpreted as the condensation of nonlocal composite bosons, but this condensation and the resulting long-range correlations have so far eluded direct confirmation beyond the simplest trial states. Here, we apply tensor-network methods to lattice Hamiltonians hosting quantum Hall states, revealing this exotic order directly in interacting ground states and demonstrating both the long-range correlations and the condensation of composite bosons. These correlations define a nonlocal order parameter that identifies phase transitions between trivial and topological phases, for both bosonic and fermionic models. The corresponding operators are accessible through site-resolved, local measurements on an extensive number of particles, making them directly probable in quantum simulators. Our work opens new avenues for microscopic studies of topological quantum matter beyond the reach of traditional solid-state approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work proposes a four-terminal Josephson junction using p-wave magnets and p_y-wave superconductors to achieve perfect, phase-controlled spin and charge diode effects. By analyzing the system's Hamiltonian and using Green's functions, the authors demonstrate that the nonreciprocity can be robustly controlled by external parameters like gate voltage and crystal orientation. This establishes the junction as a highly tunable platform for dissipationless superconducting spintronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of unconventional superconductivity (p-wave) with magnetic proximity effects in a multi-terminal junction provides a rich platform for studying non-equilibrium quantum transport, which is highly relevant to superconducting spintronics and quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the realization and control of perfect nonlocal spin and charge diode effects in a complex four-terminal Josephson junction.

**Main result.** The setup enables fully nonreciprocal spin and charge transport, with the diode efficiency being highly tunable via gate voltage and crystallographic orientation, and remaining robust against disorder.

**Method.** The study employs a theoretical Hamiltonian formalism, solving the system using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) equation and Green's function techniques.

**Model / system.** The platform is a four-terminal Josephson junction featuring a normal-metal barrier coupled to superconducting leads: top/bottom leads are equal-spin triplet p_y-wave superconductors, and left/right leads are p-wave magnets proximity-coupled to s-wave superconductivity.

**Key observables.** Nonreciprocal spin and charge supercurrents (diode effects).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse macroscopic phase difference, gate voltage applied to the barrier, and crystallographic orientation of the p-wave magnet.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The NM barrier and p-wave SC leads are assumed not to host localized magnetic moments, allowing for block-diagonalization in the sectoral basis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a schematic illustration of the four-terminal model, showing the coupling of the NM barrier to the four distinct superconducting leads.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the goal of studying diode effects. It then details the theoretical framework using the full Hamiltonian decomposition and the BdG formalism. The core analysis involves calculating currents using Green's functions, followed by demonstrating the nonreciprocity and tunability of the diode response.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate charge and spin transport in a four-terminal Josephson junction with a normal-metal barrier. The top and bottom superconducting leads are equal-spin triplet $p_y$-wave superconductors, while the left and right leads are $p$-wave magnets with proximity-induced conventional $s$-wave superconductivity. When the transverse macroscopic phase difference between the top and bottom leads is set to zero, a longitudinal phase bias generates a pure transverse spin current with perfect 100% nonreciprocity. Remarkably, a finite transverse phase difference preserves the perfect spin-diode effect while simultaneously inducing a perfect charge-diode effect, enabling fully nonreciprocal spin and charge transport. Moreover, the spin-diode efficiency exhibits sharp, step-like switching as a function of both the gate voltage applied to the barrier and the crystallographic orientation of the $p$-wave magnet, providing independent and experimentally accessible knobs for controlling the diode polarity. The diode response remains robust against asymmetric interface couplings, nonmagnetic disorder, variations in the relative singlet and triplet pairing strengths, temperature, and junction dimensions, demonstrating that the effect is not a consequence of fine-tuned parameters. These findings establish the proposed four-terminal junction as a highly tunable and structurally robust platform for dissipationless, phase-controlled spin and charge rectification, with potential applications in superconducting spintronics.

</details>
