**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the computational complexity of BosonSampling when using simplified, binary measurements like threshold or parity detection, which are more relevant to current experiments than ideal photon-number resolution. The authors prove that estimating the typical output probabilities for these binary measurements remains \#P-hard, demonstrating that the inherent quantum hardness persists even under natural coarse-graining.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of quantum computation models by showing that the hard computational structure of BosonSampling survives natural, experimentally relevant measurement approximations (threshold/parity).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Characterizing the computational complexity of BosonSampling when using binary readout measurements (threshold or parity) instead of ideal photon-number-resolving detection.

**Main result.** The average-case estimation of typical output probabilities for both threshold and parity BosonSampling is proven to be \#P-hard in the linear-mode regime.

**Method.** The authors use a Fourier-coefficient extraction method, induced by coherent beam-splitter rotations, to embed a worst-case \#P-hard instance into the coarse-grained output probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is BosonSampling, involving $n$ indistinguishable photons passing through a random linear-optical circuit ($U$). The analysis focuses on the linear-mode regime where the number of modes scales linearly with the number of photons.

**Key observables.** Threshold measurement probabilities ($T_x(U)$) and Parity measurement probabilities ($P_x(U)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The linear-mode regime ($m = \alpha n$ with $\alpha > 2$), and the error bounds $\epsilon$ and $\delta$ for the hardness proof.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The hardness proof relies on establishing average-case hardness for typical outcomes and assumes specific anti-concentration properties for Fourier coefficients (Conjecture 1 and Conjecture 2).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the basic BosonSampling setup, comparing PNR, threshold, and parity measurements on the output occupation pattern.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the hardness problems for TBS and PBS, then developing the core technical tool—the Fourier-coefficient extraction method—to show that the hard structure persists under binary coarse-graining, culminating in separate proofs for the \#P-hardness of ATE and APE.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

BosonSampling is among the most prominent candidates for demonstrating quantum advantage. However, while the hardness of BosonSampling relies on photon-number-resolving detection, many experimentally relevant settings and applications instead use binary readout based on threshold or parity measurements, whose computational complexity has not yet been rigorously characterized. In this work, we investigate the computational complexity of BosonSampling with threshold and parity measurements in the linear-mode regime, where the number of modes scales linearly with the number of photons and is most relevant to current experiments. In particular, we establish average-case #P-hardness of estimating typical output probabilities in threshold and parity BosonSampling, a crucial ingredient in proving the classical hardness of the corresponding sampling problems. The resulting imprecision bounds match those obtained in prior hardness results for standard photon-number-resolving BosonSampling in the linear-mode regime. The key technical ingredient is a Fourier-coefficient extraction method, induced by coherent beam-splitter rotations, that extracts hidden hard components within coarse-grained output probabilities. These results indicate that the hard output-probability structure of photon-number-resolving BosonSampling can persist under natural binary coarse-grainings, even in collision-dominant regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines how finite-range soft-core interactions affect the stability of quantum droplets. By solving the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation, the authors find that increasing interaction strength causes the excitation spectrum to develop a roton minimum. This softening signals an instability, which is experimentally verifiable through the growth of density correlations in the static structure factor.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the stability boundaries of quantum matter systems (like Bose-Einstein condensates) when interactions are non-local or have finite range, which is crucial for understanding superfluid behavior in ultracold atomic gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence of roton instability in self-bound quantum droplets due to the inclusion of finite-range soft-core interactions.

**Main result.** The roton energy softens towards zero as the interaction strength and range increase, signaling a dynamical instability toward density modulation, which is confirmed by peaks in the static structure factor.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling by solving the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation and analyzing elementary excitations via the Bogoliubov ansatz.

**Model / system.** The system consists of self-bound quantum droplets interacting via a finite-range soft-core potential, modeled by an extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation incorporating Lee-Huang-Yang corrections and a nonlocal interaction term.

**Key observables.** Collective excitation spectrum $\omega(k)$ (looking for roton minimum) and the static structure factor $S(k)$ (looking for pronounced peaks at roton momentum).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlocal interaction strength ($U$) and interaction range ($R$), analyzed in the large-droplet (thermodynamic) limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Bogoliubov approximation for elementary excitations and assumes bulk properties dominate in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the density profile transitioning from uniform to modulated/fragmented, and show the excitation spectrum $\omega(k)$ and static structure factor $S(k)$ developing roton features as interaction strength increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first sets up the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation incorporating the finite-range interaction. It then analyzes the ground state and collective excitations using the Bogoliubov ansatz, deriving $\omega(k)$ and $S(k)$ to diagnose the onset of roton instability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the emergence of roton instability in self-bound quantum droplets interacting via a finite-range soft-core potential modeled by a Heaviside step interaction. The ground-state properties are obtained by solving the extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation including Lee-Huang-Yang corrections with a nonlocal interaction term. The collective excitation spectrum reveals the formation and progressive softening of a roton minimum as the interaction strength and range increase. When the roton energy approaches zero, the system becomes unstable, signaling the onset of density modulation. The rotonic behavior is further characterized through the static structure factor, which exhibits pronounced peaks at the roton momentum.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes frustrated magnetism in a corner-connected square plaquette lattice using a Heisenberg model. By combining Luttinger-Tisza minimization and Monte Carlo simulations, the authors map out a phase diagram revealing three distinct magnetic states. The most notable finding is a degenerate spin state (DS) exhibiting complex, multi-$\mathbf{q}$ ordering, which is further characterized by field-induced layer correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed mapping of competing interactions and the emergence of a novel, highly degenerate spin state provides a rich theoretical playground for understanding frustrated magnetism, which is central to correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex, frustrated magnetic ground states arising from competing exchange interactions in a specific lattice geometry.

**Main result.** The system exhibits three low-temperature phases (AF, FM, and a Degenerate Spin State (DS)), with the DS state characterized by unique quasi-two-dimensional ordering and field-induced layer correlations.

**Method.** The research combines Luttinger-Tisza (LT) minimization for zero-temperature candidates with large-scale Monte Carlo (MC) simulations to map the classical magnetic phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The model is a Heisenberg spin system defined on a corner-connected square plaquette network, featuring three types of exchange couplings: $J_1$ (edges), $J_3$ (diagonals), and $J_2$ (inter-plaquette corners).

**Key observables.** Low-temperature magnetic phase diagram, two-point spin-spin correlation functions, plaquette-adapted staggered magnetization ($M_s$), and the four-spin order parameter ($O_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Normalized ratios of exchange couplings $(J_1/|J_2|, J_3/|J_2|)$ and the external magnetic field ($H$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the classical limit using hard-spin constraints in MC simulations, and the LT method provides zero-temperature lower bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice geometry (plaquettes, unit cell) and show the field-temperature ($H-T$) phase diagram for the DS regime, highlighting the separation between positively and negatively correlated layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and Hamiltonian, then applies LT analysis to find candidate ground states, followed by MC simulations to validate these states, map the phase diagram, and analyze the system's response to external fields.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate frustrated magnetism of a corner-connected square plaquette Heisenberg model with exchange interactions along the edges $(J_1)$, along inter-plaquette links $(J_2)$, and along square diagonals $(J_3)$. Using Luttinger--Tisza (LT) minimization of the Fourier interaction matrix $\mathcal J(\mathbf q)$ together with large-scale Monte Carlo (MC) simulations, we obtain a classical low-temperature magnetic phase diagram in the normalized plane $(J_1/|J_2|, J_3/|J_2|)$. The two methods play complementary roles: the LT analysis provides the zero-temperature candidate ordering wave vectors, while the MC simulations elucidate the resulting ordering tendencies under the hard-spin constraint at low but finite temperatures. Three regimes emerge at low temperatures, an antiferromagnetic phase (AF), a ferromagnetic phase (FM) and a degenerate spin state with \textit{up-up-down-down (uudd)} ordering (DS). For the frustrated degenerate spinstate, LT exhibits line-like minima along $q_x=\pm q_y$ in the $hk$-plane, revealing a highly degenerate spin configuration which violates the hard-spin constraint. The MC results uncover a state with quasi-two-dimensional ordering. The DS regime is intrinsically multi-$\mathbf{q}$: the ordered texture assembles itself from symmetry-related modes on the lines, producing a ``\textit{uudd}'' spin arrangement comprising of distinctive strong antiferromagnetic correlations on diagonals, ferromagnetic correlations on corner links, and highly suppressed correlations on the edges. The field-temperature phase diagram for a representative parameter point in the DS regime, determined by MC simulations, yields field induced distinct regions of negatively and positively correlated layers. These two regions are separated by a curve corresponding to negligible inter-layer correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Graphix, a novel Python software framework designed to standardize and simplify the development of Measurement-Based Quantum Computation (MBQC) protocols. It provides a unified environment that handles the complex interplay between graph states, single-qubit measurements, and Pauli corrections. Graphix aims to accelerate research by offering a comprehensive, extensible infrastructure for the entire MBQC workflow.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation, the rigorous mathematical treatment of flow reconstruction (Causal, Generalised, Pauli) and the standardization of patterns provides deep insights into the underlying algebraic structure of quantum operations, which is relevant to understanding constraints in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The field lacks dedicated, unified software tools to bridge the theoretical foundations of Measurement-Based Quantum Computation (MBQC) with practical, extensible research workflows.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Graphix, a comprehensive Python framework that provides a unified, modular environment for developing, simulating, and analyzing MBQC protocols.

**Method.** The framework is built around the measurement calculus, allowing for the native representation of arbitrary MBQC computations, including state preparation, entanglement, measurements, and Pauli corrections.

**Model / system.** The model is Measurement-Based Quantum Computation (MBQC), which utilizes a graph state as a resource and performs computation via single-qubit measurements followed by classical feed-forward Pauli corrections.

**Key observables.** The induced partial order ($\prec$) on measured nodes, and the resulting Pauli corrections ($X_s, Z_s$) applied to the resource state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the open graph $\Gamma$, the measurement angles ($\lambda, \alpha$), and the specific Pauli correction strategies ($x, z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption for computation is that the transitive closure of the correction set must define a strict partial order; standardization procedures assume the input pattern is well-formed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates an MBQC computation (Hadamard gate) showing the open graph, measurement maps, and resulting Pauli corrections (red arrows).

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews essential MBQC concepts (Section 2) using Graphix examples, followed by a detailed description of the library architecture and advanced formalisms like flow reconstruction (Sections 3 and beyond).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurement-based quantum computing (MBQC) is a powerful model for quantum computation, but dedicated software tools that bridge its theoretical foundations with practical research workflows remain limited. We present Graphix, a software framework for MBQC written in Python that provides a unified environment for developing, integrating, and exploring measurement-based protocols and algorithms. Graphix establishes a modular, extensible, and user-friendly architecture for the compilation and simulation of quantum computations in the MBQC model with abstractions closely aligned with the theoretical formulations of MBQC. Through examples drawn from recent research on MBQC, we demonstrate Graphix's ability to reproduce and extend previous results. Graphix thus provides a software infrastructure for MBQC, supporting education and research while enabling collaborative development and accelerating the transition toward practical implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Windowed Least Squares Amplitude Estimation (WLSAE), a quantum algorithm designed to estimate an unknown amplitude with high precision. It achieves a near-optimal query-depth scaling that is remarkably uniform across all possible input amplitudes. This robustness makes it a strong candidate for practical quantum applications like overlap certification in the early fault-tolerant regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant algorithmic improvement for fundamental quantum estimation tasks, directly impacting the feasibility of quantum simulation and verification protocols that rely on accurate amplitude measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of achieving near-optimal query-depth tradeoffs in Amplitude Estimation (AE) algorithms, particularly ensuring the quantum speedup persists uniformly across the entire range of possible amplitudes.

**Main result.** A novel algorithm, WLSAE, achieves the nearly optimal query-depth tradeoff $M^2N \in \widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$ uniformly for all amplitudes $a \in [0, 1]$, overcoming limitations of prior methods at the boundaries.

**Method.** The method develops Windowed Least Squares Amplitude Estimation (WLSAE) by sampling Grover circuit depths from a carefully constructed, admissible probability window $p$. The error is bounded using concentration inequalities like Bernstein's inequality.

**Model / system.** The core task is estimating the amplitude $a = \langle\psi| P |\psiangle$ (or the angle $\lambda = \arcsin(\sqrt{a})$) associated with a unitary evolution $U$ and a projector $P$. The measurement relies on outcomes from circuits involving the Grover operator $G$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the estimated amplitude $\hat{a}$ or angle $\hat{	heta}$, with the goal of bounding the additive error $|\hat{	heta} - \lambda| \le \epsilon$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the target error $\epsilon$, the maximum circuit depth $M$, and the number of shots $N$. The performance is analyzed across the full range of amplitudes $a \in [0, 1]$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of an admissible probability window $p$ and relies on bounding the deviation of a loss function derived from quantum measurements using concentration inequalities.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares various window functions (Gaussian, Uniform, etc.) for WLSAE, showing performance relative to the Cramér–Rao bound. Figure 2 confirms the algorithm's amplitude-uniformity by showing constant error bounds across $\lambda \in [0, \pi/2]$ for a fixed query budget.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the problem and the WLSAE framework. It then derives rigorous bounds on the estimation error by analyzing the variance of the loss function across the depth window. Finally, it applies these bounds to establish the near-optimal query-depth scaling and demonstrates the method's practical applicability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a class of amplitude estimation algorithms with tunable circuit depth $M$ and circuit repetitions $N$, requiring neither ancilla qubits nor controlled Grover operations. For additive error $ε$ in the Grover angle, they attain the nearly optimal query-depth tradeoff $M^2N\in\widetilde{O}(ε^{-2})$ uniformly over $λ\in[0,π/2]$, spanning the full range from classical sampling at $M=1$ to the Heisenberg limit at $M=Θ(ε^{-1})$. While prior depth-tunable work establishes comparable angle-accuracy guarantees only away from the boundaries $λ\to0$ or $π/2$ or at discrete depth tradeoffs, our guarantee extends to both boundaries, so the quantum speedup persists there rather than degrading to classical sampling. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted uniform angle accuracy and show low overhead in practice, making them strong candidates for practical amplitude estimation in the early fault-tolerant regime, with applications such as overlap certification, trial-state verification, and Monte Carlo methods.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how the spectral properties of random matrices change when the underlying correlations decay slowly (power-law). Using the Matrix-Dyson-Equation framework, the authors derive exact results for the bulk density deformation and pinpoint critical exponents that govern the transition between different spectral regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on random matrices, the mathematical techniques used to analyze spectral transitions and critical exponents are highly relevant to understanding phase transitions in complex quantum many-body systems, such as those modeled by disordered Hamiltonians or interacting quantum gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the spectral behavior (bulk and edge) of Wigner-type random matrices whose underlying covariance structure exhibits long-range temporal correlations.

**Main result.** The bulk spectral density deforms from the semicircle law in a predictable manner, and the analysis establishes critical exponents ($\gamma_c=1/2$) governing the divergence of bulk moments for power-law correlations.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques involving the Matrix-Dyson-Equation (MDE) framework, moment calculations, and the derivation of a scalar self-consistent equation for the Stieltjes transform.

**Model / system.** The model consists of symmetric $N 	imes N$ Wigner-type random matrices constructed from row-independent stationary Gaussian sequences, allowing for controlled correlation decay via AR(1) or power-law structures.

**Key observables.** Bulk spectral density $\varrho(x)$, the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}(S)$, and the fourth moment $\mu_4(ho)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correlation decay parameter $ho$ (for AR(1)) and the power-law exponent $\gamma$ (for $d	au \sim t^{-\gamma}$), with critical points at $\gamma_c=1/2$ and $\gamma=1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on verifying hypotheses (A)-(E) of the MDE framework, though limitations exist for strong correlations ($ho \gtrsim 0.7$).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare empirical spectral histograms against theoretical predictions (e.g., for $ho=0.5, 0.8$) and show the collapse of top eigenvalues onto a singular-value cascade for the noiseless Volterra ensemble.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing the AR(1) case, verifying MDE hypotheses and deriving the self-consistent equation. It then extends the analysis to power-law correlations, rigorously determining critical exponents ($\gamma_c$) and resolving the apparent conflict between smooth crossovers and discrete phase transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study long-range correlated Wigner-type matrices built from row-independent stationary Gaussian sequences. For exponentially decaying (AR(1)) correlations, the bulk spectral density deforms from the semicircle law via an explicit combinatorial "hub" mechanism, yet we verify the flatness and decay hypotheses of the matrix-Dyson-equation framework (MDE), with numerical evidence supporting Tracy-Widom edge universality for every fixed $ρ<1$ of the exponential decay correlations; the degenerate limit $ρ\to1^-$ reduces to a symmetrized Volterra operator, connecting to the singular-value cascade identified in a companion BBP analysis. For power-law correlations $dt\sim t^{-γ}$, we identify $γ_c=1/2$ as the critical point for divergence of the bulk fourth-moment, while $γ=1$ marks the breakdown of the flatness condition governing the MDE edge analysis. We prove the fourth-moment transition exactly and find numerically that the self-consistent edge varies smoothly across $γ=1$, with no evidence of a kink or discontinuity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work derives a fundamental thermodynamic bound showing that the computational error in stochastic networks is limited by the entropy production rate and the system's memory capacity. By applying this to models of biochemical processes, the authors establish a quantitative framework for understanding the energetic costs associated with biological computation. It demonstrates that high accuracy requires either significant energy dissipation or the retention of long-lived memory states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between non-equilibrium thermodynamics (entropy production) and information processing capacity provides a fundamental, quantitative constraint on how efficiently biological systems can compute.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a universal thermodynamic bound on the accuracy of computation performed by stochastic networks.

**Main result.** The lag error in computation is bounded by the product of the entropy production rate and a memory time ($\epsilon^2_{	ext{lag}} \le 	au_m \sigma$).

**Method.** The analysis decomposes the total error into representation and lag components, deriving the bound using entropy production rates and relative entropy decay.

**Model / system.** The study uses reversible continuous-time Markov networks to model biological information processing, including systems like ion channels, Lac repressors, and NPQ.

**Key observables.** Computational error ($\epsilon^2$), Entropy Production Rate ($\sigma$), and Memory Time ($	au_m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bound is derived for the near-equilibrium limit, highlighting the trade-off between dissipation and memory storage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decomposing the error and using quadratic approximations for entropy production in some models, though the bound itself is shown to be general.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the integrated lag error against the derived thermodynamic bound across various random input protocols for multiple biochemical models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the error decomposition, derives the core thermodynamic bound ($\epsilon^2_{	ext{lag}} \le 	au_m \sigma$), and then applies this framework to test it across several distinct, complex biological Markov models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Biological and artificial networks compute by transforming time-dependent inputs into functional outputs. Because the internal state of a stochastic network relaxes on finite timescales, its output generally lags behind a changing environment, producing computational errors. We show that for reversible continuous-time Markov networks the error admits a universal thermodynamic bound. Decomposing the total error into representation and lag contributions, we derive an inequality relating the lag error to the entropy production rate and a memory time equal to the integrated equilibrium autocorrelation of the output observable. The bound implies that accurate dynamical computation requires either substantial dissipation or long-lived memory encoded in slowly relaxing modes. We demonstrate these principles in artificial Markov networks and in models of biochemical information processing. Our results establish a thermodynamic limit on information processing in stochastic networks and provide a quantitative framework for understanding the energetic costs of biological computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the training loss of diffusion models, showing that the irreducible excess loss is mathematically equivalent to the integrated Fisher-Rao metric along the diffusion path. By deriving this from the Schrödinger bridge principle, it provides a deep geometric understanding of how the training objective captures the intrinsic information geometry of the data manifold.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML, the rigorous connection between information geometry (Fisher metric) and the fundamental path-space structure of stochastic processes is highly relevant to understanding the geometry of quantum state estimation and open quantum system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper quantifies the 'irreducible excess' in the training loss of Denoising Score Matching (DSM) by relating it to the intrinsic geometry of the diffusion process.

**Main result.** This excess loss is exactly equal to the integrated trace of the Fisher–Rao metric of the conditional endpoint family, providing a geometric interpretation of the training objective floor.

**Method.** The derivation utilizes the Schrödinger bridge variational principle and employs information-theoretic tools, specifically relating the loss to the rate of mutual information loss.

**Model / system.** The model is a general diffusion process used in diffusion models, connecting a clean data distribution ($P_0$) to a noisy state ($P_T$) via a corruption kernel.

**Key observables.** Loss Floor, Fisher–Rao metric ($g(x, t)$), Mutual Information $I(y; x_t)$, and the excess action $\Delta S$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffusion time $T$, noise schedule parameters ($\sigma_t, \alpha_t$), and the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Mild regularity assumptions are required for the general corruption kernel, and the analysis contrasts second-order training geometry with third-order sampling errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the agreement between the calculated floor ($rac{1}{2} R 	ext{MMSE} d\lambda$) and the entropy change ($\Delta h$) for Gaussian mixtures, and confirm the scaling of the floor divergence rate with the data rank $k$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general variational principle (Schrödinger bridge) to derive the geometric characterization of the loss floor via the Fisher metric, and then applies this to specific Gaussian cases to derive closed forms and test identities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Denoising score matching trains diffusion models by regressing onto a conditional score, although generation ultimately requires the marginal score. The two objectives share the same population minimizer, but the conditional target remains random at fixed noisy state and introduces an irreducible excess in the training loss. We isolate this excess and show that, for a general corruption kernel under mild regularity assumptions, it is exactly the trace of the Fisher--Rao metric of the conditional endpoint family, integrated along the diffusion trajectory. This gives an exact conditional-variance decomposition of the denoising objective and identifies the information geometry observed in diffusion latent spaces as an intrinsic component of the training loss. We derive the result from a Schr"odinger bridge variational principle, in which the ideal objective arises as excess path-space relative entropy. For corruption diffusions, the Fisher term is proportional to the rate at which the noisy state loses mutual information about the clean data, separating the loss floor into an information flow determined by the data and a weight determined by the corruption schedule and objective. In the Gaussian case, this yields a closed form for the floor, recovers reparametrization invariance of the continuous-time objective, and relates its high-SNR divergence to the information dimension of the data. Finally, we show that raw losses obtained with different noise ranges or weightings need not rank models consistently because they contain different additive floors, and contrast the second-order geometry seen by training with the third-order conditional statistics entering numerical sampling error.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper simulates the random sequential adsorption of DLA clusters on a lattice to study jamming states. It systematically analyzes how varying the diversity of cluster shapes—from fixed to highly polydisperse—affects the final jamming density and its fluctuations. The findings reveal that increased shape diversity leads to denser packing, providing quantitative scaling laws for these non-equilibrium deposition processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the study of jamming transitions and the role of quenched disorder (shape diversity) in determining macroscopic properties has analogies in understanding complex energy landscapes or disordered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the diversity of cluster shapes affects the jamming properties when using Diffusion-Limited Aggregation (DLA) clusters for irreversible adsorption.

**Main result.** The jamming density decreases with cluster size as a power-law, and increasing shape polydispersity promotes denser packing. Furthermore, the fluctuation scaling of the jamming density differs significantly depending on whether the cluster shape pool is fixed or refreshed.

**Method.** The research employs large-scale Monte Carlo simulations to model the Random Sequential Adsorption (RSA) process using DLA clusters on a square lattice.

**Model / system.** The system is the irreversible adsorption of DLA clusters on a square lattice, analyzed through several model variants: fixed-shape, refreshing-shape, and polydisperse variants controlled by a pool size $S$.

**Key observables.** Jamming density ($p_j$), the scaling exponent $\alpha$ in $p_j(k) - p_\infty \sim k^{-\alpha}$, and the fluctuation of the jamming density ($\sigma(L)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cluster size ($k$), system size ($L$), and the degree of shape diversity (controlled by $S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that differences between model variants diminish at very large cluster sizes ($k 	o \infty$) due to the statistical self-similarity of DLA clusters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate representative DLA clusters showing shape diversity and show the scaling behavior of the jamming density and its fluctuations ($\sigma(L)$) for different model variants.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results from multiple model variants (fixed vs. refreshing vs. polydisperse) to quantify the role of shape diversity on the jamming density and its fluctuations, establishing scaling laws for both observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by the ubiquity of ramified fractal deposits in nature and engineered systems, we investigate the irreversible adsorption of diffusion-limited aggregation (DLA) clusters on a square lattice. We study the role of cluster shape diversity on jamming properties of the system by systematically controlling the number of distinct shapes used across and within realizations, encompassing both monodisperse and polydisperse model variants. Our large-scale simulations over a broad range of cluster sizes $2\leqslant k\leqslant4096$ show that the jamming density decreases with cluster size as a power-law $p_j(k)-p_j^\infty\sim k^{-α}$. Both $α$ and $p_j^\infty$ are found to depend on the degree of shape diversity, with $α$ ranging from $0.374(2)$ to $0.417(1)$. It is observed that increasing shape polydispersity promotes denser packing. Importantly, the fluctuations of the jamming density exhibit distinct scaling behavior: $σ(L)\sim1/L$ for a fixed pool of cluster shape(s), but remain $L$-independent when the pool of shape(s) is refreshed across different realizations. Furthermore, our results demonstrate that the differences between the model variants systematically diminish with increasing $k$ and are expected to vanish as $k\to\infty$ due to the statistical self-similarity of the DLA clusters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a perturbative theory for quantum tunneling in the thin-barrier limit, moving beyond the standard WKB approximation. It derives that the time-dependent tunneling probability depends critically on system symmetry, predicting quadratic growth for resonant cases and linear growth otherwise. This work provides essential theoretical tools for modeling energy leakage and coherent dynamics in engineered quantum barriers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of time-dependent tunneling rates, especially the distinction between $t^2$ and linear growth, provides crucial theoretical benchmarks for understanding coherent energy transfer in engineered quantum systems like optical lattices or mesoscopic structures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes quantum tunneling when the tunneling action is small, specifically in the thin-barrier limit, contrasting with standard WKB methods.

**Main result.** The tunneling probability exhibits distinct time dependencies depending on the system's symmetry: quadratic growth ($\propto t^2$) in resonant/symmetric cases and linear growth in non-resonant/asymmetric cases.

**Method.** The analysis employs a perturbative expansion in the barrier strength ($\alpha$) and utilizes time-dependent Schrödinger equation solutions, often involving Green's functions or eigenbasis expansions.

**Model / system.** The system models a quantum particle tunneling through a potential barrier, which is idealized as a delta function barrier ($\alpha^{-1}\delta(x)$) in the thin-barrier limit. The Hamiltonian is solved using time-dependent methods.

**Key observables.** Tunneling probability ($P_R(t)$) and the time-dependent tunneling wavefunction $\psi(t, x>a)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The barrier strength $\alpha$ (control parameter), and the energy mismatch $\Delta E(a)^0 = E_a^0 - E_s^0$ which dictates the early-time rate.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative approximations in $\alpha$ and uses specific limits (e.g., early-time approximations, quadratic order in $\alpha$) to simplify the complex time evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the potential barriers (finite vs. delta function) and show the lowest energy wavefunctions for both zero and small non-zero barrier strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the thin-barrier limit equivalence, then solving the $\alpha=0$ case, and finally introducing the barrier strength perturbatively to derive the time-dependent tunneling probability in various regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The usual WKB analysis for quantum tunneling applies when the tunneling action is large, as it is for tall, wide potential barriers. In contrast we analyze tunneling when the action is small, as it is for tunneling across a tall, thin barrier. We develop a perturbative analysis where the control parameter is the inverse of the area under the potential barrier and apply our technique to several examples in $1+1$ dimensions. In resonant situations for bound particles we find that the tunneling probability grows with time as $\propto t^2$, while in non-resonant situations it grows linearly with time. We evaluate not only the tunneling probability but also the time-dependent tunneling wavefunction for a particle that escapes to infinity, {\it i.e.} from a quasi-bound state to the continuum.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the effect of quenched disorder on the electronic structure of the Kagomé lattice using the cluster CPA. The key finding is that disorder-induced renormalization of hopping terms can drive the system into a gapless phase featuring conducting edge modes. This suggests that disorder can qualitatively modify the electronic spectrum in a manner reminiscent of topological phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work uses advanced many-body techniques (CPA) to study how quenched disorder can induce emergent, quasi-topological features (conducting edges) in a lattice model, which is highly relevant to understanding disorder effects in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how disorder can induce conducting edge states on the Kagomé lattice, potentially mimicking or modifying topological behavior.

**Main result.** Disorder averaging generates a non-local self-energy that drives the system through a gapless phase characterized by conducting edge modes over a broad range of impurity concentrations.

**Method.** The authors employ a cluster extension of the Coherent Potential Approximation (CPA) within a locator-interactor formalism to calculate disorder averages.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Kagomé lattice, analyzed in a strip geometry. The model starts with a non-interacting Hamiltonian including nearest-neighbor and next-nearest-neighbor couplings, and disorder is introduced via random on-site energies (binary mixture).

**Key observables.** Spectral function, Total Density of States (DOS), Partial Density of States (PDOS) for bulk and edge contributions, and the disorder self-energy ($\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Impurity concentration (disorder strength $\delta$), and the ratio of disorder effects versus intrinsic Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the cluster CPA approximation, which treats fluctuations up to the cluster size, and the disorder is restricted to a binary mixture distribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the spectral gap closure under disorder versus SOC. They show that the edge contribution to the PDOS is consistently larger than the bulk, and the spectral evolution due to disorder is qualitatively similar to SOC-driven changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the Hamiltonian and the CPA framework. It then calculates the spectral properties (DOS, PDOS) as a function of disorder strength, comparing these results to known SOC-driven topological transitions to analyze the origin of the gapless edge modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Within a cluster extension of the coherent potential approximation, disorder averaging generates a non-local self-energy that renormalizes both diagonal and off-diagonal hopping terms of the non-interacting Kagome-lattice Hamiltonian. These renormalizations (of both nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor hopping amplitudes) drive the system between two topologically trivial insulating states through an intermediate gapless phase characterized by conducting edge modes over a broad range of impurity concentrations. Our results demonstrate that multiple-scattering effects alone can generate emergent effective spin-orbit interactions and qualitatively modify the edge spectrum of disordered Kagome systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents an executable simulation framework for quantum repeater chains, using a novel Dynamic-Kernel/QPacket logic built on Q2NS/ns-3. It rigorously tests how entanglement distribution protocols behave under varying resource constraints, showing that overhead is dictated by complex policy interactions rather than just physical size. The study validates the platform's ability to support reproducible, policy-specific evaluation of quantum network concepts.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on networking, the concept of stateful, resource-constrained, and policy-driven local computation (the Dynamic Kernel) mirrors complex control mechanisms found in open quantum systems modeling, where local state updates dictate global evolution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing and simulating quantum network protocols, specifically entanglement distribution over repeater chains, which requires logic beyond classical layering.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that the overhead and cost of quantum packet forwarding are shaped not just by encoding, but critically by the policy choices and the availability of network resources.

**Method.** The authors implemented a specialized executable logic, the Dynamic-Kernel/QPacket, within the Q2NS/ns-3 simulation framework to model node-local decision-making pipelines.

**Model / system.** The system models a linear quantum repeater chain where entanglement is the primary resource. The core mechanism involves a Dynamic Kernel executing a Planner-Executor-Engine pipeline upon receiving a QPacket.

**Key observables.** Signaling load, QPacket meta-header growth, link-resolvability success probability, and accumulated forwarding delay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pre-distributed entanglement probability (p), generator placement probability (g), and chain length (N).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The implementation is deliberately scoped to a linear chain topology, and the Dynamic Kernel processing time is assumed instantaneous to isolate forwarding effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Dynamic-Kernel pipeline, show the linear growth of QPacket size with chain length, and plot classical signaling load and success probability as functions of pre-distributed entanglement probability.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for beyond-layering protocols, details the QPacket/Dynamic-Kernel mechanism (Planner-Executor-Engine), and then presents simulations analyzing how varying resource availability (p, g) affects performance metrics like success probability and signaling load.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Quantum Internet operates on entanglement, a non-local, non-copyable, stateful network resource, which motivates protocol organization beyond classical layering. We present a first executable specialization of the Dynamic Kernel/QPacket logic from the beyond-layering protocol suite, targeting entanglement distribution over a linear quantum repeater chain. The implementation builds on Q2NS, an ns-3-based quantum-network simulation module available through the ns-3 App Store. It realizes QPacket meta-headers with service intent and append-only action-commit stamps processed by node-local Dynamic Kernels organized as a Planner--Executor--Engine pipeline, while being deliberately scoped to an analytically verifiable service and policy. Within this scoped setting, we study node heterogeneity through a link-preparation policy that accounts for pre-distributed entanglement and uneven entanglement-generation support across nodes, including delegation via QPacket forwarding. Simulations verify analytical link-resolvability models and expose signaling load, forwarding behavior, and QPacket meta-header growth. Results show that QPacket overhead is shaped by more than just encoding, including policy choices and available network resources. Overall, this study demonstrates how the Q2NS/ns-3 substrate can support reproducible, policy-specific evaluation of quantum-native protocol-suite concepts.

</details>

**Authors:** Mauris Chueng, Hexiang Wang, Keheng Zhu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24261v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical analysis of disordered Ising models, focusing on how boundary conditions and disorder affect the system's free energy. The authors establish key convergence theorems for boundary free energies and prove conditions under which the system's thermodynamic state is uniquely determined (Gibbs-state selection). It advances the mathematical understanding of disordered critical phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical techniques used—especially those involving localization, convergence of random fields, and controlling fluctuations in disordered systems—are highly relevant to understanding quantum phase transitions and quantum disordered systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical properties of boundary free energies, quenched mixing, and the selection of the correct Gibbs state in disordered Ising models.

**Main result.** Key results include proving the existence of fixed-depth tangential pressure and establishing Gibbs-state selection independence under specific mixing conditions.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques from statistical mechanics, including concentration inequalities, derivative representations derived from concavity, and stochastic localization arguments.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on nearest-neighbor disordered Ising models, characterized by random couplings ($J$) and analyzed on finite volumes that are extrapolated to infinite limits (e.g., half-spaces or infinite strips).

**Key observables.** Boundary free energies ($\Delta F$), tangential pressure ($p_L(r)$), boundary-response mixing (BRM), and seam-flip differences ($D_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), dimension ($d \ge 2$), and the mixing condition $(2d-1)\mathbb E	anh(eta|J|) < 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assumptions regarding the geometry (regularity), the existence of certain mixing conditions (e.g., quenched exponential boundary-response mixing), and the ability to control fluctuations via concentration bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometry of the slab, marking boundary bonds and different types of cuts (tangential, normal). Figure 2 shows the nested one-dimensional exhaustion used in convergence proofs.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results by analyzing finite-volume identities, establishing the existence and properties of the tangential pressure limit, proving convergence results under mixing conditions, and concluding with discussions on necessary localization bounds and open problems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study boundary free energies and half-space responses in nearest-neighbor disordered Ising models. First, we prove the existence of fixed-depth tangential pressure and identify its derivative almost everywhere with the limiting boundary response. Second, under quenched exponential boundary-response mixing, we prove Gibbs-state selection independence and exponential convergence of finite-depth pressures. We further show that uniform one-spin mixing yields DLR uniqueness and Gaussian normal localization, and verify the required mixing conditions whenever $(2d-1)\mathbb E\tanh(β\vert{}J\vert{})<1$. Finally, we establish an all-face surface limit in the bounded Dobrushin regime and provide counterexamples that delimit general surface and stiffness claims.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes superconductivity in a two-orbital Hubbard model, demonstrating that inter-orbital interactions and hopping terms are crucial for enhancing pairing. Using VMC, the authors show that these orbital degrees of freedom stabilize superconductivity by coupling different electronic bands, which is a key feature distinguishing it from single-band models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of how orbital hybridization ($	ilde{t}$) modifies the pairing mechanism by coupling different energy scales (flat vs. broad bands) provides insights into unconventional pairing mechanisms beyond simple single-band descriptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how inter-orbital interactions and kinetic terms influence the emergence and magnitude of superconductivity in a multi-orbital system.

**Main result.** Inter-orbital terms ($U'$ and $	ilde{t}$) significantly enhance superconducting correlations compared to single-orbital models, with the enhancement being driven by the coexistence of flat and broad electronic bands.

**Method.** The research employs Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) techniques using Jastrow-Slater wave functions, supplemented by comparisons with Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a minimal two-orbital Hubbard Hamiltonian, incorporating intra-orbital repulsion ($U$), inter-orbital repulsion ($U'$), and hopping terms ($t$ and $	ilde{t}$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting pairing correlations ($\phi^2_{\alphaeta}$), condensation energy ($\Delta E$), and orbital/inter-orbital doublon densities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $U/t$, the inter-orbital repulsion $U'/U$, and the ratio of inter-orbital to intra-orbital hopping $	ilde{t}/t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on uniform nonmagnetic states with an explicit pairing amplitude, and the model does not include Hund's coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the superconducting order parameter vs. electron density and vs. hopping ratios, and show the decomposition of the condensation energy into contributions from intra-orbital potential, inter-orbital potential, and kinetic energy.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the effects of $U'$ (at fixed $	ilde{t}=0$) and then introduces $	ilde{t}$, showing how each term modifies the superconducting dome and the underlying electronic structure, culminating in a discussion of the interplay between orbital character and pairing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a minimal two-orbital Hubbard model with intra- and inter-orbital nearest-neighbor hopping $t$ and $\tilde{t}$, as well as intra- and inter-orbital density-density interactions $U$ and $U'$ by means of variational ansätze based on Jastrow-Slater wave functions within quantum Monte Carlo techniques. To focus on the electronic mechanisms of superconductivity, we restrict the variational ansätze to uniform nonmagnetic states with an explicit pairing amplitude and compute the pairing correlations as a function of filling and model parameters. At $U/t=10$, superconducting correlations are highly enhanced by the presence of inter-orbital terms, $U'$ and $\tilde{t}$. For $\tilde{t}=0$, a finite value of $U'$ effectively screens the intra-orbital repulsion $U$, producing a shift in the superconducting dome. Consequently, inter-orbital repulsion yields a sizable increase in electron pairing compared to the single-orbital baseline. Furthermore, introducing a finite $\tilde{t}$ provides an additional boost to superconducting correlations, an effect driven by the simultaneous presence of flat and broad bands in the electronic structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the $p$-spin Ising spin glass model to establish a link between reentrant phase transitions and temperature chaos. By showing that the phase boundary reenters, the authors deduce that the system must exhibit temperature chaos, meaning the spin-state overlap vanishes for all pairs of temperatures within the spin glass phase. This advances the understanding of complex glassy dynamics in disordered quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of spin glasses and their phase transitions is foundational to understanding complex energy landscapes, which has parallels in optimizing quantum circuits or analyzing complex many-body quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the physical relationship between two unusual properties in spin glasses: reentrant phase transitions and temperature chaos.

**Main result.** The authors analytically demonstrate that the ferromagnetic–spin glass boundary is reentrant for the $p$-spin Ising spin glass, which strongly suggests the existence of temperature chaos across the entire spin glass phase.

**Method.** The analysis involves expanding the free energy around the triple point and utilizing the contrapositive of a proposition relating reentrance to the existence of temperature chaos.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the fully connected Ising $p$-spin glass with a ferromagnetic bias, described by a Hamiltonian involving $p$-body interactions. The analysis uses replica calculations and free energy minimization techniques.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the curvature of the ferromagnetic–spin glass phase boundary and the two-temperature spin-state overlap distribution, $P_2(x | eta_1, eta_2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the spin interaction order $p$ (specifically $p>2$), the inverse temperatures ($eta_1, eta_2$), and the system size $N$ (taken to infinity).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that if the overlap vanishes for one temperature pair, it implies the vanishing of overlap for all pairs within the spin glass phase. The analysis also relies on the NOO proposition and the validity of the replica calculations.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference a schematic phase diagram for $p=3$ showing the PM, 1RSB-SG, RS-FM, and Nishimori Line boundaries, and a table listing calculated values for various parameters across different $p$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by establishing the logical connection between reentrance and temperature chaos, provides an explicit analytical calculation for the $p$-spin model showing reentrance, and supports this conclusion with independent two-temperature replica calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reentrant transitions and temperature chaos are two unusual properties of spin glasses and had long been considered physically unrelated. We recently established a logical relation between these two phenomena [Phys. Rev. E 112, 044140 (2025)]. In the present paper, we provide an explicit example of this logical relation in the fully connected Ising $p$-spin glass with a ferromagnetic bias. By expanding the free energy around the triple point, we show analytically that the ferromagnetic--spin glass boundary is reentrant in the vicinity of the triple point throughout the examined range of finite $p>2$. It then follows from the above logical relation that there exists at least one pair of distinct temperatures in the spin glass phase for which the overlap of spin configurations vanishes. This is a necessary condition for temperature chaos, but it would be quite unusual for the overlap to vanish only for selected temperature pairs but not for others within a single spin glass phase. These results therefore strongly suggest the existence of temperature chaos in the sense that the overlaps of spin configurations vanish for all temperature pairs throughout the spin glass phase. Independent evidence for this behavior is provided by a two-temperature replica calculation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a highly abstract mathematical framework using category theory and Pontryagin duality to formalize wave-particle complementarity. It demonstrates that the mathematical structure of this complementarity is determined by duality, but the physical scale, embodied by Planck's constant $\hbar$, must be imposed externally via the canonical commutation relations. It offers a deep, structural view of the de Broglie relation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly abstract, foundational mathematical underpinning for a core concept in quantum mechanics (complementarity), which could inspire new ways to model measurement processes or quantum field theory structures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously formulate wave-particle complementarity and the de Broglie relation within the advanced mathematical framework of categorical quantum mechanics.

**Main result.** It shows that while Pontryagin duality determines the structural form of complementarity, the physical constant $\hbar$ must be supplied externally via the Weyl commutation relations to yield the correct de Broglie relation $\lambda = h/p$.

**Method.** The authors employ categorical quantum mechanics on rigged Hilbert spaces, using continuous dagger-Frobenius structures to represent position and momentum operators, and utilizing Pontryagin duality to relate these structures via the Fourier transform.

**Model / system.** The model is the quantum mechanical description of a particle, analyzed using abstract mathematical structures like rigged Hilbert spaces and categorical formalism, rather than a specific physical Hamiltonian or lattice model.

**Key observables.** Position ($\hat{X}$), momentum ($\hat{P}$), and the associated wave-particle character pairing $\chi_p(x) = \exp(ipx/\hbar)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Planck's constant ($\hbar$), the scaling parameter $\alpha$, and the physical constant $h$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the validity of the categorical structure on Schwartz spaces and treats the identification $\alpha = \hbar$ as a necessary physical input fixed by the Weyl commutation relations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Fourier correspondence between the position and momentum representations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the mathematical setting using categorical quantum mechanics and Pontryagin duality. It then derives the Fourier transform relating the two observable structures, demonstrating that the structural relationship is dictated by duality, while the physical scale ($\hbar$) is fixed by the canonical commutation relations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a formulation wave-particle complementarity and the de Broglie relation $λ=h/p$ within the framework of categorical quantum mechanics on rigged Hilbert spaces. Position and momentum are represented by continuous dagger-Frobenius structures associated with the locally compact abelian group $\mathbb{R}$ and its Pontryagin dual. The Fourier transform is the unitary implementation of Pontryagin duality and relates the two observable structures. We show that a general character pairing $χ_p^{(α)}(x)=\exp(ipx/α)$ yields a one-parameter family of unitarily equivalent Fourier transforms. Pontryagin duality therefore determines the structural form of the complementarity, but not the numerical value of Planck's constant. The identification $α=\hbar$ is a physical input fixed by the Weyl commutation relations. With this input, the spatial period of the character yields $λ=h/p$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper advances the field of quantum randomness by proving the security of a certifiable randomness protocol in the QROM. Crucially, it achieves this unconditionally by restricting the quantum adversary to a shallow query depth, thereby bypassing the need for the unproven Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is quantum cryptography, the rigorous analysis of query complexity and the use of information-theoretic bounds are highly relevant to understanding the limitations and capabilities of quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To prove the security of a certifiable randomness protocol in the Quantum Random Oracle Model (QROM) without relying on the unproven Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture.

**Main result.** The authors unconditionally prove the certifiable randomness guarantee for the Yamakawa-Zhandry protocol against quantum adversaries making only $o(\log \lambda)$ adaptive queries.

**Method.** The proof uses a two-step argument involving bounding the 'query weight' accumulated by the adversary across multiple query layers, culminating in a contradiction derived from probability bounds.

**Model / system.** The analysis is set in the Quantum Random Oracle Model (QROM), involving a quantum adversary querying a random oracle $H$ to test the validity of a candidate codeword $x$ from a list-recoverable code $\mathcal{C}$.

**Key observables.** Query weight ($w$), query depth ($D$), and the probability of the adversary successfully cheating ($\Pr[	ext{Event3}]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The depth of the adversary's queries, $D = o(\log \lambda)$, and the entropy measures $\delta(\lambda)$ and $h_\infty(\lambda)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the restriction on the adversary's query depth to $o(\log \lambda)$ layers; the proof avoids the AA conjecture.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon the YZ protocol, first establishing the necessary tools (like the Swapping Lemma) to track query weight, then proceeding through a multi-step argument that bounds the adversary's ability to accumulate weight on the correct answer $x$ when the query depth is shallow.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a recent breakthrough, Yamakawa and Zhandry (J. ACM 2024) constructed a proof of quantumness in the quantum random oracle model (QROM) in which the quantum prover samples a codeword preimage of a publicly computable function H. They conjectured that given any H, a successful prover must sample their preimage from a high-entropy distribution over possible answers. If true, this would give a certifiable randomness protocol in the quantum random oracle model. As partial evidence for their conjecture, Yamakawa and Zhandry proved the security of their certifiable randomness protocol assuming the Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture.   We prove the security of the certifiable randomness protocol of Yamakawa-Zhandry unconditionally, without relying on the unproven Aaronson-Ambainis conjecture, against low query-depth quantum adversaries: specifically, adversaries that make up to o(\log λ) adaptive quantum queries to the random oracle.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous formalism to handle the composition of quantum subsystems when viewed from different Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs). It resolves inherent paradoxes by introducing 'extra-particle' degrees of freedom, establishing a consistent hierarchy of perspectives. The work shows that QRF composition is a generalization of standard quantum composition and introduces a procedure to recover classical descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work tackles foundational issues in quantum foundations concerning how local descriptions (reference frames) interact with global composition rules, which is crucial for understanding measurements and information flow in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental difficulty of defining compositionality when combining standard quantum composition rules with the formalism of Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs), which leads to paradoxes like the 'paradox of the third particle'.

**Main result.** The authors propose a consistent formalism that resolves these paradoxes by incorporating 'extra-particle' degrees of freedom, showing that QRF composition generalizes standard quantum tensor product composition.

**Method.** The methodology involves building upon a formal QRF framework, demonstrating consistency through mathematical constraints (like Equation 5), and introducing a procedure called 'classicalisation' to recover classical limits.

**Model / system.** The study uses abstract quantum systems (A, B, S, $S_3$) and their descriptions relative to different observers or reference frames (QRFs). Specific toy models involve systems carrying representations of finite Abelian groups ($\mathbb{Z}_N$).

**Key observables.** The consistency of state and operator representations across different observers, the set of compatible states for composition, and the phase/momentum superselection rules.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the reference frames (e.g., internal vs. external observers) and the nature of the subsystems being composed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism builds on a recent QRF framework; some analyses are restricted to finite Abelian groups ($\mathbb{Z}_N$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the setup comparing reduced versus extended systems involving three subsystems; Figure 2 illustrates the setup with two systems (A and S) viewed from an external observer (Eve).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the compositionality paradox in QRFs, then proposing a formalism using 'extra-particle' degrees of freedom to restore consistency, followed by characterizing the resulting structure, and concluding with the introduction and study of 'classicalisation'.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding a composite system through its constituents is a fundamental practice in physics. In the context of quantum reference frames (QRFs), however, combining the usual quantum-theoretic notion of composition with QRF perspectives gives rise to subtle issues, such as the 'paradox of the third particle'. Here we study in depth how to compose subsystems in QRF perspectives, building on a recent formalism for QRFs [E$.$Castro-Ruiz and O$.$Oreshkov, 2025]. We first show how the frames of external observers can be internalised and treated within the framework. This establishes a consistent hierarchy of QRF perspectives, which we use to define the adding and removing of subsystems. We then explain how, owing to the so-called 'extra-particle' degrees of freedom, the formalism gives a consistent treatment of subsystems, avoiding any paradoxes by construction. Consistency then implies that composing subsystems in a QRF perspective differs from, and generalises, the standard quantum-theoretic case. In particular, not every state can be appended in tensor-product form relative to a QRF, and we characterise the set of compatible states. Finally, we introduce 'classicalisation', a procedure that recovers a classical-like perspective from a general QRF. This procedure circumvents the aforementioned state restrictions but carries a different operational meaning, which we study through a concrete example.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the quantum phase transitions in an interacting cluster Ising chain, demonstrating how a weak interaction causes a critical bifurcation from an initial high-symmetry critical point. The study successfully maps out a deconfined quantum critical line characterized by emergent O(2) symmetry, using a combination of tensor-network methods and weak-coupling field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed mapping of quantum phase transitions, especially the realization of DQC and the connection to specific universality classes (like the eight-vertex model), provides deep insights into strongly correlated quantum matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the critical bifurcation and the nature of deconfined quantum criticality (DQC) in an interacting cluster Ising chain.

**Main result.** The interaction splits the non-interacting critical point into two distinct critical lines (Ising and Gaussian), leading to a DQC line with emergent O(2) symmetry, which is captured by weak-coupling field theory.

**Method.** The study employs tensor-network methods and weak-coupling field theory, supplemented by numerical simulations (MPS) to analyze the phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is the cluster Ising chain (CIM) augmented with a nearest-neighbor interaction. The non-interacting limit exhibits an O(3) symmetry and a critical point at c=3/2.

**Key observables.** Bipartite entanglement entropy (S), string order parameter (OS), and magnetizations in x, y, and z directions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction strength ($V_{	ext{nn}}$) and the coupling constants ($\lambda$) are key parameters controlling the phase transitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The weak-coupling theory is assumed valid in the analyzed regimes, though simulations reveal first-order transitions beyond its scope.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the critical bifurcation, showing the splitting of the initial critical point into Ising (c=1/2) and Gaussian (c=1) lines, and mapping out the subsequent DQC line and floating phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the non-interacting limit (O(3) symmetry, c=3/2), then introduces the interaction which causes a critical bifurcation into two separate critical lines. It then analyzes the DQC line connecting two ferromagnets and the subsequent floating phase, concluding with the failure of the theory at strong coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the cluster Ising chain with an additional nearest-neighbor interaction using tensor-network methods and a weak-coupling field theory. Without the interaction, the Jordan-Wigner transformation decomposes the model into a triplet of identical Majorana chains related by an exact $O(3)$ flavor symmetry. Their common mass vanishes at the $SU(2)_2$ Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten critical point with central charge $c = 3/2$ separating the symmetry-protected topological cluster phase from a ferromagnet. The interaction reduces $O(3)$ to its cyclic subgroup $C_3$, splitting the triplet into a singlet and a doublet whose gaps close separately, producing a critical bifurcation into Ising ($c = 1/2$) and Gaussian ($c = 1$) critical lines. A second ferromagnetic phase opens between these critical lines for repulsive interactions and a disordered phase for attractive ones. The Gaussian line then separates two Landau-incompatible ferromagnets, realizing a deconfined quantum critical line with emergent $O(2)$ symmetry, along which our independently extracted exponents vary continuously yet satisfy the parameter-free relation $β= (2ν- 1)/4$ of the eight-vertex weak universality class. At stronger repulsion this line opens into an extended gapless floating phase with incommensurate algebraic correlations, entered through Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transitions. All of these phases and the transitions between them are captured by the weak-coupling theory. Beyond its regime of validity, our simulations reveal a translation-symmetry-breaking antiferromagnet reached through first-order transitions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work resolves long-standing debates about the magnetic structure of alpha-Cu2P2O7 by combining inelastic neutron scattering, magnetization measurements, and spin-wave theory. It conclusively models the material as a 2D spin-dimerized antiferromagnet, pinpointing the dominant magnetic exchange to a specific, longer-range Cu-Cu interaction. The findings provide a detailed magnetic phase diagram and elucidate the roles of weak interlayer coupling and anisotropy.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of magnetic excitations and the identification of specific, long-range exchange pathways (7th nearest neighbors) in a transition metal phosphate provides a concrete case study for understanding magnetic interactions in strongly correlated insulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The microscopic magnetic model of the low-dimensional quantum magnet alpha-Cu2P2O7 has been controversial, requiring clarification of the dominant magnetic exchange pathways.

**Main result.** The study establishes the material as a two-dimensionally coupled spin-dimerized antiferromagnetic lattice, identifying the dominant exchange interaction as occurring between seventh-nearest-neighbor Cu-Cu pairs, not nearest neighbors.

**Method.** The research combines comprehensive experimental measurements (inelastic neutron scattering, neutron diffraction, magnetization) with advanced computational modeling (spin-wave theory, DFT).

**Model / system.** The system is alpha-Cu2P2O7, modeled as a 2D coupled spin-dimerized antiferromagnetic lattice. The Hamiltonian incorporates intradimer exchange (J2), interdimer exchanges (J1, J3, J4), weak interlayer coupling (J5), and single-ion anisotropy (D).

**Key observables.** Energy gaps in the spin-excitation spectrum, magnetic ordering temperature (TN), metamagnetic transitions in the H-T plane, and the magnitude of various exchange couplings (J2, J5, D).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dominant intradimer exchange J2 = 7.73 meV; weak interlayer coupling J5 = 0.03 meV; anisotropy D; critical magnetic field near 13 kOe.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting experimental data to a spin-wave model derived from a 2D coupled dimer Hamiltonian, contrasting with previous GGA+U predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature-dependent INS spectra, field-dependent neutron diffraction patterns, and simulated spin-wave spectra comparing experimental observations to theoretical models based on fitted exchange parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first presenting the experimental evidence (INS, diffraction, magnetization) to constrain the magnetic model, followed by detailed spin-wave calculations that fit the observed energy gaps and magnetic phase boundaries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The microscopic magnetic model of the low-dimensional quantum magnet alpha-Cu2P2O7 has remained controversial. We present a comprehensive study of its magnetic ground state and excitation spectrum using temperature-dependent inelastic neutron scattering, neutron diffraction, magnetization measurements, and comprehensive spin-wave modeling. Our results unambiguously establish alpha-Cu2P2O7 as a two-dimensionally coupled spin-dimerized antiferromagnetic (AF) lattice within the bc plane, with a dominant AF exchange J2 = 7.73 meV (hereafter referred to as "intradimer exchange") and weaker exchange couplings J1, J3, and J4 in the two-dimensional lattice (hereafter referred to as "interdimer exchange"), in agreement with LDA-based density functional theory and in contrast to previous GGA+U predictions. The dominant intradimer AF exchange is found between seventh-nearest-neighbor Cu-Cu ion pairs [d(Cu-Cu) = 5.125(3) A] rather than nearest-neighbor Cu-Cu ion pairs [d(Cu-Cu) = 3.014(1) A] of the structural dimers. Weak interlayer coupling (J5 = 0.03 meV) stabilizes long-range antiferromagnetic order below TN = 25 K. We further identify a weak single-ion anisotropy, associated with the distorted CuO5 polyhedra, that opens a gap in the spin-excitation spectrum and drives a field-induced metamagnetic transition. Systematic spin-wave calculations elucidate the distinct roles of interlayer coupling J5 and anisotropy term D in producing two distinct energy gaps at different antiferromagnetic zone centers. Complementary neutron diffraction and magnetization measurements as a function of applied magnetic field uncover a previously overlooked metamagnetic transition near 13 kOe and allow construction of the magnetic phase diagram in the H-T plane.

</details>
