**Summary.** This paper presents QisMC, a novel model checker designed to formally verify quantum programs written in Qiskit. It achieves this by modeling the program as a quantum-classical transition system and employing a specialized quantum computation tree logic (qCTL). QisMC offers automated, exhaustive verification and generates concrete counterexamples when bugs are found, significantly advancing quantum software debugging tools.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on software verification, the rigorous formalization of quantum control flow and state evolution using temporal logic is highly relevant for understanding the theoretical limits and guarantees of quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of formally debugging complex quantum programs written in Qiskit, particularly those involving dynamic quantum-classical interactions like measurements and control flow.

**Main result.** It introduces QisMC, the first dedicated quantum model checker, which provides an automated, exhaustive verification framework for quantum programs.

**Method.** The authors develop a quantum-classical transition system and a quantum computation tree logic (qCTL) to model program behavior, reducing the problem to classical CTL model checking solvable via decision diagrams.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum program executed on a quantum computer, modeled as a quantum-classical transition system. The state space involves quantum states (density matrices) and classical control flow variables.

**Key observables.** Safety, liveness, fairness, and invariance properties, diagnosed by generating a concrete counterexample execution path.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales with the size of the quantum circuit and the depth of the control flow structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the ability to model quantum operations and classical control flow constructs within the Qiskit API structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a buggy Repeat-Until-Success (RUS) program, its Qiskit circuit, and the corresponding transition system. Figure 2 shows the overall QisMC workflow, detailing the qCTL logic and reduction to CTL.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical foundation by defining the quantum-classical transition system and qCTL, details the end-to-end QisMC framework (generation, logic, checking), proves the correctness of the model checking algorithm, and concludes with practical examples and benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present QisMC, the first quantum model checker dedicated to debugging Qiskit programs. On the theoretical side, we introduce the notion of quantum-classical transition system and a quantum computation tree logic (qCTL) grounded in Birkhoff-von Neumann logic for modeling the behaviors and specifying the properties of Qiskit programs, respectively. On the implementation side, QisMC provides an end-to-end framework encompassing transition system generation, logical formula representation, and model checking algorithms, and it efficiently performs image computation based on decision diagrams. These methods and design principles give QisMC advantages over previous Qiskit program debuggers, including a unified property specification language, a fully automated and exhaustive verification process, and the ability to generate counterexamples. Extensive illustrative examples and benchmark evaluations demonstrate the practicality, efficiency, and scalability of QisMC in verifying realistic quantum programs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced range-based criteria to solve the problem of quantum state certification, determining when a mixed quantum state is uniquely specified by its local marginals. Using techniques like range-restricted SDP and analyzing state rank, the authors establish sharp bounds and necessary/sufficient conditions for $k$-UDA in multi-qubit systems. This has direct implications for certifying genuine multipartite entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical criteria for state reconstruction from local measurements, which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum tomography and certifying entanglement in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental problem of determining whether a mixed quantum state is uniquely determined by its local $k$-body marginals ($k$-UDA) in quantum system certification.

**Main result.** The authors derive necessary and sufficient range criteria for $k$-UDA states, establishing sharp rank bounds (e.g., no three-qubit state of rank $\ge 5$ is 2-UDA) and characterizing when $(n-1)$-UDA holds for specific families of states.

**Method.** The methodology involves developing a range-based approach by analyzing the structure of the global state's range, utilizing advanced tools like range-restricted semidefinite programming (SDP) and concepts from projective geometry.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on general mixed quantum states on multipartite systems, specifically analyzing three-qubit states and extending results to arbitrary finite-dimensional tripartite and $n$-qubit systems.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the $k$-body marginals ($M_k(ho)$), the state's rank, and the certification status ($k$-UDA).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the number of subsystems ($n$), the order of marginals ($k$), and the rank of the quantum state $ho$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon structural tools like characterizing GHZ-SLOCC-free ranges and relies on extending criteria from specific low-rank cases to general multipartite systems.

**Figures summary.** A summary table (Table I) is mentioned, which compiles the derived 2-UDA criteria for three-qubit states, high-rank obstructions for multipartite systems, and results for GME certification.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general problem, then deriving specific criteria for three-qubit states (including rank-two analysis), extending these range criteria to arbitrary tripartite states, and finally applying the results to characterize $(n-1)$-UDA for channel-based families and certify genuine multipartite entanglement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Determining whether a mixed quantum state is uniquely determined among all states by its k-body marginals (k-UDA) is a fundamental problem in quantum system certification. We develop a range-based approach to this problem by analyzing the structure of the range of the global state. For three-qubit states, we show that states with GHZ-SLOCC-free ranges are 2-UDA at ranks one, three, and four. We derive a necessary and sufficient range criterion for rank-two 2-UDA states and reduce it to a finite quadratic-form test. To cover the remaining range configurations, we formulate an exact range-restricted semidefinite programming criterion and extend it to arbitrary finite-dimensional tripartite states. We also show that every three-qubit state of rank at least five is not 2-UDA, and further extend high-rank obstructions to multipartite systems. For a channel-based multipartite family, we characterize exactly when a state is $(n-1)$-UDA and show that lower-order marginals never suffice. Finally, we apply these results to the certification of genuine multipartite entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops optimal quantum estimation protocols for quantifying the state frame potential, a measure of how close a quantum state ensemble is to perfect randomness. It achieves state-of-the-art resource bounds—including a quadratic improvement in query complexity—across three distinct physical access models. The results are highly relevant for characterizing the randomness of quantum states in various physical settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous, optimized resource bounds for quantifying quantum randomness, which is a fundamental concept in quantum information theory and has implications for quantum cryptography and state verification.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the problem of estimating the state frame potential, $F_t(E)$, of a quantum state ensemble to a specified additive error $\epsilon$. This potential serves as a diagnostic for how close the ensemble is to approximating Haar randomness.

**Main result.** The authors establish near-optimal resource bounds for three different access models: $\Theta(\sqrt{t}/\epsilon)$ queries for the query model, $\Theta(t/\epsilon^2)$ samples for the general sample model, and a complexity dependent on the Rényi entropy for the single-copy model.

**Method.** The methodology involves establishing both upper and lower bounds for the estimation task across different quantum access models (query, general sample, single-copy). Techniques include composing block-encoding primitives and utilizing quantum singular value transformations.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on quantum state ensembles $E = \{(\mu_i, |\psi_iangle)\}$, particularly those derived from projected state ensembles, which are relevant for assessing randomness in subsystems.

**Key observables.** State frame potential $F_t(E)$, additive error $\epsilon$, and the Rényi-$\alpha$ entropy $H_\alpha(\mu)$ of the ensemble weights.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the potential $t$, the desired accuracy $\epsilon$, and the ensemble structure defined by the weights $\mu_i$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of specific oracles (e.g., multi-state-preparation oracle) for complexity bounding, and the single-copy model requires prior knowledge of the Rényi entropy.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the notes refer to Table 1 which compares resource bounds across the three access models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the estimation problem under three distinct resource constraints: query access, general sample access, and single-copy sample access, deriving complexity bounds for each.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The state frame potential is a standard diagnostic of how closely a quantum state ensemble approximates Haar randomness. In this work, we study the problem of estimating the state frame potential of order $t$ to within additive error $\varepsilon$ under three progressively weaker access models: (i) query access to a multi-state-preparation oracle, (ii) general sample access, and (iii) single-copy sample access. In the query model, we establish a near-optimal query complexity of $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{t}/\varepsilon)$, yielding a quadratic improvement in the dependence on $t$ over the previous best result of Nakata, Takeuchi, Kliesch, and Darmawan (PRX Quantum 2025). In the general sample model, we establish the optimal sample complexity $Θ(t/\varepsilon^2)$. In the single-copy sample model, we present a store-and-estimate approach whose sample complexity depends on the Rényi entropy of the ensemble weights. As an application, we use the single-copy algorithm to assess the randomness of projected state ensembles, where the entropy term becomes the observational Rényi entropy associated with measuring one subsystem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'weak irreducibility' as a spectral criterion to differentiate true thermodynamic phase coexistence from pseudo-transitions in finite systems. By analyzing the transfer operator's spectrum, the authors show that genuine coexistence requires the inter-sector connectivity to vanish in the thermodynamic limit. This provides a deep spectral understanding of how finite-size effects round off phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The spectral analysis provides a powerful, non-perturbative tool to characterize the nature of phase transitions, linking spectral properties directly to thermodynamic phase boundaries, which is relevant for understanding quantum critical points and phase separation in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a rigorous, operator-level criterion to distinguish genuine thermodynamic phase coexistence from mere avoided crossings (pseudo-transitions) in finite-size systems.

**Main result.** Genuine phase coexistence emerges as the singular limit where inter-sector connectivity vanishes, leading to a reducible thermodynamic manifold, while pseudo-transitions retain finite connectivity.

**Method.** The analysis uses the transfer-matrix formalism, decomposing the operator into sector imbalance and inter-sector connectivity components to analyze the spectral structure.

**Model / system.** The study applies to statistical mechanics systems undergoing first-order phase transitions, analyzed using restricted transfer operators for finite transverse width (L).

**Key observables.** Sector imbalance, spectral gap (connectivity), and the weak irreducibility of the transfer operator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite size (L), temperature (T), and the interface tension ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes short-range interactions and relies on the Peierls estimate to show exponential suppression of connectivity in $d \ge 2$ systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the effective two-sector transfer-operator reduction, introducing imbalance and connectivity coordinates, and then applies this framework to distinguish between pseudo-transitions (finite connectivity) and true coexistence (vanishing connectivity).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional finite-size theories describe first-order coexistence through free-energy competition and interfacial tunneling, but the distinct spectral roles of sector balance and inter-sector connectivity are not usually explicit. We show that pseudo-transitions and thermodynamic phase coexistence are governed by two spectral coordinates: sector imbalance locates balance, while connectivity determines whether that balance remains avoided. At balance, finite connectivity produces a unique dominant state with equal spectral weights in the symmetrized two-sector representation. For short-range systems with positive interface tension, interfacial costs suppress the connectivity exponentially, close the coexistence splitting, and asymptotically restore reducibility. Thus, genuine phase coexistence emerges as the singular limit of finite-size avoided coexistence, whereas one-dimensional pseudo-transitions retain finite connectivity. In a decorated bilayer Ising model, the coexistence gap at independently est

</details>

### [Classification of Non-Hermitian Flat Bands](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23769v1)

**Authors:** Chang-geun Oh, Jun-Won Rhim  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.23769v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.23769_figures/2608.23769_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Projector-regularity classification of Hermitian and non-Hermitian exact flat bands. (a) Hermitian flat bands are classified by the regularity of the flat-band projector PFB(k): analytic (H-A), continuous but nonanalytic (H-C), and discontinuous (H-D). In non-Hermitian systems, the corresponding classification is governed by the biorthogonal projector Pbi(k), yielding NH-A, NH-C, NH-D, and NH-E. (b) Relation to spectral isolation. A gapped flat band is necessarily analytic (H-A or NH-A), whereas gapless flat bands can realize all Hermitian classes or the NH-A–NH-E hierarchy, depending on the regularity of the corresponding projector.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2608.23769_figures/2608.23769_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Chern-mismatch-induced enhancement of driven in- terorbital transfer. (a)–(c) Band structures of the NH-A, NH-C, and NH-EOD control models (see SM for details). (d)–(f) Magni- tude of the off-diagonal biorthogonal flat-band projector element, |(Pbi)AB(k)|. CL, CR and Cbi indicate left-eigenstate Chern num- ber, right-eigenstate Chern number and biorthogonal Chern num- ber, respectively. (g)–(i) Cross-orbital transfer intensity TB→A(k) at η = 0.1. (j) The maximum transfer intensity T max as a function of damping rate η and the fitted exponents.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive, projector-based classification system for non-Hermitian flat bands, distinguishing four classes based on the mathematical regularity of the system's biorthogonal projector. The core finding is that the breakdown of projector continuity leads to topological invariants mismatch ($C_R 
eq C_L$), which is directly correlated with an anomalous enhancement in resonant cross-orbital energy transfer.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous mathematical framework linking topological invariants (Chern numbers) to measurable transport phenomena (cross-orbital transfer) in non-Hermitian systems, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and engineered materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a projector-based classification scheme for non-Hermitian (NH) flat bands, extending known concepts from Hermitian systems.

**Main result.** The classification identifies four classes (NH-A, NH-C, NH-D, NH-E) based on the regularity of the biorthogonal projector, showing that topological mismatch ($C_R 
eq C_L$) in the NH-E class leads to anomalous resonant cross-orbital transfer.

**Method.** The methodology involves analyzing the mathematical regularity (analyticity, continuity, singularity) of the biorthogonal projector $P_{bi}(k)$ across the Brillouin zone and relating this regularity to topological invariants (Chern numbers) and transport properties.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on electronic systems exhibiting flat bands described by non-Hermitian Hamiltonians ($H 
eq H^\dagger$). The analysis compares the behavior of these bands across different mathematical regimes defined by the projector's properties.

**Key observables.** Right Chern number ($C_R$), Left Chern number ($C_L$), Biorthogonal Chern number ($C_{bi}$), and the cross-orbital transfer intensity $T_{B	o A}(k)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The classification depends critically on the regularity of the biorthogonal projector $P_{bi}(k)$ at the band touching points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily restricted to rank-one flat bands, though generalizations to higher-rank manifolds are mentioned as future work.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between Hermitian and non-Hermitian classifications, showing band structures, projector magnitudes, and the scaling of maximum cross-orbital transfer intensity ($T_{	ext{max}}$) versus damping rate ($\eta$) for different classes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the need for a projector-based classification for NH flat bands. It then details the four classes (NH-A to NH-E) based on projector regularity, linking each class to specific topological invariants and measurable physical responses like cross-orbital transfer scaling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exact flat bands provide a versatile setting for correlated and topological phenomena, yet their properties are controlled not only by their dispersion but also by the structure of their Bloch projectors. Here, we establish a projector-based classification of non-Hermitian flat bands. In contrast to Hermitian flat bands, non-Hermiticity introduces a biorthogonal projector whose left-right pairing permits a distinct norm-pole singularity. We identify four classes: analytic NH-A, continuous but nonanalytic NH-C, bounded but discontinuous NH-D, and pole-singular NH-E. We show that continuity of the biorthogonal projector preserves the locking of the right, left, and biorthogonal Chern numbers, $C_R=C_L=C_{\rm bi}$, thereby realizing non-Hermitian critical topological flat bands. Once continuity is lost, this locking can break down; in particular, a mismatch $C_R\neq C_L$ can occur in the NH-E class. Finally, we show that this Chern number mismatch ensures to produce an anomalous enhancement of resonant cross-orbital transfer. Our results establish biorthogonal projector regularity as the organizing principle linking compact localized states, topology, and driven response in non-Hermitian flat bands.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically maps the complex phase diagram of a fluid confined between patterned walls subjected to shear. The competition between capillary condensation and interface delocalization forces the formation of novel 'bridging phases.' The research uses generalized Kelvin equations and DFT to predict multiple types of phase transitions, providing detailed insights into how geometry and external fields dictate fluid behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of confined fluids and wetting transitions, especially when coupled with external fields like shear, provides a rich playground for testing theories of phase transitions and interfacial physics, which share conceptual links with many-body systems in external potentials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the equilibrium phase behavior of a fluid confined within a slit formed by two patterned walls, focusing on the complex transitions induced by external shear.

**Main result.** The competition between capillary condensation and interface delocalization leads to an unusually rich phase diagram featuring multiple bridging phases (Type I, IIA, IIB, III) and both first-order and continuous phase transitions.

**Method.** The analysis employs generalized Kelvin equations to determine phase boundaries, supplemented by a microscopic density functional model (DFT) to characterize the bridging phases.

**Model / system.** The system is a fluid confined in a slit with patterned walls exhibiting competing wetting properties, subjected to a lateral shear displacement. The theoretical framework utilizes the grand potential ($\Omega$) calculated from surface tensions.

**Key observables.** Phase identification (CG, CL, bridging phases), phase transition types (first-order/continuous), and the correlation length ($\xi_y$) within the bridging phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Slit width ($L$), shear displacement ($\delta$), and the wetting contrast ($	heta$). Specific regimes are defined by comparing $L$ and $\delta$ against critical widths like $L_T(\delta)$ and $L_D(\delta)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on macroscopic descriptions corrected by mesoscopic terms (e.g., logarithmic corrections to the triple line) and uses DFT approximations for the grand potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four types of bridging states based on pinning conditions, show surface phase diagrams detailing transitions between gas, liquid, and bridging phases, and compare theoretical predictions for the triple-point line $L_T(\delta)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps out the phase diagram by analyzing different slit width regimes (e.g., $L > L_T(\delta)$ vs. $L < L_D(\delta)$) under varying shear conditions, deriving specific equations for phase boundaries for each transition type.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the equilibrium phase behavior of a fluid confined in a slit made from two patterned walls. Shearing the walls frustrates the fluid, due to a competition between capillary condensation and interface delocalization, forcing the formation of bridging phases with different pinning properties. This leads to an unusually rich phase diagram, displaying first-order and continuous phase transitions, depending sensitively on the slit width and shear. Generalized Kelvin equations determine the phase boundaries, while the bridging phases are characterized by large correlation lengths, predictions for which are tested using a microscopic density functional model.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24116v1>

**Summary.** This work proposes a current-driven mechanism to create magnetic hopfions, which are topologically knotted spin textures, starting from initial magnetic torons. By simulating the dynamics using the LLG equation, the authors show that the annihilation of an emergent monopole-antimonopole pair, induced by an electric current, converts the toron into a hopfion. This establishes a controllable, current-mediated route for generating complex topological states in chiral magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study connects macroscopic current-driven dynamics (STT) to the creation of highly non-trivial topological excitations (Hopfions), providing a pathway to study knot theory in condensed matter systems, which is relevant to understanding complex magnetic memory or spintronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a controlled, deterministic mechanism for generating magnetic hopfions, which possess complex knotted topology, from simpler magnetic textures like torons.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that applying an electric current drives the annihilation of an emergent monopole-antimonopole pair within a toron, successfully converting it into one or more hopfions.

**Method.** The dynamics are simulated by solving the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (LLG) equation, incorporating spin-transfer torque (STT) driven by an external current.

**Model / system.** The system is a chiral magnet modeled by a Hamiltonian including exchange, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI), and Zeeman coupling. The dynamics are governed by the LLG equation under the influence of STT.

**Key observables.** Hopf number (H), which quantifies the knot topology; toron length (L); and the time evolution of the topological invariant.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial toron length (L) dictates the number of hopfions; the sign of the applied current controls the sign of the Hopf invariant; and the threshold current is sensitive to material parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on the competition between toron length shortening and radial expansion, and the initial state is assumed to be metastable.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hopfion topology and toron structure, show the time evolution of toron dynamics under current for varying initial lengths, and plot the time evolution of the Hopf number to distinguish between trivial annihilation and hopfion generation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using the LLG equation, simulates the evolution of torons under current, analyzes the dependence of the final topological state (Hopfion vs. trivial) on initial length and current strength, and concludes by mapping out an experimentally accessible creation pathway.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in three-dimensional magnetization imaging techniques have opened new avenues for exploring topological spin textures beyond domain walls and skyrmions. Among them, magnetic hopfions are particularly promising, as their knotted topology is expected to give rise to unconventional dynamics and responses; however, their controlled creation remains challenging. Here we propose a simple mechanism for generating hopfions from magnetic torons, three-dimensional textures hosting an emergent monopole-antimonopole pair. Using Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert simulations, we show that an electric current drives the annihilation of this pair, converting a toron into a hopfion. The initial toron length determines the number of generated hopfions, while the current direction selects the sign of the Hopf invariant. We further find that the threshold current depends sensitively on material parameters, indicating a close connection to skyrmion dynamics. Our results establish an experimentally accessible route to hopfion creation and reveal a pathway from monopole defects to knotted topological textures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the magnetocaloric effect in the quasi-1D antiferromagnet BaCo2V2O8 by combining angle-resolved measurements with advanced tensor-network simulations. The research distinguishes between the field associated with the Ising critical point and a higher-field transverse response. It concludes that the MCE can be engineered by tailoring anisotropic Zeeman couplings, opening a route for magnetic cooling in quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed study of magnetic phase transitions and thermodynamic responses in a highly anisotropic quantum magnet, which is relevant for understanding magnetic field control over quantum ground states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine whether the anomalous magnetocaloric effect (MCE) in the quasi-1D antiferromagnet BaCo2V2O8 is governed by the intrinsic Ising critical field or by transverse spin fluctuations when the applied magnetic field is rotated.

**Main result.** The dominant MCE response persists away from the intrinsic Ising critical region, suggesting that magnetic cooling can be achieved by engineering anisotropic Zeeman-coupling configurations in quantum magnets.

**Method.** The study combines angle-resolved MCE measurements with tensor-network calculations (DMRG) on an effective XXZ spin chain model.

**Model / system.** The system is the quasi-one-dimensional Ising-like antiferromagnet BaCo2V2O8, modeled by an effective spin-1/2 XXZ chain Hamiltonian incorporating site-dependent Zeeman couplings due to the material's geometry and anisotropic g-tensor.

**Key observables.** Magnetocaloric effect (MCE), magnetic Grüneisen ratio ($\Gamma_H$), Ising critical field ($H_{	ext{thr1}}$), and high-field temperature minimum ($H_{	ext{mc2}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied magnetic field up to 45 T, temperature range around 1.6 K, and field rotation angles ($\phi_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system can be effectively described by an XXZ chain model, and DMRG methods are used to calculate ground state and finite-temperature properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure and geometry for different field directions, showing experimental MCE dips and angle-dependent magnetization/susceptibility data.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model and experimental measurements, then using theoretical calculations (DMRG) to analyze the field dependence of various magnetic order parameters and thermodynamic quantities, culminating in a comparison between the theoretical and experimental findings regarding the MCE source.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the transverse-field thermodynamics of the quasi-one-dimensional Ising-like antiferromagnet BaCo$_2$V$_2$O$_8$, whose tilted screw-chain geometry and anisotropic Landé $g$ tensor generate spatially modulated Zeeman couplings. Angle-resolved magnetocaloric-effect (MCE) measurements reveal a high-field temperature minimum near the transverse-field Ising critical field for $H\parallel[110]$ that persists and shifts only weakly upon field rotation. Tensor-network calculations show that the rotation-induced staggered transverse field rapidly lowers the Ising critical field and that the magnetic Grüneisen ratio changes sign near the high-field temperature minimum, consistent with experiment. Our results establish that a dominant MCE response can persist away from the Ising critical region, suggesting a route to magnetic cooling by tailoring anisotropic Zeeman-coupling configurations in quantum magnets.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an exact autoregressive sampling algorithm for planar Ising spin glasses by creatively adapting the Kac--Ward theory. It overcomes the mathematical hurdles posed by boundary fields in conditional probability calculations using an auxiliary spin construction. The resulting method generates perfectly uncorrelated samples, offering a crucial, exact benchmark for validating approximate machine learning samplers in disordered systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the development of an exact, non-Markovian sampling technique that provides a rigorous benchmark for neural samplers is highly relevant to developing robust generative models for complex physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving exact sampling from the Boltzmann distribution of spin glasses, as standard MCMC methods suffer from critical slowing down, and modern neural samplers are only approximate.

**Main result.** The authors developed an exact autoregressive sampling algorithm that generates strictly i.i.d. samples with exact normalized likelihoods, providing a rigorous baseline for benchmarking machine learning samplers.

**Method.** The method adapts the Kac--Ward theory by introducing a planarity-preserving auxiliary spin construction to map the conditional partition functions, which are otherwise corrupted by boundary fields, into an extended zero-field Ising model solvable by a determinant formula.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the planar Ising spin glass, governed by the Hamiltonian E(s) = -sum<i,j> J_ij s_i s_j. The analysis is applied to specific cases like the triangular AFM Ising model.

**Key observables.** Autocorrelation function (expected to be delta-correlated, $\delta_{t,0}$), Entropy (S), Magnetization (|m|), and Specific Heat (c_v).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), system size (N), and the coupling constants J_ij.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies critically on the planarity of the underlying graph structure and the successful construction of the auxiliary spin to maintain solvability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sampling process using auxiliary spins, compare autocorrelation functions (Metropolis vs. exact sampler), and show temperature dependence of observables like entropy and specific heat.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the difficulty of exact sampling, introduces the Kac--Ward theory framework, details the novel auxiliary spin construction to handle boundary fields, derives the conditional probabilities via determinant ratios, and finally validates the method by calculating observables like entropy and comparing autocorrelation functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exact sampling from the Boltzmann distribution of spin glasses remains an outstanding challenge: Markov chain Monte Carlo methods suffer from critical slowing down and metastable trapping, while modern neural autoregressive samplers such as variational autoregressive networks are approximate and, in the absence of exact reference samples, cannot be rigorously benchmarked. Here we present an exact autoregressive sampling algorithm for planar Ising spin glasses based on the Kac--Ward theory. Under the chain-rule factorization, sequentially fixing spins induces boundary-localized external fields, which destroy the zero-field structure required for exact evaluation. By encoding these fields with a planarity-preserving auxiliary spin construction, the conditional partition functions are mapped to an extended zero-field Ising model and exactly evaluated using the Kac--Ward determinant formula. The method generates strictly independent and identically distributed samples with exact normalized likelihoods at a computational cost of $\mathcal{O}(N^{5/2})$ for $N$ spins, thereby providing an exact baseline for benchmarking neural autoregressive samplers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents FaSHIoNPOp, a novel algorithm for designing point patterns on the sphere by enforcing desired spectral properties using spherical harmonics. It extends powerful spectral optimization techniques from flat space to curved manifolds, enabling applications in photonics and optimal sampling. The method achieves high efficiency ($O(N \log N)$) while allowing control over complex physical properties like hyperuniformity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of spectral optimization techniques for curved spaces provides a powerful, general tool for designing structured matter or optimal sampling configurations, which has analogues in designing quantum metamaterials or optimizing quantum state preparation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing disordered point patterns with specific spectral properties on curved manifolds, specifically the sphere.

**Main result.** They introduce FaSHIoNPOp, an algorithm that successfully extends spectral optimization methods from flat Euclidean space to the sphere with $O(N \log N)$ complexity.

**Method.** The method uses an optimization framework based on enforcing target power spectra ($S_0(\ell)$) by minimizing a loss function derived from the Spherical Harmonics Transform (SHT).

**Model / system.** The system is point patterns distributed on the unit 2-sphere ($S \subset \mathbb{R}^3$). Applications include designing photonic coatings and sampling on curved manifolds.

**Key observables.** Power Spectrum ($S_\ell$), Radial Distribution Function ($g(	heta)$), Constraint Count ($\chi$), and Reduced Variance ($s^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of points ($N$), maximum constrained mode ($\ell_{	ext{max}}$), and the target spectral coefficients ($S_0(\ell)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimization relies on fast, non-uniform spherical harmonics transforms (NUSHTs) and assumes small steps during gradient-based updates, projecting positions back onto the sphere.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the algorithm flow (Initialization $	o$ SHT $	o$ Gradient $	o$ Update), and show results like the generation of hyperuniform structures ($S_\ell=0$ for $\ell \le \ell_{	ext{max}}$) and the scaling of reduced variance ($s^2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theory by first establishing the spectral optimization framework on the sphere, deriving the necessary gradient calculations for the loss function, and then applying this machinery to generate and characterize specific disordered states like hyperuniform structures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The design of disordered point patterns with desirable properties is an exciting and ongoing research endeavor, with applications ranging from materials to computer science. A successful approach in recent years has been the optimization of point patterns through a loss function that enforces properties in their Fourier-space representation. Yet, these methods have so far strictly been limited to flat Euclidean space, precluding their use in the contexts of coatings of curved surfaces for photonics, or of sampling of curved manifolds for instance. We introduce FaSHIoNPOp, an algorithm that relies on fast non-uniform spherical harmonics transforms, to enforce pair correlations in point patterns on the sphere with an $O(N \log N)$ complexity in $N$ the number of points. Having demonstrated its performance, we showcase applications of FaSHIoNPOp, ranging from the generation of hyperuniform structures on the sphere for sampling and physical applications, to the design of gyromorphs (disordered structures with maximal scattering power at a given frequency) on the sphere. We additionally show that FaSHIoNPOp can be combined to both global constraints like centrosymmetry, with applications to the design of more isotropic $3d$ gyromorphs, and local real-space constraints like pair repulsion. Our work paves the way for optimal sampling and coating design on curved manifolds, with many applications across physics, materials and computer science.

</details>

**Authors:** Stacey Jeffery, Freek Witteveen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24494v1>

Sup- pressing the probability of a bad estimate below the signal level γ leads to logarithmic overhead. (b) In a QSVT-based approach, one approximates an ideal step function by a polynomial. **Summary.** This paper develops an optimal quantum algorithm for estimating the ground-state energy of a system, which is mathematically equivalent to finding the largest eigenphase of the system's time-evolution unitary. By utilizing quantum transducers, the authors eliminate a previously unavoidable logarithmic overhead factor related to the guiding state overlap ($\gamma$), achieving a query complexity that matches theoretical lower bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant algorithmic improvement for a fundamental problem in quantum simulation, directly impacting the feasibility of quantum chemistry and condensed matter simulations on near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantum algorithm for estimating the ground-state energy of a Hamiltonian, which is equivalent to estimating the largest eigenphase of the time-evolution unitary operator $U$ in a guided setting.

**Main result.** The authors present a quantum algorithm that achieves a query complexity for the guided ground-state energy estimation that matches the established lower bound, improving upon previous methods by eliminating a $\log(1/\gamma)$ overhead.

**Method.** The core method involves designing and composing exact quantum transducers for the decision versions of phase estimation and amplitude amplification, allowing for efficient composition of subroutines.

**Model / system.** The problem is framed using a general Hamiltonian $H$ and its time-evolution unitary $U = e^{-iH}$. The estimation is guided by an initial state $|\psiangle$ that has a guaranteed overlap $\gamma$ with the ground state space.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_0$), largest eigenphase ($x_0$), and the unitary operator $U$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Overlap parameter $\gamma$, desired precision $\delta$, and the error parameter $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the efficient composition properties of transducers and the ability to solve the problem via binary search on the threshold eigenphase problem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the source of previous $\log(1/\gamma)$ overheads in QPE and QSVT. Figure 8 shows the interval update process during one round of the search algorithm.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the algorithm by first solving the decision version (threshold problem) using transducers, which is then embedded into a binary search framework to achieve the final complexity bounds for the max eigenphase estimation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the problem of ground-state energy estimation, one aims to estimate the smallest eigenvalue of a Hamiltonian, often given a guiding state, with some promised overlap $γ$ with the ground space. The main approach to this problem is to simulate its evolution, and estimate the smallest (or equivalently, largest) eigenphase of the resulting unitary $U$. We give a quantum algorithm that estimates the largest eigenphase of a unitary $U$ in this guided setting using a factor of $\log\frac{1}γ$ fewer queries to $U$ than the previous best approach. The result matches an existing lower bound, and answers an open question from Mande and de Wolf. The algorithm is based on transducers, which often allow composition of quantum algorithms without overhead from error reduction.

</details>

### [Accidental accuracy and vertex corrections in $GW$: Exact benchmarks for the extended Hubbard model](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24838v1)

**Authors:** Michael O. Atambo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24838v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

</details>

<img src="2608.24838_figures/2608.24838_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

<img src="2608.24838_figures/2608.24838_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously benchmarks the GW approximation against exact diagonalization for the extended Hubbard model. It finds that the approximation fails to correctly predict the Mott gap, quantifying the necessary vertex correction ($\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$) which varies significantly across the phase diagram. The results establish quantitative criteria for assessing the reliability of GW in strongly correlated electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of correlation effects, gap opening, and the failure of simple approximations like GW provides crucial theoretical diagnostics for understanding strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to quantify the reliability and error of the GW approximation for quasiparticle predictions in correlated materials, specifically addressing the role of vertex corrections.

**Main result.** The effective vertex correction ($\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$) changes character across the phase diagram: it is $<1$ in the weak-coupling regime but grows large in the Mott regime, indicating that standard GW fails to capture the full gap opening.

**Method.** The authors benchmark GW theory against numerically exact results obtained via exact diagonalization (ED) of the extended Hubbard model on finite rings.

**Model / system.** The study uses the half-filled extended Hubbard model, described by a Hamiltonian including on-site ($U$) and nearest-neighbor ($V$) interactions, studied on finite rings.

**Key observables.** Effective vertex ($\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$), quasiparticle gap ($\Delta$), and the comparison between ED and GW calculated gaps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($U, V$), finite size ($N=6, 10$), and the crossover point $U^*$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the static limit (COHSEX) for the self-energy and focuses on the spin-integrated density-channel functional.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the exact ED gap versus the GW gap, show the dependence of $\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$ on $U$, and illustrate how non-local interactions split the accidental crossover point.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the failure of GW in different regimes, first establishing the ED benchmark, then diagnosing the failure via $\Gamma_{	ext{eff}}$, and finally showing how non-local interactions complicate the picture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The $GW$ approximation is the standard tool for quasiparticle predictions in materials, yet its regime of validity in correlated systems remains poorly quantified, because \textit{ab initio} vertex corrections are computationally prohibitive. Using exact diagonalization of the half-filled extended Hubbard model on finite rings as a numerically exact reference, we construct the corresponding model-space $GW$ theory on the identical Hilbert space and quantify its error as a function of local ($U$) and non-local ($V$) interaction strength. We find that the vertex correction changes character across the phase diagram: in the weak-coupling regime the effective vertex $Γ_{\rm eff} < 1$, reflecting the suppression of RPA charge fluctuations by exact short-range correlations, whereas in the Mott regime $Γ_{\rm eff}$ grows monotonically (to $\sim 3$ for $N=6$, reflecting the local vertex required to open the Hubbard gap. Vertex corrections in the electron-hole (polarizability) channel are shown to \emph{worsen} the gap error, indicating that the Mott gap resides in the self-energy channel. For $V=0$, static $COHSEX$ is accidentally exact at a single crossover point $U^* \approx 3.5\,t$; finite $V$, through non-local Fock exchange, splits this point into a double-crossover window that collapses toward weak coupling. These results yield quantitative diagnostics for the reliability of $GW$ in correlated materials.

</details>

**Authors:** John Yuehan Zhang

CircLS allocates the patch at its first use and frees it at its last use, and the freed span is open for ancilla path reuse. (c) and (d) Freeing shortens ancilla paths: patches 1 and 3 are jointly measured. With patch 2 still allocated, the ancilla path detours through five tiles. With patch 2 freed, it runs straight through three tiles.</sub>

Parallel execution runs ¯𝑍1 ¯𝑍5 and ¯𝑋3 ¯𝑋4 together. Mapping places the five patches on the grid, and routing draws every ancilla path: the ancilla path of ¯𝑍1 ¯𝑍5 runs through the tile freed by patch 2.</sub>

(b) The PPM ¯𝑋2 ¯𝑋4 needs an ancilla path between the ¯𝑋boundaries of patches 2 and 4, but both ¯𝑋boundaries of patch 2 touch allocated patches: no ancilla path exists. After rotating patch 2, ¯𝑋2 sits on the left and right boundaries, and the ancilla path connects on the right.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces CircLS, a compiler designed to translate abstract Lattice Surgery protocols into concrete, fault-tolerant physical quantum circuits. By implementing dynamic resource allocation—reusing physical tiles as they become free—CircLS drastically reduces the required spacetime volume and improves the predicted logical error rate compared to existing methods. This advancement is crucial for scaling quantum computation hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the resource optimization techniques (dynamic allocation, scheduling) are highly relevant to any physical implementation of quantum algorithms, including those using AMO platforms where qubit connectivity and coherence time are critical constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of compiling Lattice Surgery (LS) protocols, specifically the Pauli Product Measurement (PPM) sequence, from a high-level logical description down to a detailed, fault-tolerant physical quantum circuit.

**Main result.** The proposed CircLS compiler successfully completes this pipeline and significantly improves resource efficiency, reducing the allocated spacetime volume by $5.5	imes$ and the Logical Error Rate (LER) by $14	imes$ compared to prior methods.

**Method.** It employs a linear-time stabilizer construction rule set to lower the PPM sequence to a Stim circuit, coupled with a novel dynamic data patch allocation scheme that reuses freed physical tiles as ancilla paths.

**Model / system.** The work is situated in the context of fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC) using Quantum Error Correction (QEC). Logical operations are realized via Lattice Surgery, which relies on sequences of Pauli Product Measurements (PPMs) implemented on physical qubits.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), allocated spacetime volume, and qubit-cycles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), physical error rate ($p$), and the structure of the quantum program (e.g., CNOT, GHZ states).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that local stabilizer construction rules are sufficient to guarantee global fault tolerance, and that dynamic allocation accurately models resource reuse.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between static and dynamic resource allocation, the three stages of LS for measurement, and the required Pauli corrections for logical gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces CircLS to bridge the gap between logical PPM sequences and physical circuits. It details the dynamic allocation technique, shows how this improves resource usage, and validates the compiler's performance by measuring LER and volume reduction against prior toolchains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In fault-tolerant quantum computing, lattice surgery (LS) is one of the leading ways to realize logical operations, and the Pauli product measurement (PPM) is the basic instruction of LS-based computing. Compilers on the PPM sequence, however, stay at the logical level rather than the physical circuit level. This is because the lowering is complicated: PPMs differ widely from each other, and each must be realized on the physical circuit without breaking fault tolerance. CircLS lowers the PPM sequence to a Stim circuit through linear-time stabilizer construction rules. This completes the pipeline from a quantum program through the PPM sequence to a Stim circuit, on which the compiled program can be verified at the circuit level and its logical error rate (LER) measured. Based on the lowering, we develop a compiler that allocates data patches dynamically: each patch is allocated at its first use and freed at its last use, and the freed tiles are reused as ancilla paths. CircLS reduces the allocated spacetime volume by $5.5\times$ and the LER by $14\times$ against the prior toolchain producing runnable circuits. CircLS is open source at https://github.com/John-YuehanZhang/CircLS.

</details>

### [Dynamical Consequences of Nontrivial Topology of Molecular Conical Intersections](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24864v1)

**Authors:** Indranil Ghosh, Kush Banker, Gregory S. Engel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24864v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper explores how the underlying topology of molecular electronic structure, particularly at conical intersections, dictates chemical dynamics. By constructing two Hamiltonians with the same potential energy surfaces but different topological invariants, the authors demonstrate that the topology influences observable nonadiabatic hopping rates via surface hopping simulations. This work establishes topology as a fundamental determinant of molecular excited state relaxation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects abstract concepts from topological physics (Berry curvature, topological invariants) to concrete, measurable quantities in molecular dynamics (nonadiabatic coupling, reaction rates), providing a powerful diagnostic tool for understanding excited state dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the nontrivial topology of molecular conical intersections (CIs) influences nonadiabatic chemical dynamics, extending beyond the standard Berry phase description.

**Main result.** The authors show that a topological invariant, derived from the Berry curvature, can distinguish between two Hamiltonians ($H_{top}$ and $H_{triv}$) that share identical adiabatic potential energy surfaces, and this difference manifests in measurable, direction-dependent surface hopping rates.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical derivations using the Jahn-Teller model to calculate topological invariants and utilizes Fewest-Switches Surface Hopping (FSSH) simulations to compute nonadiabatic relaxation rates.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a two-level Jahn-Teller Hamiltonian, comparing a topologically non-trivial version ($H_{top}$) against a trivially gapped counterpart ($H_{triv}$), both exhibiting a CI.

**Key observables.** Relaxation rates ($R$) of population decay on the upper eigensurface, the topological invariant ($C$), and the Non-Adiabatic Coupling (NAC) vector components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength parameters ($F, \lambda$) and the approach direction ($\hat{Q}_x, \hat{Q}_y$) are crucial for observing the rate differences.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the adiabatic approximation for the FSSH simulation, and the topological invariant is derived from the Berry curvature integral.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three Hamiltonians (topological, CI, trivial) and their associated geometric structures. Figure 2 shows the surface hopping kinetics (population decay) for different approach directions and Hamiltonians.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by defining the topological invariant using the Berry curvature in the Jahn-Teller model. It then compares the resulting Hamiltonians ($H_{top}$ vs $H_{triv}$) and uses FSSH simulations to demonstrate that the topological difference leads to distinct, measurable nonadiabatic relaxation rates, particularly along certain nuclear coordinates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The topology of the electronic structure for an avoided crossing and a conical intersection (CI) is different and is characterized by the presence of the geometric phase in the electronic wavefunction in the latter. Using the linear Jahn-Teller model, we show that an avoided crossing can be created from the conical intersection while preserving the nontrivial topology of the latter by adding a Pauli $σ_y$ term to the Hamiltonian. Analogously to solid state systems, we derive a half-integer topological invariant as the integral of the Berry curvature over the CI nuclear branching space. We investigate the influence of electronic topology on chemical dynamics by conducting fewest-switches surface hopping simulations and find distinct hopping rates on identical eigensurfaces but with different topologies. Our work extends the influence of topology on molecular excited state dynamics beyond the Berry phase that can be practically realized through electron-nuclear and spin-orbit coupling.

</details>

**Authors:** Pradip Kattel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.23770v1>

The colored curves in ZRL are the images of constant-time slices Mti under the characteristic map ι. They are equivalently level sets of the spectral difference uLR(ζR, ζL) = ζL −ζR. **Summary.** This paper presents a general geometric construction for time-dependent integrable chiral field theories. It shows that the time evolution of the system's interaction is not arbitrary but is rigidly fixed by the initial autonomous scattering data and the constraints of chiral kinematics and spatial homogeneity. This provides a powerful, unified method for generating complex, nonautonomous integrable models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework connecting autonomous scattering data (a common input in integrable models) to time-dependent dynamics is highly relevant for understanding how external time-varying fields or time-dependent couplings affect integrable quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a general mathematical procedure to construct time-dependent integrable chiral field theories starting only from autonomous unitary difference-form S-matrices satisfying the Yang-Baxter equation.

**Main result.** The nonautonomous dynamics are entirely determined by the initial autonomous scattering data, chiral kinematics, and the constraint of spatial homogeneity, providing a geometric route to time-dependent integrable systems.

**Method.** The method involves mapping spectral parameters along free chiral characteristics to define a spectral space, using the inverse Cayley transform to derive the local interaction, and imposing spatial homogeneity to fix the time dependence.

**Model / system.** The focus is on chiral field theories with linear dispersion, characterized by a Hamiltonian derived from a local contact interaction $V(u)$. The theory is analyzed using factorized scattering data derived from the S-matrix $S(u)$.

**Key observables.** The time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t)$ and the resulting many-body wavefunctions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral parameter $u$, which evolves affinely in time as $u(t) = 2\kappa t + eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes chiral field theories with linear dispersion and requires spatial homogeneity of the right-left scattering forces.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the framework by first establishing the connection between autonomous scattering data and the local interaction via the Cayley transform. It then uses chiral kinematics and spatial homogeneity to determine the time dependence of the interaction, leading to the final construction of the time-dependent Hamiltonian and wavefunctions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a general procedure for constructing time-dependent integrable chiral field theories from autonomous unitary difference-form $S$-matrices satisfying the Yang-Baxter equation. Spectral parameters are transported along the free right- and left-moving characteristics, defining a map from physical spacetime to spectral space on which the two-body scattering data are evaluated. Requiring spatially homogeneous right-left scattering forces the characteristic map to be affine. The inverse Cayley transform then determines the local contact interaction, while evaluation along the affine spectral trajectory fixes its time dependence. Thus, the nonautonomous interaction is determined by the autonomous scattering data together with chiral kinematics and spatial homogeneity, rather than being introduced independently. The Yang--Baxter equation supplies the factorized many-body transport, and on a spatial circle periodicity leads to quantum Knizhnik--Zamolodchikov (qKZ) equations whose compatibility defines a flat discrete transport in spectral space. Pulling the corresponding spectral-space amplitudes back to physical coordinates gives the time-dependent many-body wavefunctions. Rational $SU(N)$, trigonometric $U_q(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_2)$, and rational $O(N)$ scattering illustrate how the same mechanism generates distinct nonautonomous interactions. The resulting framework gives a geometric route from autonomous factorized scattering data to time-dependent integrable field theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a framework to determine when a fusion category, particularly those arising from quantum groups, admits a 'split real form.' By translating the problem into group-theoretic splitting conditions and analyzing cohomology obstructions, the authors establish powerful criteria for the existence of such real structures, which has implications for classifying physical symmetries in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep connection between abstract algebraic structures (fusion categories, Galois theory) and physical symmetries (like those governing quantum groups) provides powerful mathematical tools applicable to classifying phases of matter or topological orders.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical conditions for a fusion category, particularly those arising from quantum groups, to possess a 'split real form' over a specified field extension.

**Main result.** It proves that every fusion category $\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{g}, k)$ associated with a quantum group has a split real form, although the standard braiding structure itself often does not.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques from Galois descent theory, group cohomology ($H^2, H^3$), and the study of algebraic obstructions derived from fusion coefficients and R-matrices.

**Model / system.** The primary mathematical objects are fusion categories, specifically $\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{g}, k)$ derived from quantum groups at roots of unity, and representation categories $	ext{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(G)$ of certain finite groups $G$.

**Key observables.** The existence of a split form, the degree-two obstruction $\Omega_{\Phi}(C)$, and the vanishing of cohomology classes are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quantum parameter $k$, the root datum $(\mathfrak{g}, k)$, and the structure of the Galois group $	ext{Gal}(L/K)$ are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a finite Galois extension $L/K$ and relies on specific constructions involving the quasi-R-matrix for quantum groups.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general descent theory for fusion categories (Chunk 2) to specific applications to quantum groups (Chunk 1), and further analyzes the concrete case of representation categories of Frobenius groups (Chunks 3 & 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate split Galois descent for fusion categories through framed equivalences, reducing the existence of a split form to a group-theoretic splitting problem. The associated degree-two obstruction combines categorical coherence with stable descent of the simple objects, while fusion spaces impose index and parity restrictions. For quantum groups at roots of unity, we use the quasi-$R$-matrix to make the bar involution tensor-compatible and prove that the underlying fusion category $\mathcal C(\mathfrak g,k)$, associated with the simply connected root datum, has a split form over the maximal totally real subfield of the cyclotomic field generated by the quantum parameter. The same holds for all its cyclotomic Galois conjugates; in particular, every $\mathcal C(\mathfrak g,k)$ has a split real form. By contrast, no split real form exists for the standard braiding except on $\mathcal C(E_8,1)$ and $\mathcal C(D_{4m},1)$, $m \geq 1$. We further show that associative zesting can destroy real descent of $\mathcal C(\mathfrak g,k)$, while every braided zesting of $\mathcal C(\mathfrak g,k)$ still admits a split real form as an underlying fusion category. Applications to pointed and finite-group representation categories give criteria for real descent in terms of cohomology and Frobenius--Schur indicators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a provable quantum-classical separation for sampling from continuous Gibbs distributions on a torus. It shows that quantum algorithms can achieve a quadratic speedup in the barrier amplitude compared to the best classical algorithms. The separation relies on the specific smoothness properties (s-Gevrey class) of the underlying energy potential.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, quantitative quantum advantage for sampling complex, high-dimensional probability distributions arising in statistical mechanics, which is highly relevant for simulating physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proves the first quantum-classical separation for sampling from a Gibbs distribution over a continuous domain.

**Main result.** It shows that quantum algorithms can sample the state quadratically faster in the barrier amplitude ($\alpha$) compared to the best classical algorithms, achieving a separation ratio of $\Omega(\alpha^{c})$.

**Method.** The separation is established by deriving an information-theoretic lower bound for classical query complexity using an adversarial 'hide-and-seek' argument, and an upper bound using a quantum algorithm based on temperature annealing and quantum singular value thresholding.

**Model / system.** The system is sampling from a Gibbs state $p \propto e^{-eta E}$ defined on the $d$-dimensional torus $\mathbb{T}^d$, where $E$ is a smooth ($s$-Gevrey) potential. The difficulty is controlled by the barrier amplitude $\alpha = e^{eta\Delta}$.

**Key observables.** Total variation distance ($	ext{TV}$), query complexity ($N_{	ext{cl}}$ vs $N_q$), and the barrier amplitude $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Barrier amplitude $\alpha$, inverse temperature $eta$, Gevrey smoothness exponent $s$ ($s>1$ required for separation), and dimension $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The separation holds for $s$-Gevrey functions where $s>1$ and $s 
eq \infty$. The quantum bound relies on the structure of the annealing schedule and the coherent gradient oracle.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the role of Gevrey smoothness by showing the decay of Fourier modes (requiring $s>1$) and demonstrating that mere smoothness ($s=\infty$) or analyticity ($s=1$) is insufficient for the separation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the main theorem, proves the classical lower bound using an adversarial argument, presents the quantum algorithm using temperature annealing, and finally proves the separation ratio.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove the first quantum--classical separation for a sampling problem over a continuous domain. For a class of Gibbs states $p\propto e^{-βE}$ on the torus $\mathbb{T}^d$ with smooth ($s$-Gevrey) potential and barrier amplitude $α=e^{βΔ}$, where $Δ= \max E-\min E$, every classical algorithm---querying the value, gradient, or any higher-order derivatives of the log-density---requires $Ω(α)$ queries to sample at constant accuracy in total variation distance, while a quantum algorithm based on quantum singular value thresholding and temperature annealing samples with $\tilde{O}\left(\sqrtα\right)$ queries to an oracle for the gradient. The advantage is quadratic in the barrier amplitude, which becomes exponential in the dimension, $e^{Ω(d)}$, at low temperature. The classical bound is information-theoretic, holding for every classical algorithm with query access to the Gibbs potential and its derivatives at any order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to characterize the higher-order quantum geometry of magnons in magnetic materials using two-magnon Raman Circular Dichroism (RCD). By decomposing the RCD signal into six distinct geometric tensor contributions, the authors establish RCD as a powerful, non-trivial spectroscopic tool for probing generalized quantum geometry beyond standard metrics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous theoretical framework connecting measurable spectroscopic signals (RCD) directly to abstract, higher-order geometric properties of the underlying quantum excitations (magnons).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a gauge-invariant theory to probe the higher-order quantum geometry of magnons using two-magnon Raman Circular Dichroism (RCD).

**Main result.** RCD is established as a spectroscopic probe for generalized magnon quantum geometry, with the spectrum being governed by higher-order geometric tensors.

**Method.** The theory decomposes the Raman operator into Berry connections and covariant derivatives, leading to the identification of six distinct higher-order quantum-geometric tensors that contribute to the two-magnon RCD intensity.

**Model / system.** The study applies the theory to a field-polarized Kitaev magnet, modeled by a spin Hamiltonian, and analyzes magnon excitations using Linear Spin-Wave Theory (LSWT).

**Key observables.** Two-magnon RCD intensity, $I_{2M}^{RCD}(\omega)$, and six higher-order quantum-geometric tensors (QGTs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kitaev coupling constant ($K$), exchange coupling ($J$), applied magnetic field ($h$), and field angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on LSWT and the successful decomposition of the Raman operator into specific geometric terms using projector representations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the RCD intensity dependence on the magnetic field angle, resolved into three two-magnon sectors, and decomposed into six distinct geometric components.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically builds the theory by defining single-state quantum geometric tensors, deriving their projector representations for second-order terms, and finally applying this framework to calculate the two-magnon RCD intensity as a sum of six geometric contributions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a gauge-invariant theory of higher-order magnon quantum geometry probed by two-magnon Raman circular dichroism (RCD). The Raman operator decomposes into Berry connections, covariant derivatives, and products of Berry connections, giving rise to quantum geometric tensors beyond the quantum metric and Berry curvature. Applying this theory to a field-polarized Kitaev magnet, we show that higher-order geometric tensors govern the dichroic response. Our results establish RCD as a spectroscopic probe of generalized magnon quantum geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a deep physical principle governing when the fundamental F-symbols of anyon theories can be made real in a gauge. By linking this reality condition to the flatness of the charge-conjugation symmetry, the authors provide a unifying framework for understanding topological phases. This is significant because it offers a concrete, symmetry-based criterion for classifying quantum materials exhibiting topological order.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological invariants (F-symbols) and symmetry constraints (charge-conjugation flatness) provides a powerful, general principle for classifying realizable quantum phases, which is relevant for understanding emergent symmetries in strongly correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the physical conditions under which the F-symbols, which encode the associativity of fusion in anyon theories, can be made real via a suitable gauge transformation.

**Main result.** The reality of the F-symbols is shown to be governed by a physical mechanism related to the flatness of the braided charge-conjugation symmetry within the associated unitary ribbon category.

**Method.** The authors analyze the relationship between the F-symbols and their complex conjugates using gauge transformations, reducing the problem to a condition on the symmetry structure of the category.

**Model / system.** The theory is formulated using Unitary Modular Tensor Categories (UMTCs) that model $2+1$ dimensional Topological Quantum Field Theories (TQFTs) and anyon models, including families of Chern-Simons theories.

**Key observables.** The F-symbols, which quantify the associativity of fusion, and the associated R-matrices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the charge-conjugation symmetry, specifically its 'flatness' or the grading property of the associated Frobenius-Schur data.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a unitary ribbon category and relies on relating the reality condition to the trivialization of local gauge transformations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the concept of 'flatness' in the context of the charge-conjugation symmetry, showing the required triviality of the holonomy at fusion vertices.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the argument by first establishing the gauge equivalence between F-symbols and their conjugates, then linking this equivalence to the physical condition of charge-conjugation symmetry, and finally showing that 'flatness' of this symmetry guarantees the existence of a real gauge.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The $F$-symbols of an anyon theory encode the associativity of fusion and constitute some of the theory's most fundamental and, simultaneously, subtle data. Much of the subtlety lies in the gauge-dependence of the $F$-symbols. Despite their generic complexity, many braided anyon theories admit gauges in which all $F$-symbols are real, a phenomenon for which no general organising principle has been known. We identify a physical mechanism underlying this reality. For a unitary ribbon fusion category admitting an appropriate braided charge-conjugation symmetry, we show that the complex-conjugated $F$-symbols are related to the original ones by a gauge transformation. Finding a real gauge is thereby reduced to a condition on these transformations. When the charge-conjugation symmetry is suitably "flat,'' or equivalently when the associated "twisted'' Frobenius-Schur (or "generalized'' Kawanaka-Matsuyama) data respects a grading, these local transformations can be trivialised and a real gauge exists. This framework unifies a broad range of previously disparate examples, including families of Chern-Simons theories whose $F$-symbols are difficult to directly compute. Finally, we exhibit a unitary ribbon category that realizes a novel obstruction to the existence of a real gauge and therefore has inherently complex $F$-symbols.

</details>
