<a id="paper-2608.24631"></a>
### [When Similarity Is Interaction-Driven: Quantum Kernels for Regime-Sensitive Learning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24631v1)

**Authors:** Hanqiu Peng, Jianlong Lu, Ying Chen  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.24631v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.24631_figures/2608.24631_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Learning curves for the balanced (d, k) = (20, 4) setting. Lines and bands show mean test accuracy and ±1 standard deviation.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes an interaction-driven quantum kernel designed for machine learning tasks where data similarity depends on complex, high-order variable interactions. By encoding these interactions via entangled Pauli-string feature maps, the kernel achieves superior predictive performance on synthetic and real-world fraud detection benchmarks. The study emphasizes that the advantage lies in the feature-map's geometric alignment with the underlying physical structure, rather than quantum computational speedup.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in ML, the use of entangled Pauli-string feature maps and the focus on encoding specific, high-order correlations relates to how complex quantum states can represent structured correlations, which is relevant to understanding entanglement structure in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how similarity in complex decision systems, such as fraud detection, is governed by high-order interactions between variables rather than simple Euclidean distance.

**Main result.** The proposed interaction-driven quantum kernel consistently outperforms standard kernels on synthetic benchmarks and achieves state-of-the-art performance on real-world fraud detection datasets.

**Method.** They introduce a thin-slab interaction model and construct a quantum kernel using entangled Pauli-string feature maps that explicitly encode sparse, high-order block interactions.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum kernel applied to data structured into disjoint blocks, where the label generation is based on planted high-order block interactions, creating a regime-sensitive learning task.

**Key observables.** Mean accuracy and F1 score on fraud detection benchmarks; the kernel's ability to induce a geometry sensitive to interaction regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction order (up to eighth-order); input dimension (up to 80 dimensions); imbalance ratio of the data.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance advantage is shown to be predictive and representational, not necessarily evidence of a computational quantum speedup, as the kernel is classically evaluable.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the thin-slab model, proposes the interaction-driven quantum kernel, proves its theoretical properties (PSD, block-factorized), and validates its performance through controlled synthetic experiments across various interaction orders, culminating in real-world fraud detection benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Similarity in many decision systems is governed not by distance alone but by interactions among variables. In fraud and anomaly detection, small local perturbations can cross interaction-sensitive decision boundaries while leaving ambient distance almost unchanged. Motivated by this setting, we introduce a thin-slab interaction model and an interaction-driven quantum kernel constructed from entangled Pauli-string feature maps. The feature map explicitly encodes sparse high-order block interactions. We show that the resulting fidelity kernel is positive semidefinite, admits an exact block-factorized formulation, and induces a geometry sensitive to changes in interaction regime. Across balanced and imbalanced synthetic experiments spanning third-, fourth-, sixth-, and eighth-order interactions, the proposed kernel consistently outperforms linear, radial basis function, Laplacian, and polynomial kernels, as well as an engineered-interaction linear baseline supplied with the planted block products. On real fraud-detection benchmarks, it achieves the highest mean accuracy and F1 on Credit Card Fraud Detection and ranks second on IEEE-CIS Fraud Detection. These findings show that quantum-kernel performance depends on alignment between feature-map geometry and the underlying predictive structure, rather than on Hilbert-space dimension alone. Because the prescribed block-factorized kernel can also be evaluated exactly on a classical computer, the results establish predictive and representational value rather than computational quantum speedup.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how high readout power drives nonlinear kinetic inductance detectors into complex dynamic regimes, leading to a stable, metastable latched state. By understanding this nonlinear response, the authors propose a method for achieving tunable, threshold-based detection for single-photon and rare-event experiments. The research bridges nonlinear circuit theory with superconducting detector physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concepts of driven nonlinear oscillators, bifurcation points, and metastable states are central to open quantum systems and quantum optics, providing a physical realization of non-trivial dynamical switching.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex pulse dynamics and the emergence of metastable latching states in nonlinear microwave kinetic inductance detectors (MKIDs) when driven by high readout power.

**Main result.** Strong driving biases can induce a transition into a metastable latched state beyond resonance bifurcation, creating a tunable detection threshold independent of the initial quasiparticle transient decay.

**Method.** The authors combine nonlinear resonator theory (Duffing oscillator framework) with time-domain circuit calculations to model the detector's response to absorbed energy pulses.

**Model / system.** The system is a MKID modeled as a nonlinear resonator coupled to a readout circuit, where the kinetic inductance introduces nonlinearity dependent on the readout current and absorbed energy.

**Key observables.** Pulse amplitude, shape, relaxation time, and the existence/stability of the latched metastable state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Readout bias power ($P_g$), frequency detuning ($x_0$), and the absorbed pulse energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between local steady-state behavior and finite pulse excursions, and the nonlinear response is dominated by the current-dependent inductance rather than just quasiparticle decay.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare linear vs. nonlinear pulse responses under varying bias and energy, and illustrate state-space trajectories showing switching to a stable, latched branch beyond the bifurcation point.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the nonlinear resonator framework to analyzing the time-domain response, demonstrating the transition from simple exponential decay to complex, extended, and finally, bistable/latching behavior.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microwave kinetic inductance detectors are typically operated at high readout power to raise the detector signal above system noise. At sufficiently large readout power, the current-dependent kinetic inductance couples the detector response to its readout bias. Using a nonlinear resonator framework and time-domain circuit calculations, we show that the amplitude, shape, and relaxation time of the driven detector's response depend on both the absorbed energy and on the readout bias. Strongly driven bias points produce amplified, extended, and non-exponential pulse responses. Qualitative agreement between calculated and measured pulse responses indicates that these effects are dominated by the driven nonlinear resonator dynamics rather than by altered quasiparticle dynamics. Beyond resonance bifurcation, sufficiently large pulse events drive the resonator between stable branches, resulting in a metastable latched state which persists after the quasiparticle transient has decayed. The pulse energy required for branch switching is set by the readout bias, suggesting a mode of triggered detection with an in-situ tunable threshold. Although nonlinear operation requires calibration of the bias- and energy-dependent response, the enhanced pulse amplitude and duration, together with tunable latching and the ability to select these parameters via the readout operating state, are likely to be of interest for single-photon and rare-event experiments, especially those limited by amplifier or system noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates how off-resonant light irradiation (Floquet engineering) can induce a valley-dependent effective mass in a jacutingaite tunnel junction. By analyzing spin- and valley-resolved transport, the authors show that the junction acts as a tunable opto-spintronic element capable of achieving high levels of spin and valley polarization. This control is achieved by engineering the effective Hamiltonian using external fields and the driving light.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of Floquet engineering to engineer topological properties (mass terms) in a solid-state system is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics, providing a mechanism to control quantum states using light.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how to achieve highly selective electron transport based on spin and valley degrees of freedom in a two-dimensional material junction.

**Main result.** The system can exhibit near-perfect valley filtering and substantial spin polarization by tuning external fields and light-matter interactions. The spin and valley polarizations can be independently switched by controlling the drive amplitude, sublattice potential, and exchange field.

**Method.** The analysis employs scattering theory applied to an effective static Dirac Hamiltonian derived from the high-frequency limit of Floquet engineering.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a monolayer jacutingaite tunnel junction ($	ext{Pt}_2	ext{HgSe}_3$) where the barrier is irradiated by off-resonant circularly polarized light. The low-energy theory is based on a Dirac-Weyl Hamiltonian incorporating intrinsic spin-orbit coupling, which is modified by the photon dressing effect.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization ($P_s$), valley polarization ($P_v$), spin- and valley-resolved transmission/reflection, and Landauer conductance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive amplitude ($A_0$), staggered sublattice potential ($V_z$), substrate-induced exchange field ($m_s$), and barrier width ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the high-frequency limit (off-resonant driving) and models the junction as having an undriven source/drain connected to a driven barrier region.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of conductance ($G_{\eta s z}$) on barrier width ($L$) showing Fabry-Pérot oscillations, and density plots showing the tunability of $P_s$ and $P_v$ across parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the effective Hamiltonian via high-frequency expansion, uses scattering theory to calculate transport coefficients, and then analyzes how tuning $A_0$, $V_z$, and $m_s$ controls the resulting spin and valley polarizations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study electron transport through a monolayer jacutingaite (Pt$_2$HgSe$_3$) tunnel junction in which only the barrier is irradiated by off-resonant circularly polarized light, while the leads remain undriven. In the high-frequency regime, the driven barrier reduces to an effective static Dirac Hamiltonian with a photon-dressed, valley-dependent mass term. A staggered sublattice potential $V_z$ and a substrate-induced exchange field $m_s$ provide additional tunable mass terms. Using scattering theory, we compute spin- and valley-resolved transmission and reflection, as well as the Landauer conductance. Photon dressing shifts the barrier {Dirac masses} with opposite signs in the ($\boldsymbol{K}, \boldsymbol{K}'$) valleys and induces a splitting of the propagation thresholds. The finite barrier then produces channel-dependent Fabry--Pérot-type interference through the phase $q_x^{ηs_z}L$. We find broad parameter windows with near-perfect valley filtering ($|P_v|\simeq 100\%$) and substantial spin polarization ($|P_s|\sim 70\%$). The dominant spin and valley polarizations can be switched by tuning the drive amplitude $A_0$, $V_z$, and~$m_s$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows that topological edge states in 2D Kerr resonator lattices can generate optical frequency combs and various coherent soliton structures, including dark, platicon, and bright-like states, even when the material operates in the normal-dispersion regime. This circumvents the need for auxiliary mechanisms typically required for comb generation in such materials. The findings establish topological arrays as a highly versatile platform for engineering broadband coherent light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from topological physics (edge states) with nonlinear optics (solitons/frequency combs), providing a novel, robust platform for integrated photonics that overcomes limitations imposed by material dispersion.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to demonstrate the generation of optical frequency combs and various coherent dissipative structures (dark, platicons, bright-like solitons) in the normal-dispersion regime without needing auxiliary physical mechanisms.

**Main result.** Topological edge states in 2D Kerr resonator lattices intrinsically satisfy the phase-matching condition for four-wave mixing, enabling comb generation and supporting a versatile range of soliton states in normal dispersion.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical modeling based on tight-binding Hamiltonians and the Ikeda map formalism to simulate field evolution in coupled resonator arrays.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional Kerr resonator lattice modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian, incorporating topological edge states and operating in the normal-dispersion regime.

**Key observables.** Collective topological dark solitons, topological platicons, bright-like soliton states, and the associated frequency comb spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Normal-dispersion regime, 2D lattice structure, pump detuning, and coupling strengths (J).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that topological edge states provide an intrinsic phase-matching mechanism, eliminating the need for external pumping or avoided crossings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the linear absorption spectrum showing edge state resonances, intensity distributions of dark solitons on the edge, and the evolution of the comb spectrum across different soliton regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system and the problem (comb generation in normal dispersion), details the mechanism (topological edge states satisfying phase-matching), and presents results showing the continuous evolution between different soliton types.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bright dissipative Kerr solitons generated in anomalous-dispersion microresonators underpin integrated optical frequency combs that have enabled applications including precision metrology, spectroscopy, coherent communications, and optical frequency synthesis. In contrast, Kerr resonators operating in the normal-dispersion regime can support dark solitons and platicons, but even the initiation of frequency comb generation typically requires auxiliary mechanisms, such as avoided mode crossings or pulsed pumping, to satisfy the phase-matching condition for four-wave mixing. Here we show that topological edge states in two-dimensional Kerr resonator lattices intrinsically satisfy this phase-matching condition, enabling the generation of optical frequency combs and novel coherent dissipative structures in the normal-dispersion regime without any auxiliary mechanisms. The resulting frequency combs self-organize into collective dark solitons that exhibit large-scale spatiotemporal synchronization across multiple resonators at the entire lattice edge. By tuning the pump detuning, we demonstrate the continuous evolution of topological dark solitons into topological platicons while preserving collective synchronization. Remarkably, we show that the topological lattice also supports collective bright-like soliton states despite all constituent resonators operating in the normal-dispersion regime. Our results establish topological Kerr resonator arrays as a versatile route for engineering a broad family of coherent dissipative structures, ranging from dark solitons to bright-like soliton states, within a single platform. They also provide a route toward integrated frequency-comb generation in material platforms and wavelength ranges where normal material dispersion has traditionally constrained comb generation in conventional single-resonator systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theoretical framework to classify the complex composite fermion states expected in the fractional quantum spin Hall effect at $\nu=3$. It extends established FQHE concepts to two-spin systems and proposes a rigorous experimental protocol combining shot noise, thermal conductance, and edge tunneling to definitively identify the underlying topological order.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical framework for characterizing topological phases in multi-component systems, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron behavior in novel 2D materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To classify and understand the composite fermion (CF) states in the putative fractional quantum spin Hall effect (FQSHE) at filling factor $\nu=3$, and to propose experimental methods to identify the relevant state.

**Main result.** The paper classifies two-component CF states into several groups (SV, JCX, MSD) and proposes a combination of three experimental probes—shot noise, thermal conductance, and edge tunneling—to uniquely identify the true topological state.

**Method.** The analysis uses the composite fermion picture, extending it to two spin components, and derives theoretical constraints on edge physics using effective Lagrangians and scaling dimensions.

**Model / system.** The system is the FQSHE in $\text{MoTe}_2$, modeled using composite fermions that account for two spin components. The theory builds upon established FQHE descriptions by incorporating spin-related degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Fractional quasiparticle charges ($e/2$ or $e/4$), chiral central charge (from thermal conductance), and the emergent symmetry group revealed by edge tunneling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor $\nu=3$; the flux quanta parameters ($m, s_1, s_2$); and the scaling dimension $\Delta$ for tunneling operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the applicability of the composite fermion picture to the two-component spin system, and that the three proposed experimental probes are sufficient for full characterization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a device setup involving two Quantum Point Contacts (QPCs) connecting the $\nu=3$ edge to other interfaces, relevant for the proposed tunneling measurement.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the CF classification at $\nu=3$, then developing the edge theory using particle-hole transformations and effective Lagrangians, and finally detailing the necessary experimental diagnostics using noise, thermal transport, and tunneling measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Well-understood fractional quantum Hall states in GaAs and graphene can be described in terms of weakly interacting composite fermions. It is natural to expect that the same unifying principle applies to the putative fractional quantum spin Hall effect in MoTe$_2$. Since the quantum spin Hall effect involves two spin components, two types of composite fermions must be present. We classify all two-component composite-fermion states at the filling factor $ν=3$. The classification includes the three classes of states, which were introduced from different physical perspectives in Refs. Sodemann Villadiego, Phys. Rev. B 110, 045114 (2024), Jian et al., Phys. Rev. X 15, 021063 (2025), and May-Mann et al., Phys. Rev. B 111, L201111, (2025), as well as two new classes of states. A majority of the composite-fermion states break the time-reversal symmetry. We review quasiparticle charges, statistics, and edge theories for each possible state. We also address a way of identifying the experimentally relevant state or states. This can be accomplished by combining three probes. First, the shot noise technique provides information about fractional charges. Second, thermal conductance helps count edge modes. The third probe is based on a new idea and involves transport between two quantum point contacts along a single edge. We find that the current from one contact to the other depends on the shape of the edge channel, which can be controlled with a side gate. The probe reveals the emergent symmetry group of the low-energy edge theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses STM to visualize how the moiré potential in graphene/hBN superlattices causes a periodic, site-dependent renormalization of flat electronic bands. The key finding is that this spatial modulation only exists above a critical moiré period ($\sim 10 	ext{ nm}$), establishing a direct, real-space link between the moiré potential and the emergence of topological order like the FQAHE.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct visualization of how a geometric potential (the moiré pattern) dictates the emergence of topological order in a correlated system provides a concrete, experimentally verifiable mechanism for realizing exotic quantum states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to clarify the microscopic role of the moiré potential in driving emergent correlated phases, such as the Fractional Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect (FQAHE), in rhombohedral graphene superlattices.

**Main result.** The authors visualize that the moiré potential causes a spatially periodic renormalization of flat bands, and this modulation vanishes below a critical moiré period ($\sim 10 	ext{ nm}$), which coincides with the threshold for FQAHE.

**Method.** The research combines Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy (STM/STS) to image real-space electronic structure with theoretical modeling to attribute the modulation to atomic corrugation-induced charge redistribution.

**Model / system.** The system studied is rhombohedral graphene (RG) superlattices formed with hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) substrates (e.g., R4G/hBN, R5G/hBN). The physics involves flat-band physics, correlated electron behavior, and the formation of topological states.

**Key observables.** Spatially resolved energy shifts of flat bands ($	ext{d}I/	ext{d}V$ spectra), the magnitude of the band splitting gap ($\Delta$), and the persistence of moiré-scale modulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Moiré period ($\lambda_m$), which shows a critical threshold around $10 	ext{ nm}$; the filling level (full vs. partial); and the twist angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism driving the modulation is theoretically attributed to atomic corrugation inducing a spatially varying local chemical potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM topography and spatially resolved $	ext{d}I/	ext{d}V$ spectra demonstrating site-dependent energy shifts. They illustrate the disappearance of this modulation when the moiré period is too small, and map the spatial reshaping of correlated states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the importance of moiré-driven phases. It then uses STM/STS to observe the spatial renormalization of flat bands, demonstrating the dependence on the moiré period. Finally, it links this observed periodicity threshold to the theoretical requirement for topological order.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rhombohedral graphene/hBN moiré superlattices exhibit flat-band-driven emergent phases, including superconductivity and the fractional quantum anomalous Hall effect (FQAHE), yet the microscopic role of the moiré potential remains unclear. Here, using scanning tunneling microscopy, we visualize moiré-modulated spatial renormalization of flat bands in rhombohedral pentalayer and tetralayer graphene/hBN superlattices. We observe spatially hierarchical filling, manifested as periodic energy shifts of the flat bands at the moiré scale, leading to spatial reshaping of correlated states in the interacting regime. Remarkably, this modulation vanishes below a ~10 nm moiré period--the same threshold below which the FQAHE is absent. Theoretical modeling attributes this mechanism to atomic-corrugation-induced charge redistribution. Our work provides real-space visualization of moiré-engineered flat-band reconstruction, resolving a key link between moiré periodic potential and emergent topological order.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates how structured light, specifically optical vortices, can be used to write precise, controllable topological spin textures in chiral magnetic films. By controlling the beam's orbital and spin angular momentum, researchers can deterministically encode these quantum optical properties into the magnetic topology, opening a new pathway for quantum-controlled spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics (structured light, OAM) directly into condensed matter magnetism, providing a mechanism for non-invasive, quantum-number-controlled manipulation of magnetic topology, which is highly relevant for advanced spintronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the deterministic and local writing of in-plane topological spin textures (like bimerons and antibimerons) within chiral ferromagnetic thin films using structured light, specifically optical vortices (OVs).

**Main result.** Structured light provides a versatile route for topology-selective writing, where the optical quantum numbers (OAM, SAM) can be directly encoded into the topological charge (Q) of the resulting magnetic texture.

**Method.** The study employs micromagnetic simulations to model the time evolution of magnetization dynamics driven by pulsed Laguerre-Gaussian optical vortex fields.

**Model / system.** The system is an ultrathin ferromagnetic film exhibiting chiral magnetic properties, governed by a free energy functional and simulated using the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation. The driving force is a time-dependent magnetic field derived from the structured light.

**Key observables.** Topological charge (Q), magnetic texture morphology (bimerons/antibimerons), and the dependence of these on optical quantum numbers (m, p, polarization s).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Material parameters include $M_s$, $A$, $D$, and $K_x$; simulation parameters involve beam waist ($W$), pulse duration ($t_{	ext{pulse}}$), and OAM ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the paraxial approximation for the OV field and assumes the dynamics are governed by the LLG equation under the influence of the structured light pulse.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of OV incidence on a chiral film, the spatial profiles of LG modes, and time-evolution maps showing the nucleation of single or clustered topological textures corresponding to specific input optical parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical model and Hamiltonian, detailing the simulation protocol (three-stage writing process), presenting results for specific polarization/OAM combinations, and concluding with a general framework for topology-selective writing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structured light offers a powerful means of controlling light-matter interactions through multiple tunable optical degrees of freedom. Using micromagnetic simulations, we investigate the nucleation of asymmetric bimerons and antibimerons by pulsed Laguerre-Gaussian optical vortices in chiral ferromagnetic thin films with $C_{nv}$ and $D_{2d}$ symmetries, respectively. For optical vortices with orbital angular momentum (OAM) $|m|=1$, circularly polarized beams deterministically nucleate a single bimeron or antibimeron via the interplay of spin angular momentum, OAM, and magnetic chirality, whereas linearly polarized beams produce textures whose topological charge directly follows the OAM ($Q=m$). Optical vortices with OAM $|m|>1$ nucleate clusters and other configurations composed of multiple spin textures, whose morphology and topological charge depend sensitively on the optical quantum numbers and pulse parameters. These findings reveal a route to topology-selective writing through the encoding of optical quantum numbers into distinct in-plane topological magnetic states.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel, scattering-driven mechanism for the layer Hall effect in magnons, bypassing the need for topological band structures. By analyzing a nanowire on a magnetic film, the authors show that non-reciprocal dipolar scattering under a temperature gradient generates measurable transverse thermal Hall currents. This provides a robust, field-tunable pathway for realizing this effect in conventional magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the primary focus is condensed matter magnetism, the development of a scattering-theory approach to engineer directional transport (thermal/spin currents) based on symmetry breaking (non-reciprocity) is highly relevant to open quantum systems and engineered quantum transport phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a fundamentally distinct mechanism for the layer Hall effect (LHE) in bosonic excitations, specifically magnons, that is driven by non-reciprocal dipolar scattering rather than geometric Berry curvature effects.

**Main result.** The study predicts a magnon LHE where a longitudinal temperature gradient drives opposite transverse thermal Hall currents, yielding a sizable Hall angle ($\sim 6^\circ$) in conventional magnetic heterostructures.

**Method.** The authors employ a microscopic scattering theory approach, deriving hybridization coefficients and analyzing the resulting magnon current densities under a temperature gradient.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a hybrid chiral architecture: a magnetic nanowire placed atop a magnetic film, interacting via an interlayer dipolar coupling. The analysis focuses on the magnon excitations in this coupled system.

**Key observables.** Transverse thermal Hall currents ($	ilde{J}_z, J_z$), the Hall angle ($\sim 6^\circ$), and the dependence of currents on the external magnetic field orientation ($	heta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-reciprocal nature of the dipolar coupling ($g_{\kappa}(kz)$), the applied longitudinal temperature gradient ($
abla T$), and the external magnetic field strength ($\mu_0H_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ballistic regime (micrometer length scales) and attributes the effect to the non-reciprocity of the dipolar coupling, thereby bypassing the need for topological engineering.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical setup: a magnetic nanowire on a magnetic film subjected to a longitudinal temperature gradient, with magnetization tilted by angles $	heta$ and $	ilde{	heta}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the limitations of Berry curvature mechanisms to developing a scattering-theory framework based on non-reciprocal dipolar coupling, calculating the resulting transverse currents, and demonstrating the tunability and magnitude of the effect.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The layer Hall effect has been exclusively attributed to layer-locked Berry curvature, posing a fundamental barrier to its realization in conventional magnets. Here we report a fundamentally distinct layer Hall effect for bosonic excitations, i.e., magnons, which originates solely from non-reciprocal dipolar scattering at heterointerfaces, thereby decoupling the phenomenon from geometric-phase mechanisms. Using a microscopic scattering theory, we demonstrate that a longitudinal temperature gradient drives opposite transverse thermal Hall currents in a nanowire atop a magnetic film, with the direction fully reconfigurable by the applied magnetic field. The effect yields a significant Hall angle of $\sim 6^{\circ}$ in conventional magnetic heterostructures, eliminating the need for topological engineering. Our findings establish a scattering-driven paradigm for layer Hall effect, extendable to ferrons and polar phonons, and predict a Hall response that is readily detectable in conventional magnetic heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically models intervalley magnetotrions in 2D materials like silicene and stanene under combined electric and magnetic fields. It establishes that the system exhibits an exact separation of center-of-mass and internal dynamics when carrier masses are equal. This allows for independent, field-tunable control over both the collective motion (Landau levels) and the binding energy of the trion complex.

**Why it may be interesting.** The independent tunability of collective (c.m.) and internal degrees of freedom by external fields provides a powerful platform for engineering quantum states, relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to study intervalley magnetotrions in buckled two-dimensional materials under external electric and magnetic fields.

**Main result.** It shows that the center-of-mass and internal motions of these trions can separate exactly under the condition of equal electron and hole effective masses. Furthermore, the trion binding energy is found to increase monotonically with both applied fields.

**Method.** The analysis uses the effective-mass approximation to formulate the three-particle Schrödinger equation, solving the center-of-mass motion via a harmonic oscillator Hamiltonian and the internal motion using the hyperspherical harmonics method.

**Model / system.** The system involves trions (three-particle bound states) in Xene monolayers (silicene, germanene, stanene), modeled with the Rytova–Keldysh interaction potential, and subjected to perpendicular electric and magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Trion binding energy, quantized Landau surface energies, and the separation of center-of-mass and internal motion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-magnetic-field regime ($B \gtrsim 15 	ext{ T}$), electric field ($E_\perp$), and the condition of equal effective masses ($m_{e1} = m_{e2} = m_h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The effective-mass approximation is employed, and the analysis is restricted to the high-magnetic-field regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates equal-mass intervalley trions in Xene monolayers, detailing the conditions for mass equality and showing the associated band structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework for the three-particle system, then analyzes the center-of-mass motion leading to tunable Landau states, and finally investigates the internal dynamics using advanced quantum mechanical methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a theoretical framework for intervalley magnetotrions in buckled two-dimensional materials, including silicene, germanene, and stanene, subjected to perpendicular electric and magnetic fields. Within the effective-mass approximation, the three-particle Schrödinger equation is formulated with the Rytova--Keldysh interaction potential and analyzed in the high-magnetic-field regime. We demonstrate that intervalley trions with equal electron and hole effective masses constitute an exceptional case for which the center-of-mass and internal motions separate exactly. The center-of-mass motion is governed by a two-dimensional harmonic-oscillator Hamiltonian, leading to quantized Landau states whose energies form electrically tunable Landau surfaces controlled by the magnetic field and the electric-field dependence of the carrier effective masses. The internal motion is investigated by solving the three-body Schrödinger equation within the framework of the hyperspherical harmonics method. Numerical calculations reveal that the trion binding energy increases monotonically with both magnetic and electric fields owing to the combined effects of magnetic confinement and electric-field-induced enhancement of the effective masses. The strongest binding is obtained for silicene, followed by stanene and germanene. The present work provides a unified description of both the collective center-of-mass motion and the internal dynamics of magnetotrions in Xene monolayers, demonstrating that both degrees of freedom can be independently manipulated by external electric and magnetic fields.

</details>

### [Electronic cooling of a TiW alloy normal-metal island using Nb-based superconducting tunnel junctions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24455v1)

**Authors:** Joel Hätinen, Renan Pires Loreto, Arvind Kumar, Jani Taskinen, Mika Prunnila

**Summary.** This paper reports on the successful demonstration of electronic cooling in a TiW normal-metal island using superconducting tunnel junctions (NIS coolers). By measuring the temperature drop and characterizing the cooling power, the authors validate the use of these junctions for cryogenic applications. The work provides quantitative data on cooling efficiency and discusses discrepancies between theory and experiment regarding the optimal operating voltage.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work explores non-equilibrium thermodynamics and energy transport in mesoscopic superconducting circuits, which touches upon non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and heat engine concepts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates and characterizes the electronic cooling effect achieved in a normal-metal island using superconducting tunnel junctions.

**Main result.** Significant electronic cooling, up to 217 mK absolutely, is observed in the TiW-based normal-metal island, confirming the viability of NIS junctions for cryogenic cooling applications.

**Method.** The study combines electrical measurements (I-V curves, temperature sensing) with theoretical modeling based on energy-selective quasiparticle tunneling.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a mesoscopic NIS tunnel junction stack (TiW-Al/AlOx/Nb), where cooling arises from the energy-selective tunneling of quasiparticles between the normal metal and the superconductor.

**Key observables.** Normal-metal electron temperature ($T_N$), absolute and relative temperature reduction ($\delta T_N$), and the optimal bias voltage ($V_{Opt}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature range (0.6 K to 3.5 K), maximum cooling values (217 mK, 27%), and superconducting energy gaps ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on single-particle tunneling expressions and assumes that fitted junction parameters are temperature-independent, although discrepancies suggest otherwise.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SEM images, $T_N$ vs. voltage curves, and plots detailing the maximum absolute and relative cooling performance as a function of bath temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by demonstrating the cooling effect, calibrating the thermometer, quantifying the cooling performance across a temperature range, and finally comparing the measured optimal voltage against ideal BCS theory predictions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

TiW thin films remain non-superconducting down to millikelvin temperatures and are compatible with large-scale CMOS manufacturing, making them attractive for cryogenic and quantum tunnel-junction devices, such as normal-metal-insulator-superconductor (NIS) thermal sensors and electron refrigerators. We demonstrate significant electronic cooling of a TiW-based normal-metal island and electron thermometry by using TiW-Al-AlOx-Nb NIS tunnel-junction stacks. The NIS thermometer enables local electron temperature measurements from 0.5 to 8.32 K. NIS cooling is observed between 0.6 and 3.5 K, with maximum absolute and relative temperature reductions of 217 mK and 27%, respectively. Calculations using tunnel-junction parameters obtained independently from current-voltage fits reproduce the measured optimum-voltage scale, which lies substantially below the ideal low-temperature prediction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a nonlocal reaction-diffusion model for population dynamics to study pattern formation influenced by the specific mathematical form of interaction kernels. It finds that the kernel choice dictates the system's resilience, demonstrating a 'Turing-before-Tipping' mechanism for some kernels but not others. This highlights how nonlocal interactions fundamentally alter the stability and pattern-forming capabilities of complex ecological systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ecology, the mathematical framework of pattern formation, bifurcation analysis, and resilience mechanisms in non-equilibrium systems shares theoretical overlap with pattern formation in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the choice of nonlocal interaction kernel affects pattern formation and persistence in a population model incorporating nonlocal competition and facilitation.

**Main result.** The choice of kernel creates a dichotomy in pattern behavior; Gaussian-type kernels show robust 'Turing-before-Tipping' resilience, while hat-type kernels do not exhibit this behavior regarding the death rate.

**Method.** The study employs center manifold analysis and numerical continuation methods to analyze pattern-forming bifurcations and pattern stability far from onset.

**Model / system.** It uses a single-species reaction-diffusion model for population density, incorporating nonlocal facilitation and competition terms governed by various kernels (Gaussian, exponential, hat, etc.).

**Key observables.** Pattern formation/bifurcation, pattern persistence past the tipping point, and early warning signs for population extinction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The death rate ($d$) and the relative spatial scale of competition ($\sigma_c$) to facilitation ($\sigma_f$) are key bifurcation parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis fixes the facilitation scale ($\sigma_f = 1$), and the study compares kernels based on whether their Fourier transforms are non-negative (Gaussian-type) or take on negative values (Hat-type).

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures illustrating example kernels in physical and Fourier space, and plots showing uniform solutions versus the death rate ($d$) for tipping (fold) and non-tipping (transcritical) bifurcations.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the model via center manifold expansion to determine pattern-forming bifurcations, followed by numerical continuation methods to study pattern stability and the 'Turing-before-Tipping' phenomenon.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spatial patterns, such as those in dryland vegetation models, have historically been studied   in systems of reaction-diffusion systems with pattern onset via a Turing bifurcation from a spatially uniform state. More recently, spatial patterns have been considered in models that incorporate spatially extended interactions via nonlocal interaction kernels. It remains largely underexplored if and how the choice of nonlocal interaction kernel contributes to differences in pattern formation and persistence, particularly in models that contain competition and facilitation. Here, we investigate spatial patterns in a reaction-diffusion model for a single species that includes nonlocal competition and facilitation processes; Gaussian, exponential, algebraic, hat, and smooth hat kernels are considered as specific examples. Via a center manifold analysis, and using the relative spatial scale of competition to facilitation and the death rate as bifurcation parameters, we identify that the choice of kernel has impacts on the pattern forming bifurcation. Bifurcations using the Gaussian, exponential, and algebraic kernels largely follow expectations of Turing patterns, but patterns in the hat and smooth hat kernels can form even when the scale of competition is less than that of facilitation. The dynamics of patterns far from onset are investigated via numerical continuation methods. The model produces the so-called "Turing-before-Tipping" phenomenon demonstrating that the arrangement into spatial patterns is an effective resilience mechanism against harsh conditions. Again, there is a kernel-dependent dichotomy in pattern behavior. Early warning signs for population extinction are observed with the Gaussian, exponential, and algebraic kernels, but not under the hat or smooth hat cases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel opto-spintronic platform using a $	ext{WSe}_2-	ext{NiPS}_3$ van der Waals heterostructure. By using circularly polarized light, the researchers generate highly spin-polarized photocurrents. Crucially, they show that the spin polarization remains robust across the material's magnetic phase transition, establishing spectral tuning as a powerful, field-free control mechanism for spin-current generation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using spectral tuning as a control knob for spin transport is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics, as it suggests a non-magnetic, purely optical method to manipulate spin states in solid-state devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge addressed is the efficient generation and control of spin-polarized currents in semiconductors, overcoming limitations like impedance mismatch and the need for external magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that spectral tuning of excitation light in a $	ext{WSe}_2-	ext{NiPS}_3$ van der Waals heterostructure can generate robust, spectrally protected spin-polarized photocurrents without external magnetic fields.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (photocurrent, photogalvanic spectroscopy using circularly polarized light) with first-principles electronic structure calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a p-n diode heterostructure composed of the antiferromagnetic semiconductor $	ext{NiPS}_3$ and the semiconductor $	ext{WSe}_2$. The mechanism relies on interfacial hybridization and spin-layer locking induced by an electric field.

**Key observables.** Photocurrent spectra ($	ext{I}$ vs. photon energy $\epsilon$), degree of photocurrent polarization ($\eta$), and the persistence of spin polarization across the magnetic phase transition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (especially near the Néel temperature), excitation photon energy ($\epsilon$), and the applied electric field strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed effects are attributed to electric-field-induced interfacial hybridization and symmetry breaking, which allows spectral tuning to control spin transport.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the diode, temperature-dependent photocurrent spectra showing circular dichroism, and the polarization dependence of the photogalvanic response as a function of polarization angle.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem, presents the $	ext{WSe}_2-	ext{NiPS}_3$ platform, details measurements showing high spin polarization, explains the mechanism via interfacial hybridization and symmetry protection, and concludes by positioning the material for opto-spintronic applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient generation and control of spin-polarized currents in semiconductors remain central challenges for spin-based electronics, particularly due to impedance mismatch and the reliance on magnetic fields or ferromagnetic contacts. Here, we introduce a materials platform for spectrally programmable spin transport based on a van der Waals heterostructure combining the antiferromagnetic semiconductor NiPS$_3$ with WSe$_2$. In a p-n diode architecture, circularly polarized excitation produces pronounced photoconductive resonances with spin polarization reaching 80% near the Neel temperature and persisting at 30% at room temperature. Remarkably, selected spectral bands retain their polarization sign across the magnetic phase transition, evidencing robust, spectrally protected spin-polarized current generation. Polarization-resolved photogalvanic measurements reveal a dominant circular injection-current mechanism, confirming spin-polarized carrier transport. First-principles calculations show that an applied electric field induces interfacial hybridization and spin-layer locking, giving rise to localized symmetry breaking and enhanced optical absorption while preserving global time-reversal symmetry. These results establish spectral tuning of excitation as a new control knob for spin transport, enabling spin-current generation without magnetic fields or polarization switching. Our findings position magnetic van der Waals heterostructures as a versatile platform for opto-spintronic functionality and spectrally programmable spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores quantum transport in SSH chains containing topological domain walls, showing that their interaction creates a self-similar hierarchy of minibands. By developing a recursive construction and analyzing the system via successive decimation, the authors demonstrate how localized topological states act as emergent degrees of freedom to engineer complex, multiscale spectral features. This establishes a general connection between recursive topological building blocks and self-similar spectral organization in 1D quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent, self-similar spectral structures arising from simple local topological defects provides a powerful, general framework for understanding multiscale physics in low-dimensional quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the interaction of topological domain-wall states in Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chains generates a self-similar, hierarchical structure in quantum transport properties.

**Main result.** The hybridization of domain-wall localized states leads to minibands whose structure can be recursively constructed, revealing a self-similar hierarchy of spectral gaps and resonances.

**Method.** The analysis combines the recursive scattering-matrix method with the development of an effective renormalized description based on successive decimation procedures.

**Model / system.** The system is based on SSH chains containing multiple topological domain walls. The model is analyzed by treating the domain-wall states as emergent degrees of freedom that define an effective, recursively built lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Transmission spectra, minibands, spectral gaps, and the hierarchy of interlaced singularities in the energy axis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimerization parameter (delta), number of domain walls ($N_d$), and the recursive generation index ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the recursive nature of the scattering-matrix method and the mathematical validity of the decimation procedure for generating effective parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the formation of domain-wall lattices, the resulting transmission spectra for increasing domain-wall numbers, and the recursive rules generating higher-order structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the initial SSH model with domain walls to demonstrating the recursive construction of effective SSH structures within minibands, culminating in a rigorous mathematical analysis of the singularity generation via successive decimation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the transport properties of Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chains containing multiple topological domain walls and show that their interaction generates a hierarchy of emergent spectral structures. Each domain wall contributes a localized state inside the SSH gap, and the hybridization of these states produces minibands whose signatures are directly reflected in the transmission spectra. By combining domain-wall lattices with different domain-wall separations, we construct effective SSH structures within the miniband subspace. The resulting transmission spectra reproduce the characteristic features of conventional SSH chains, including gap formation, finite-size resonances, and the correspondence between transmission spectra and band structure. The construction can be applied recursively, generating successive generations of effective SSH structures. As a consequence, effective SSH spectra repeatedly emerge within progressively narrower energy intervals, producing a self-similar hierarchy of minibands and spectral gaps. To understand the origin of this hierarchy, we develop an effective renormalized description based on successive decimation. The effective parameters exhibit a hierarchy of interlaced singularities whose number increases at each iteration. These singularities partition the energy axis into progressively finer intervals and provide a natural interpretation of the repeated fragmentation of the spectrum. Our results show that topological domain-wall states can act as emergent degrees of freedom from which multiscale transport channels, effective couplings, and hierarchical spectral structures may be engineered. More generally, the framework introduced here establishes a connection between recursive topological constructions, effective Hamiltonians, and the emergence of self-similar spectra in one-dimensional systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores Josephson junctions utilizing bipolar magnetic semiconductors (BMSs) to demonstrate electrically tunable superconducting behavior. The authors show that gate-tuning the BMS chemical potential can induce a $0-\pi$ transition without external magnetic fields. Furthermore, they analyze an anomalous supercurrent arising from spin-polarized structure, distinguishing it from general superconducting nonreciprocity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to control superconducting phase transitions purely electrically using magnetic semiconductors provides a novel platform for superconducting spintronics, relevant to understanding superconducting order parameters in engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Josephson effect in Josephson junctions incorporating bipolar magnetic semiconductors (BMSs) to achieve electrically tunable superconducting behavior.

**Main result.** The intrinsic exchange splitting allows for purely electrical control of the 0-$\pi$ transition without external magnetic fields, and the system exhibits an anomalous Josephson effect due to time-reversal symmetry breaking.

**Method.** The supercurrent and critical current are calculated by numerically diagonalizing the system's Hamiltonian, which models the junction as SC/BMS/SC.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Josephson junction built using a BMS. The model uses a lattice Hamiltonian incorporating hopping integrals, chemical potentials, and spin-dependent terms arising from the BMS's intrinsic exchange splitting and Rashba SOC.

**Key observables.** Critical Josephson current ($I_c$), Current-Phase Relation (CPR), and the occurrence of the $0-\pi$ transition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** BMS chemical potential ($\mu_b$), Rashba SOC strength ($\alpha$), and the superconducting gap.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed on a one-dimensional junction, and the conclusion regarding the absence of a diode effect is specific to this 1D geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the critical current dependence on the BMS band gap and chemical potential, and show the CPR relation for the junction under varying conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using a lattice model, then analyzes the dependence of $I_c$ on the BMS chemical potential, demonstrating the electrical control of the $0-\pi$ transition, and finally discusses the anomalous Josephson effect and its relation to nonreciprocity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate equilibrium currents in a one-dimensional Josephson junction incorporating a bipolar magnetic semiconductor (BMS). We show that the intrinsic exchange splitting of the spin-resolved bands enables purely electrical control of the $0$--$π$ transition through gate-tunable modulation of the BMS chemical potential, eliminating the need for an external magnetic field. This provides a viable route toward electrically tunable $π$-junction behavior and highlights the potential of BMS-based Josephson devices for phase-controllable superconducting electronics. Furthermore, in the presence of Rashba spin--orbit coupling, we find an anomalous Josephson effect characterized by a finite equilibrium supercurrent at zero phase difference. This behavior originates from the intrinsic breaking of time-reversal symmetry associated with the spin-polarized electronic structure of the BMS. Interestingly, despite the simultaneous breaking of time-reversal and inversion symmetries---conditions often associated with nonreciprocal superconducting transport---we do not observe a Josephson diode effect. Our results therefore highlight an important distinction between anomalous Josephson transport and superconducting nonreciprocity: the former does not necessarily imply a finite critical-current asymmetry between opposite current directions.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that depositing ultrathin oxide layers on graphene allows for precise engineering of surface plasmon polaritons via interfacial charge transfer doping. By using s-SNOM and quantitative modeling, the authors show that the oxide material dictates the plasmon wavelength and stability, establishing oxide/graphene stacks as a robust platform for tunable infrared optoelectronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a clear, tunable mechanism (interfacial charge transfer) to control plasmonic properties in 2D materials, which is highly relevant for designing stable, functional nanophotonic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how interfacial charge transfer between oxide and graphene layers can be used to systematically engineer the optical and electronic properties, specifically the surface plasmon polaritons (SPPs), in these heterostructures.

**Main result.** Depositing $	ext{MoO}_x$ strongly enhances the plasmonic response and increases graphene doping, while subsequent $	ext{ZnO}_x$ deposition partially reverses these effects; the structure proves to be a robust platform for stable nanophotonics.

**Method.** The study combines experimental characterization using scattering-type s-SNOM with quantitative modeling based on multilayer transfer-matrix methods and charge transfer doping models.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an oxide/graphene heterostructure (e.g., $	ext{MoO}_x/	ext{graphene}$), where the plasmon dispersion and carrier density are modeled as functions of oxide thickness and external gating voltage.

**Key observables.** Plasmon wavelength ($\lambda_p$), damping rate ($\gamma_p$), graphene carrier density ($n$), and the evolution of the plasmon dispersion relationship $E(q_p)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Oxide layer thickness ($d$), gate voltage ($V_g$), and the work functions of the constituent materials ($\Phi_M, \Phi_Z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the plasmon field decay using the complex in-plane wavevector, leading to an intensity decay length $L_I = \lambda_p / (4\pi)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show nano-IR images comparing bare graphene to structures with $	ext{MoO}_x$ and $	ext{ZnO}_x$, illustrating changes in fringe contrast and wavelength; dispersion plots map the plasmon energy vs. momentum.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents experimental measurements varying oxide thickness and gate voltage, correlating observed plasmon shifts and damping rates with theoretical models of interfacial charge transfer doping and plasmon field decay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interfacial charge transfer provides an effective route for tailoring the optical and electronic properties of two-dimensional materials. Here, we investigate infrared surface plasmon polaritons in oxide/graphene heterostructures using scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy. Ultrathin oxide overlayers deposited by physical vapor deposition enable systematic engineering of graphene plasmons through interfacial charge redistribution. MoOx strongly enhances the plasmonic response, producing a longer plasmon wavelength, stronger fringe contrast, and reduced damping, whereas a subsequently deposited ZnOx overlayer partially reverses these changes. Energy-dependent nano-infrared imaging combined with quantitative modeling reveals an increased graphene carrier density and the resulting modification of the plasmon dispersion. Thickness-dependent measurements show a rapid increase in charge-transfer doping at sub-nanometer MoOx thicknesses, followed by a weaker long-range contribution at larger overlayer thicknesses. Electrostatic gating further modulates the carrier density and produces a nonlinear response consistent with gate-dependent interfacial charge redistribution. In addition, an approximately 3-nm-thick MoOx overlayer stabilizes the plasmonic response for at least seven months under ambient conditions. These results establish oxide/graphene heterostructures as a robust platform compatible with scalable fabrication, providing a pathway toward stable and tunable infrared nanophotonic and optoelectronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports a breakthrough in synthesizing superconducting FeSe films by developing a greatly simplified MBE growth protocol. By eliminating complex substrate pre-treatments and post-growth annealing, the authors achieved high-quality, multi-unit-cell films exhibiting a high superconducting transition temperature of $\sim 20 	ext{ K}$. This significantly advances the practical synthesis of high-temperature superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state superconductivity, the detailed control over epitaxial growth, stoichiometry, and structural transitions (e.g., island vs. layer-by-layer growth) provides valuable experimental insights into achieving highly ordered quantum materials, which is relevant for understanding correlated electron behavior in artificial quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to achieve enhanced superconductivity in multilayer beta-FeSe films while significantly simplifying the complex molecular beam epitaxy (MBE) growth protocols previously required.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an enhanced superconducting transition temperature ($T_c \sim 20 	ext{ K}$) in 14-unit-cell films using a greatly simplified MBE protocol that eliminates the need for substrate pre-treatment or post-growth UHV annealing.

**Method.** The study employs MBE growth techniques coupled with extensive characterization, including AFM, RHEED, XRD, and ex situ electrical transport measurements, to correlate growth parameters with film quality and superconducting properties.

**Model / system.** The physical system is multi-unit-cell (UC) beta-FeSe films epitaxially grown on SrTiO3(100) substrates. The research focuses on controlling the film's stoichiometry and crystalline quality to maximize the superconducting transition temperature.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), root mean square (rms) roughness (AFM), XRD peak width (FWHM), and sheet resistance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Growth Temperature ($T_{	ext{GG}}$), Se/Fe flux ratio (FR), and film thickness (up to 14 UC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors challenge the prior understanding that elaborate protocols (pre-treatment, annealing) are strictly necessary for high-$T_c$ FeSe films.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of AFM surface morphology on growth temperature and Se/Fe FR, while XRD scans track changes in crystallinity (peak width) across different growth conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the background on enhanced $T_c$ in FeSe, presents the novel simplified growth protocol, details the characterization of morphology and structure using multiple techniques, and concludes with the measurement of high $T_c$ values.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-unit-cell (UC) \b{eta}-FeSe films grown on SrTiO3(100) continue to attract attention because of the significant enhancement in the superconducting transition temperature (Tc) compared to that in bulk FeSe. In prior reports of molecular beam epitaxy (MBE)-grown \b{eta}-FeSe/SrTiO3(100), elaborate growth protocols have been used to achieve enhanced Tc, leading to a general belief that careful pre-treatment of the SrTiO3 substrate and post-growth annealing in ultrahigh vacuum (UHV) are essential. Here, we report a greatly simplified protocol for the MBE growth of superconducting multi-UC \b{eta}-FeSe films on SrTiO3(100), eliminating the need for careful substrate pre-treatment and post-growth UHV annealing while still achieving an enhanced Tc. With appropriate capping, epitaxial films with 14 UC thickness exhibit a zero-resistance transition temperature Tc ~ 20 K in ex situ electrical transport measurements. The MBE optimization process is guided by the growth-parameter dependencies of film morphology and structural properties, as characterized by reflection high-energy electron diffraction, X-ray diffraction, atomic force microscopy, and scanning transmission electron microscopy.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work models the exciton fine structure in semiconductor nanocrystals with non-spherical shapes like cuboids and spheroids. It rigorously incorporates the effects of crystal symmetry and shape anisotropy into the exciton Hamiltonian. The key finding is that shape-induced exchange interactions significantly split the dark exciton states, providing crucial design rules for quantum emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed quantum mechanical treatment of shape-dependent excitonic properties in low-dimensional semiconductors is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information applications utilizing semiconductor quantum emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the exciton fine structure in semiconductor nanocrystals (NCs) that possess non-spherical, cuboidal, or spheroidal shapes.

**Main result.** The study identifies a non-negligible, shape-induced contribution to the short-range exchange interaction in cuboidal NCs, which causes a pronounced splitting of the dark exciton states.

**Method.** The authors employ symmetry analysis and non-perturbative theoretical calculations using an effective Hamiltonian derived from the Luttinger Hamiltonian for hole states.

**Model / system.** The system is colloidal semiconductor nanocrystals (e.g., zinc-blende structure) modeled with electron and hole wave functions, considering the effects of cubic symmetry and uniaxial anisotropy on the exciton Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Exciton fine structure splitting, hole ground state splitting, and oscillator strength.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Anisotropy ratios (q=c/b), material parameters (e.g., hole mass ratio $eta$), and the relative strength of anisotropic splitting versus exchange constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the energy potential profile is box-like (infinite potential) and that electron/hole wave functions can be factorized.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of hole ground state energy on material ratios for spherical and cubical NCs, and show the calculated exciton splitting ($\Delta$) as a function of the anisotropy ratio ($q=c/b$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general symmetry analysis to derive an effective Hamiltonian, then applies this to calculate hole energy splittings under anisotropic conditions, and finally analyzes the resulting exciton fine structure and oscillator strength.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the theory of the band-edge exciton fine structure in nanocrystals (NCs) with spheroidal and cuboidal shapes. The effects of the cubic symmetry of the crystal lattice, the cubic shape of the NC, and NC uniaxial anisotropy on the hole energy states and electron-hole exchange interactions are considered non-perturbatively. Symmetry analysis yields an effective Hamiltonian for the exciton fine structure, parameterized by one constant for hole energy splitting and five independent constants for exchange interaction. Numerical calculations reveal that in uniaxially anisotropic zinc-blende NCs, the sign of the hole ground state splitting depends on the material parameters and on the orientation of the anisotropy axis relative to the crystallographic axes. Beyond the conventional bulk cubically-symmetric contribution to the exchange interaction, which originates from Bloch-function symmetry and is typically negligible, in nanocrystals, we identify the contribution arising from the cubic symmetry of the envelope wavefunction. This cubically symmetric envelope-induced short-range exchange is non-negligible in cuboidal NCs and induces a pronounced splitting of the dark exciton states. We further analyze the influence of uniaxial anisotropy of the exchange constants on both the exciton fine structure and the oscillator strength. Special attention is paid to NCs, where the anisotropy of the exchange constants is comparable to a relatively small hole energy anisotropic splitting.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates the coupled spin, charge, and lattice ordering in La3Ni2O7 using first-principles calculations. It finds that spin-charge-stripe states are the ground state, which spontaneously distort into a polar structure. The work establishes that these three degrees of freedom are strongly intertwined, providing insights into the mechanism behind superconductivity in nickelates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between spin, charge, and lattice degrees of freedom, and the emergence of superconductivity linked to spin fluctuations, are central themes in strongly correlated electron physics, relevant to understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to clarify the microscopic relationship among spin, charge, and lattice degrees of freedom in the low-temperature density-wave state of La3Ni2O7.

**Main result.** The spin-charge-stripe states are found to be energetically favored, and the system spontaneously relaxes into the polar Am2m structure via a polar distortion, establishing a unified picture of coupled responses.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations using DFT+U and analyzes the pressure evolution of electronic structure and static spin susceptibility.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the nickelate material La3Ni2O7, which exhibits complex electronic, magnetic, and structural instabilities, particularly in its density-wave state.

**Key observables.** Static spin susceptibility (chi_S(q)), Fermi surface nesting vectors, local Ni magnetic moments, and lattice parameters (Ni–O bond lengths).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (P), which is used to tune the system through different electronic and magnetic regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on DFT+U calculations and treats the spin susceptibility using a simplified Random Phase Approximation (RPA).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electronic structure, Fermi surface reconstruction, and the pressure dependence of the spin susceptibility peak, correlating it with both the density-wave and superconducting transition temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing the ambient pressure SDW state driven by FS nesting, then investigating the pressure evolution, identifying the stable spin-charge-stripe states, and finally demonstrating the structural relaxation into the polar Am2m phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The low-temperature density-wave state of La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ hosts pronounced spin-density-wave (SDW) order, while recent experiments further reveal charge redistribution and a concomitant lattice-symmetry lowering. However, the microscopic relationship among spin, charge, and lattice remains unclear. Using first-principles calculations, we investigate the pressure evolution of the electronic structure and static spin susceptibility of La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$, together with the energetics and lattice response of representative magnetic configurations. We trace the SDW instability to strong Fermi-surface nesting and find that the high-pressure spin response closely tracks $T_{\mathrm C}$, suggesting spin-fluctuation-mediated pairing. Among the candidate magnetic states considered, the spin-charge-stripe states emerge as energetically favored and dynamically stable, developing pronounced disproportionation of both the local Ni moments and the Ni--O bond lengths. Remarkably, the lowest-energy a-stripe state spontaneously relaxes into the experimentally proposed polar Am2m structure through a polar distortion along the b axis. These results establish a unified picture in which spin, charge, and lattice responses are strongly intertwined in the low-pressure density-wave state, while spin fluctuations remain a plausible ingredient of superconductivity under pressure.

</details>

**Authors:** Gonzalo Álvarez-Pérez, Line Jelver, Alexander Paarmann, Simone De Liberato

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory to study nonlocal nonlinear optics in polar crystals by examining the coupling between light and lattice vibrations (phonon polaritons). It reveals a novel second-order nonlinear process stemming from lattice anharmonicity that can generate strong SHG signals even in materials that are otherwise centrosymmetric. The findings establish a new theoretical regime for light-matter interaction in dielectrics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges condensed matter theory (lattice dynamics, DFPT) with quantum optics (polaritons, nonlinear light-matter interaction), providing a detailed theoretical mechanism for enhanced nonlinear optical responses in solid-state dielectrics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To theoretically investigate how nonlocality, arising from phonon confinement, reshapes nonlinear light-matter interactions in dielectrics, particularly in polar crystals.

**Main result.** The study identifies a new, nonlocal second-order nonlinear mechanism enabled by the interplay between optical-phonon propagation and ionic-bond anharmonicity, which can dominate SHG even in centrosymmetric materials.

**Method.** The authors use Density Functional Perturbation Theory (DFPT) to quantify the nonlocal second-order susceptibilities by analyzing the anharmonic potential energy functional.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using polar crystals exhibiting phonon polaritons (PhPs). The nonlinearity is derived from the anharmonic terms ($U_3$) in the lattice potential, which are expanded in terms of wavevectors ($k, k'$).

**Key observables.** Nonlocal second-order susceptibilities ($\chi^{(2)}$), Second-Harmonic Generation (SHG) signals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phonon polariton resonance frequencies, wavevector dependence ($k$), and the degree of confinement (deeply subwavelength volumes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes the potential energy $U$ depends only on lattice displacement, confining nonlocality and anharmonicity to lattice origins.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares local and nonlocal $\chi^{(2)}$ across the Reststrahlen Band for various crystals. Figure 2 compares the local versus nonlocal origins of the nonlinearity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (nonlocal nonlinearity in dielectrics), develops the theoretical framework using lattice dynamics and anharmonic potentials, calculates the nonlocal susceptibilities using DFPT, and presents results showing the new nonlinear regime.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When light is confined to deeply subwavelength volumes, optical responses can become nonlocal. Yet how nonlocality reshapes nonlinear light-matter interactions remains largely unexplored, particularly in dielectrics. Here, we theoretically uncover a new class of nonlinear optical processes arising from the interplay between optical-phonon propagation and ionic-bond anharmonicity in polar crystals. We show that, unlike conventional local nonlinearities, this mechanism enables second-order responses even in centrosymmetric materials. Moreover, these responses can be tuned by the confinement and resonantly enhanced near optical-phonon resonances. Using density functional perturbation theory, we quantify the resulting nonlocal second-order susceptibilities in representative polar crystals. Our results establish nonlocal-nonlinear optics as a new regime of light-matter interaction in dielectrics.

</details>
